# Cultural and Creative Arts (CCA)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 2 · Creativity · Second Term W4, W8, W9 and Third Term W1, W2, W4, W8
(pp 415–420), read with the Nursery 3 band (pp 568–575) for the same topics.*


### WEEK 1 — MAKING AND DESIGNING A HAND FAN

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Second Term W4 (pp 415–417); Nursery 3 · Creativity · Second Term W4 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a fan is for
• cut and fold a fan from cardboard or thick paper
• fix a handle to my fan
• decorate my fan with a pattern I made myself

**Things to know**

• A **fan** is a flat thing we move to and fro so that air comes to us — it keeps us **cool**.
• Two kinds we can make: the **paddle fan** (one flat piece with a handle) and the **folding fan** (paper folded like a fan and joined at one end).
• Materials: **cardboard or thick paper, popsicle sticks, glue or tape, crayons, paint, stickers**.
• Steps: cut the shape → colour or draw on the flat side → fix the stick handle at the back → let the glue dry → wave it to test it.
• A pattern is a design that repeats: red, blue, red, blue · zigzag, dot, zigzag, dot.
• Folding for the folding fan: turn the paper back and forth in little folds like a plate, then join both ends with a thread or tape.
• The handle must be firm: hold the fan by the stick and shake it — if the paper falls, the glue needs more time.
• Cardboard is stiff and keeps its shape; tissue paper is soft and tears, so thick paper is better for the paddle.
• Art is not only for looking — a fan is a thing I made and can still use on a hot day; that is **functional art**.
• Care: scissors cut paper, not hair or fingers; the glue pot is closed again; the table is wiped clean.
• We end with a **fan wave**: everybody stands, counts four, and waves their own fan — the coolest class in the school.

**Words for my notebook**

A fan moves air and keeps us cool. Cardboard, handle, pattern, glue, dry, wave.

**Let us talk**

1. What do you use a fan for?
2. Which shape did you cut for your fan?
3. Say the colours of your pattern in order.
4. Why do we put the stick at the back and not in the middle?
5. What other thing at home moves air?


![A child holds up a folded hand fan marked with zigzags, dots and stripes, while scissors, a folded card and a stick lie on the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week1-the-fan-i-made.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A fan is used to A) keep us cool B) cut paper C) carry books D) boil water
2. The stick we hold a fan by is the A) cover B) wing C) spine D) handle
3. A design that repeats is called a A) pattern B) song C) picture D) story
4. Which material is best for a paddle fan? A) cardboard B) cloth of water C) tissue paper D) a leaf only
5. Fill in: The two kinds of fan we can make are the paddle fan and the ____ fan.
6. Say three materials you used to make your fan.
7. Write two sentences on your fan: its shape, its colours and its pattern.

**My own work**

Wave-test your fan at home on a hot afternoon and write one line about whether it threw enough air; then draw its pattern again in your book.

---

### WEEK 2 — COLOUR SPLASH ART

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · Second Term W7 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• splash paint on paper in a controlled way
• wear my apron and cover my table first
• mix two colours and watch a third appear
• say what abstract art means

**Things to know**

• **Colour splash (splatter) art** is made by throwing small drops of paint on paper — not by drawing lines.
• Before paint touches anything: **apron on, table covered, sleeves up**; paint that dries on cloth is very hard to remove.
• Three ways to splash: **flick** the wet brush with a finger, **drop** paint from a height, or **tap** the brush against a stick.
• From high up the drops are small and many; from low down they are big and few — that is how I control the mess.
• A toothbrush and a comb make a fine spray: pull the brush teeth with a finger over the comb.
• Two colours mixed: blue + yellow = green · red + yellow = orange · red + blue = purple.
• Water makes the colour light; little paint and much water give a pale splash.
• **Abstract art** does not look like a thing; it shows a **feeling** — fast lines for joy, dark splashes for anger.
• A famous splash painter, **Jackson Pollock**, laid his paper on the floor and moved the paint with sticks and his body.
• Name the feeling in your own splash: is it a happy day or a rainy day?
• Cleaning up: brush in the water pot, drops wiped from the table, hands washed with soap, paper left flat to dry.

**Words for my notebook**

Splash, flick, drop. Blue and yellow make green. Art that shows a feeling and not a thing is called abstract.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we wear before painting?
2. Show me the hand movement for a flick.
3. Which two colours make purple?
4. Is your splash fast or quiet? What feeling is in it?


![A child in a tied apron flicks a brush over one sheet of paper so the splashes land as blobs of many sizes, with two plain pots at the corner of the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week2-the-brush-flick.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Colour splash art is made with drops of A) chalk B) water only C) paint D) clay
2. Before we splash paint we put on an A) apron B) hat only C) school shoe D) glove of straw
3. Blue and yellow mixed together give A) orange B) brown C) green D) black
4. Art that shows a feeling instead of a thing is A) abstract B) a map C) a letter D) a copy
5. Fill in: To make a fine spray we use a toothbrush and a ____.
6. Mention two ways of making drops fall on the paper.
7. Write two sentences on the feeling your splash picture shows.

**My own work**

Make one splash picture on a small sheet that says "rainy day" and one that says "party day", and write the feeling under each.

---

### WEEK 3 — SPIKY HEDGEHOG

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Second Term W8 (pp 415–417); Nursery 3 · Second Term W8 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• draw or trace the body of a hedgehog
• make spikes with a fork and with paper strips
• colour the animal brown or grey
• put my hedgehog in a home of leaves and grass

**Things to know**

• A **hedgehog** is a small animal with a **round body, a pointed nose, short legs and many spikes** on its back.
• It rolls into a ball of spikes when it is frightened; the soft front is what it keeps safe.
• Draw the body as a big half circle, then add a pointed nose, two eyes and four small feet.
• For the spikes, **dip a fork in paint and print it** across the back — the fork teeth leave a fan of lines.
• Other spike ways: glue **cut paper strips** standing up, press on **safe little twigs**, or draw spikes with a pencil.
• **Mixed media** means using more than one material in one picture: paint + paper + twigs.
• **Texture** is how a thing would feel: smooth, rough, spiky, soft — a hedgehog picture should feel rough.
• Colours of a hedgehog: brown, grey, dark tan, with a lighter face.
• The background is its **habitat**: grass, dry leaves, a garden wall, the moon at night (it walks at night).
• Glue is spread with a stick, not a finger; too much glue makes the paper wet through.
• The finished hedgehog goes on the class board under the title "Spiky and Safe".

**Words for my notebook**

A hedgehog has a round body, a pointed nose and spikes on its back. Fork print, paper strips and twigs all make texture.

**Let us talk**

1. What does a hedgehog do when it is afraid?
2. Which part of the hedgehog is soft?
3. Show me how you press the fork for spikes.
4. Name two materials you used besides paint.


![A child kneels beside a paper hedgehog and presses a fork along its back to make the spikes, with paper strips and a little pile of leaves near by.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week3-fork-pressed-spikes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A hedgehog's back is covered with A) spikes B) hair only C) feathers D) scales
2. The front of the hedgehog ends in a A) big ear B) pointed nose C) shell D) long tail
3. Using paint, paper and twigs together in one picture is called A) modelled art B) mixed media C) mixing D) splash art
4. How a thing would feel is its A) texture B) name C) colour D) size
5. Fill in: A hedgehog rolls into a ____ when it is frightened.
6. Name three ways of making spikes for your hedgehog.
7. Draw a hedgehog, give it spikes and one line of grass under it.

**My own work**

Make a second hedgehog in the same size from only paper (no paint), then compare the two and say which one feels spikier.

---

### WEEK 4 — PAPER COLLAGE AND THE FRIENDSHIP TREE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Second Term W9 (Paper Collage, pp 415–417) and Third Term W1 (Friendship Tree, pp 418–420) — the school keeps both in one week, so this note takes both.*

**You will learn to**

• tear and cut coloured paper into small pieces
• stick the pieces inside a drawn shape
• make a tree that carries the names of my friends
• say what patience does for my work

**Things to know**

• A **collage** is a picture made by **sticking pieces of material** on a surface.
• Tearing gives soft, feathered edges; cutting gives hard, straight edges — good pictures use both.
• Fill the inside of the shape, not outside it: the drawing is the wall of the room.
• Small pieces go near the edge, bigger ones in the middle — the shape stays tidy.
• **Composition** means how things are put together: what is big, what is small, what goes in front.
• Glue is spread little by little with a stick; a wet finger makes the paper wrinkle.
• The **friendship tree**: draw a strong trunk and wide branches; the leaves are our hand prints or cut paper leaves.
• One leaf carries one name — my own, my friends', my class mates' — so the tree is the class.
• A tree with many leaves stands better than one with two; so everybody's name goes on.
• Collage work takes **patience**: it is slow, and slow work looks careful.
• When it is finished we stand back and look: can we see the shape of the tree? Are the names readable?

**Words for my notebook**

Collage means sticking torn or cut paper into a shape. Our friendship tree carries a name on every leaf.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a collage?
2. Which edge is nicer for the tree trunk — torn or cut?
3. How many names are on your tree?
4. Why is patience part of art?


![Torn scraps of paper are glued inside a drawn tree whose leaf shapes are left empty, with a bowl of scraps and a stick of glue on the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week4-the-tree-of-torn-paper.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A collage is a picture made by A) pouring water B) sticking pieces on a surface C) tying ropes D) singing
2. Torn paper gives an edge that is A) hard B) soft and feathered C) hot D) straight
3. A tree whose leaves carry names in our class is the A) palm tree B) mango tree only C) friendship tree D) family tree
4. How the parts of a picture are put together is its A) glue B) paper C) composition D) size
5. Fill in: Glue is spread little by little with a ____.
6. Mention two things you cut or tore for your collage.
7. Write two sentences on why everybody's name was needed on the friendship tree.

**My own work**

Cut five small pieces of paper and make a house on a fresh sheet with them: roof, wall, door and a garden.

---

### WEEK 5 — MAKING JEWELLERY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Third Term W2 (pp 418–420); Nursery 3 · Second Term W11 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• thread beads in a pattern
• make a necklace, a bracelet and a ring
• match colours that look good together
• keep small things away from the mouth

**Things to know**

• **Jewellery** is the ornament people wear: necklace, bracelet, ring, anklet and bead for the wrist.
• Safe materials: **big beads, painted pasta shells, buttons, paper beads, pieces of straw, thread or yarn**.
• **Paper beads**: cut a long triangle of paper, roll it from the wide end on a stick, glue the tip, and let it dry — it becomes hard like wood.
• A **pattern** or **sequence** is an order that repeats: red, red, blue · red, red, blue.
• Count as you thread: two small, one big, two small, one big.
• Colour matching: yellow looks bright beside blue; white goes with everything; too many colours make the pattern hard to see.
• The thread must be **knot-ty at the end**; a knot is tied before threading so the beads do not fall off the back.
• To finish: tie the two ends twice and pull — a bracelet is a ring with a knot.
• Safety: beads are **not for the mouth, the nose or the ear**; a small bead swallowed is dangerous and must be reported at once.
• Scissors cut the thread only; the needle (a blunt plastic one) is carried point-down.
• We wear our own work for one song, then the jewellery goes home in a pouch, not in a pocket.

**Words for my notebook**

Necklace, bracelet, ring. Red, red, blue — the pattern repeats. Beads never go into the mouth.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the pattern of your necklace in words.
2. How do we stop the beads falling off the thread?
3. Which colours did you choose, and why?
4. Name one thing that is never safe to put in your mouth.


![A half-made necklace lies in a loop on the mat with a short bracelet, one ring and a little bowl of spare beads.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week5-the-beads-strung.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is jewellery worn on the wrist? A) collar B) bracelet C) anklet D) necklace
2. A roll of paper glued and dried can be used as a A) plate B) bead C) cup D) shoe
3. Two small, one big, two small, one big is a A) colour B) song C) story D) pattern
4. Before we tie the ends we must make a A) drawing B) hole C) mark of chalk D) knot
5. Fill in: A bead must never be put into the ____.
6. Name three materials we threaded with.
7. Write two sentences on the necklace you made and the pattern in it.

**My own work**

Make one bracelet for somebody at home from the last of your beads, and say what colours you chose for them.

---

### WEEK 6 — RUBBER AND FOAM COLLAGE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · Third Term W2 (pp 572–575).*

**You will learn to**

• cut shapes from craft foam
• glue foam on paper in layers
• tell a flat collage from a 3D one
• say why a soft material is fun to build with

**Things to know**

• A **foam collage** is a picture made by sticking **cut pieces of foam or soft rubber** on paper.
• Foam comes in **craft foam sheets**, in old **slippers**, in **sponge** and in the packing foam things are sold in.
• Foam is soft, so the scissors must be **sharp and small**, and the cut is made on a board, not on the hand.
• **Flat collage** lies on the paper; **3D (dimensional) collage** rises up from it because pieces are stacked.
• **Layering** means sticking one piece on top of another: a big green leaf, then a small dark green leaf on it.
• Layering makes the picture look near and far: the biggest piece is nearest to me.
• Foam holds its shape, so a foam animal has a body that stands out; paint on it looks soft, not shiny.
• Glue for foam must be thick (paste or fabric glue); watery glue lets the piece lift up again after a while.
• Weight matters: too many layers make the paper bend — the base sheet should be card, not foolscap.
• **Spatial awareness** is what I am learning here: which piece goes above, below, beside or behind another.
• Clean hands before the work is done; foam scraps go in the box, not on the floor for the class to slip on.

**Words for my notebook**

Cut, layer, press. A flat collage lies down; a 3D collage stands up.

**Let us talk**

1. Where does the biggest piece of foam go?
2. What kind of glue holds foam best?
3. Name one thing you would not cut foam on.
4. Is your picture flat or raised? Show me.


![A child cuts a fish shape out of foam while two foam pieces already lie glued in layers on a card, one standing proud of the other.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week6-foam-cut-in-layers.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A foam collage is a picture made of A) song B) water C) sand only D) cut foam pieces
2. A picture that rises up from the paper is called A) 3D B) old C) quiet D) flat
3. Sticking one piece on top of another is A) waving B) counting C) tearing D) layering
4. Which base is best for a heavy foam picture? A) cardboard B) a leaf C) cloth of water D) foolscap paper
5. Fill in: Foam is soft, so we cut it on a ____, not on the hand.
6. Mention two things that can give us foam for collage.
7. Write two sentences on your foam picture: what you cut and what you layered.

**My own work**

Cut three foam shapes and build an animal with them on a card — two shapes must be layered under the others.

---

### WEEK 8 — SAND COLLAGE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Third Term W4 (pp 418–420); Nursery 3 · Third Term W3 (pp 572–575).*

**You will learn to**

• put glue only where the sand must stick
• sprinkle sand and shake off the extra
• use sand of different colours
• say what texture a sand picture has

**Things to know**

• A **sand collage** is a picture with parts of it covered in sand, so that it feels **rough and grainy**.
• Steps: draw the picture → paint glue on one small area at a time → **sprinkle** sand over it → **shake off** what did not stick → let it dry.
• Glue the parts first with clean paper beside them, or the sand will stick everywhere.
• **Natural sand** is pale yellow; sand can be coloured by mixing it with a little dry cement or paint powder and drying it on paper.
• Different sands give different feelings: fine sand is smooth, coarse sand is rough, gravel is bumpy.
• Sand is a **natural material** — like clay, stick, leaf and shell, it came from the earth and not from a factory.
• Sections: sky (no sand), beach (fine sand), sea (blue paper), rocks (grit) — the eye sees the difference.
• Sprinkle low and gently; from a height the sand flies out of the shape.
• Sand goes on the paper, not on the floor, and never near the eyes; hands are washed after the work.
• Extra sand is poured back into the tin — it is still good for the next class.
• A sand picture is finished when the glue is dry and the surface is firm: touch it and it should not move.

**Words for my notebook**

Glue a small area, sprinkle, shake off the extra, let it dry. Sand is a natural material; it gives texture.

**Let us talk**

1. What does a sand picture feel like?
2. Why do we put glue only on part of the drawing?
3. Name one other thing that comes from the earth and is used in art.
4. What do we do with the sand that does not stick?


![A fish is drawn on the paper with its glue patch inside, a cup lies tipped and the spare sand waits on a plate.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week8-the-sand-and-the-fish.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In a sand collage, sand sticks because of A) glue B) sun C) water D) chalk
2. After sprinkling sand we ____ A) burn it B) press with the foot C) shake off the extra D) wet it again
3. Sand, clay and shells are A) painted things B) natural materials C) factory things D) soft foods
4. Which part of a beach picture is best left without sand? A) the rocks B) the path C) the sand bank D) the sky
5. Fill in: Coarse sand feels ____ while fine sand feels smooth.
6. Say the four steps of putting sand on a picture, in order.
7. Write two sentences on the texture of your sand picture and where you got the sand.

**My own work**

Collect a little sand at home, dry it on paper in the sun, and use it to fill one shape you have drawn.

---

### WEEK 9 — DESIGNING A CONE HAT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · Third Term W8 (pp 418–420); Nursery 3 · Third Term W8 (pp 572–575).*

**You will learn to**

• roll paper into a cone
• fix the cone so that it keeps its shape
• decorate my hat with ribbons, stars and colour
• wear my hat in the class parade

**Things to know**

• A **cone hat** is a hat that comes to a point at the top, like a party hat or the cap of a fishing net.
• Roll a **fan-shaped piece** of cardboard: the wide arc becomes the bottom, the point becomes the top.
• A **sector** is that fan shape — a circle with a wedge cut out of it.
• Hold the roll with a clothes pin while the glue dries; tape or staples can do the same work.
• Do not roll it too tight — the head must go inside; try it on a friend's head before the glue sets.
• Decorate with paint, crayons, **stickers, ribbons, pom-poms, paper stars and streamers**; a hat is the place for the brightest colours.
• A band of paper round the base hides the rough edge and makes the hat stronger.
• To keep it on the head, thread an **elastic or string** through two holes at the base and tie it.
• The **hat parade**: one by one, walk to the drum, turn at the end of the line, and come back; nobody laughs at another's hat.
• Wearable art is 3D art — it moves with the body, so it must be light and must not cover the eyes.
• When the parade is over, the hat is kept on a shelf, not folded — a cone that is crushed cannot be opened again.

**Words for my notebook**

Roll, hold, glue, dry, decorate, wear. A cone hat has a point at the top and a band at the base.

**Let us talk**

1. What shape must the paper be before you roll it?
2. What holds the roll while the glue dries?
3. Which decorations did you put on your hat?
4. Why must the hat be light?


![The finished cone hat stands on the table with its ribbon, two stars and a zigzag, beside a rolled strip held by a peg.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week9-the-cone-hat-and-the-peg.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A cone hat ends at the top in a A) point B) flat board C) hole D) wheel
2. Before rolling, the paper is cut in the shape of a A) square B) triangle of cloth C) long rope D) circle with a wedge out
3. While the glue dries, the roll can be held with a A) shoe B) stone C) clothes pin D) wet cloth
4. A band of paper round the base of the hat A) hides the eyes B) makes it heavier C) shortens it D) hides the rough edge
5. Fill in: To keep the hat on the head we tie an ____ or string under the chin.
6. Mention four things used to decorate a cone hat.
7. Write two sentences on your hat and what happened in the class hat parade.

**My own work**

Make a small cone hat for a doll or a bottle from a leftover piece of paper, and give it one pattern of three repeating colours.


---

# Christian Religious Studies (CRS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing and Awards — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Christian Religious Studies · Second Term W1–W10 (pp 640–641) and
Third Term W1–W2 (pp 642–643) — the school moves these topics down to Nursery 2, so each week is
written at Nursery 2 language with the Primary 1 content kept whole.*


### WEEK 1 — GOD IS OUR FATHER (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W1 (pp 640–641).*

**You will learn to**

• say that God is my Father in heaven
• tell how God loves and cares for me
• name the good things God gives me every day
• say how a father on earth is different from God

**Things to know**

• A **father** in the family is the man who owns the house, works and takes care of the children.
• **God is my Father in heaven** — He is not in the house, He is in heaven, but He still keeps me and gives me all good things.
• God **loves** me: the Bible says God so loved the world that He gave His only Son (John 3:16).
• God **cares** for me: He gives rain, sunshine, food, sleep and the people who keep me.
• God **provides**: He gave birds food, and He gives me my daily bread.
• A father on earth is a man; he can be far away, he can be tired, he can die. God is a spirit — He is always near, never tired, and He never dies.
• A father can give a shoe; God gives the **feet** that put on the shoe.
• God's love is **perfect** — it has no mistake in it; God's love is **everlasting** — it does not finish.
• Nobody is too small for God: the little child in the class is as much God's child as the big man.
• I am not God's child because I am good; I am God's child because God chose to love me.
• We close with a small prayer: Thank you God, our Father, for today.

**Words for my notebook**

God is my Father in heaven. He loves me, cares for me and provides for me.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is your father at home?
2. Who is your Father in heaven?
3. Name three things God gave you today.
4. How is God different from the father at home?


![A child stands in the garden with both arms lifted, under the sun, one rain cloud, a bird and a mango tree with three fruits.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week1-the-good-things-given-today.png)
**Worksheet**

1. God is called our Father in A) school B) the market C) heaven D) the river
2. God's love for us is described as perfect and A) far B) everlasting C) cold D) small
3. Which of these shows that God provides? A) He sleeps B) He gives rain and food C) He cannot hear D) He is far away
4. A father on earth and God are different because God A) eats our food B) is a spirit and never dies C) goes to work D) lives next door
5. Fill in: The Bible says God so loved the ____ that He gave His only Son.
6. Mention three ways God cares for you.
7. Write two sentences on why God is called your Father.

**My own work**

Count the good things that came to you today and write four of them in your book as thanks to God.

Draw your own home and colour the window that is lit in the evening.

---

### WEEK 2 — GOD IS OUR FATHER (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W2 (pp 640–641).*

**You will learn to**

• say that prayer is talking to God
• tell God everything I need
• say sorry when I do wrong
• begin the Lord's Prayer

**Things to know**

• **Prayer is talking to God.** It does not need big words; it can be whispered, and God hears it.
• God **hears** every prayer, even the one said in the heart with no sound at all.
• God **answers** prayers: sometimes He says yes, sometimes wait, sometimes no — all three are answers.
• When I do wrong, I **say sorry to God**; that is confession, and God forgives and cleans me.
• God **protects** me like a father shields his child from the road, the dog and the rain.
• The **Lord's Prayer** is the prayer Jesus taught: "Our Father who art in heaven, hallowed be thy name. Thy kingdom come, thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven."
• Give God the name He loves: Father.
• Four easy ways to pray: **thank** Him, **ask** Him, **sorry**, and **praise** Him.
• We can pray anywhere: in bed, at the table, in the bush, in class; and any time: morning, noon and night.
• A prayer may be one line: "God, keep me tonight." God is not tired of short prayers.
• Kneeling, folding hands or lifting them is the body's way of praying; the mouth's way is the word.

**Words for my notebook**

Prayer is talking to God. God hears, answers, forgives and protects.

**Let us talk**

1. What did you thank God for this morning?
2. Is prayer a big talk or a talk to a Father?
3. What do we say when we have done something wrong?
4. Say the first line of the Lord's Prayer with me.


![A child kneels on a mat beside the bed with hands folded, under a round window holding a moon and three stars.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week2-kneeling-by-the-bed.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Prayer is best described as A) reading to a friend B) singing C) talking to God D) sleeping
2. When I do wrong and say sorry, God A) forgives me B) forgets me C) punishes all children D) leaves me
3. The prayer Jesus taught His disciples is the A) school prayer B) morning song C) Lord's Prayer D) harvest prayer
4. God's answer to a prayer can be yes, no or A) wait B) never C) maybe not D) again
5. Fill in: The Lord's Prayer begins "Our Father who art in ____."
6. Name four things we can bring to God in prayer.
7. Write the prayer you said last night before you slept.

**My own work**

Say one prayer of thanks and one prayer of asking on each of the next three nights, and write tomorrow what you asked for.

Draw one good thing God gives you every day and colour it.

---

### WEEK 3 — GOD IS OUR FATHER (PART 3)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W3 (pp 640–641).*

**You will learn to**

• say that we are all children of God
• know that God knows me well
• say that I am never alone
• trust God when I am afraid

**Things to know**

• All of us in this class are **children of God** — that makes us brothers and sisters.
• God **calls me to call Him "Father"**; a servant says master, a child says Father.
• God **knows everything** about me: my name, my face, my thoughts, what I ate, where I am.
• The Bible says God even knows the number of the hairs on my head.
• God is **always with me**: at school, at home, in the bus, in the dark, when nobody else is there.
• Because He is with me, I am **never alone**; being alone is not the same as God leaving me.
• **Trust** means depending on somebody: trusting God is leaning on Him the way I lean on a strong wall.
• What trust looks like: not crying in the dark, sharing when I am afraid of having nothing, saying sorry first, telling the truth when lying would pay me.
• Children of God do not fight over who is God's favourite; God loves each one fully.
• Fear and trust cannot sit together on the same chair — when I trust God, fear goes out of the chair.
• We say the verse together: "The Lord is my helper; I will not be afraid."

**Words for my notebook**

I am a child of God. God knows me, God is with me, I can trust God.

**Let us talk**

1. Whose children are we all?
2. Does God know your name? Does He know your thoughts?
3. When are you most afraid? Who is with you there?
4. What is one thing you can stop worrying about today?


![A child sits calmly under a wide tree beside a little lamb with curved horns, a bird on the branch and a butterfly on the grass.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week3-under-the-tree-with-the-lamb.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Every child in our class is a child of A) the school only B) the driver C) the market D) God
2. God knows my name, my face and my A) desk B) thoughts C) bag D) shoes only
3. Because God is with me, I am never A) hungry B) alone C) small D) late
4. Trusting God is like leaning on a A) tall ladder B) weak rope C) strong wall D) wet cloth
5. Fill in: A child of God says "Father"; a servant says "____."
6. Mention three things God knows about you.
7. Write two sentences on a time you were afraid and God was with you.

**My own work**

Write the name of three children in your class and pray one line for each of them tonight.

Draw a family sitting together and colour the shirt of the one who takes care of you.

---

### WEEK 4 — THINGS WE MUST DO AS CHILDREN OF GOD (PARTS 1 AND 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W4 (Part 1) and W7 (Part 2), pp 640–641 — the school puts two parts in one week.*

**You will learn to**

• pray in the morning, at the table and at night
• thank God and ask Him for help
• obey and respect those who keep me
• be kind to the children in my house

**Things to know**

• Part 1 is about **prayer**; Part 2 is about **obedience**.
• Praying every day: **morning prayer** before I leave the bed, **meal prayer** before I eat, **night prayer** before I sleep.
• **Thanking** God for everything He gives — food, clothes, school, health, family.
• **Asking** God for help when I need it: for strength in an exam, for protection on the road, for a sick person.
• **Saying sorry** when I do wrong, the same day, not after one week.
• A simple prayer I know: "God, thank You for today. Forgive my wrong. Help me tomorrow. In Jesus' name, Amen."
• **Obey** means do what you were told, the first time, without crying about it.
• Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for this is right (Ephesians 6:1) — the first commandment with a promise.
• **Respect and honour** my father and mother: answer them politely, do not shout them down, stand when they come, carry what they hand me.
• Be **kind to brothers and sisters**: share the chair, do not tear their book, do not tell on them for small things.
• **Help with the small work of the house**: fetch water, sweep, arrange shoes, wash the plate after eating.
• Obedience is proved by what I do when nobody is watching.

**Words for my notebook**

Morning, table and night — pray every day. Obey and respect those who keep me (Ephesians 6:1).

**Let us talk**

1. When do you pray in a day?
2. What do you say before you eat?
3. Name two things you were told to do at home today.
4. How do you show respect to your father and mother?


![A child puts a bowl and two cups on the low table for two younger children sitting on the mat, with a broom leaning at the wall.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week4-we-share-and-we-obey.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is a time for prayer every day? A) morning, table and night B) when I am punished C) Saturday only D) morning only
2. Before we eat we ____ God for the food. A) thank B) forget C) owe D) blame
3. "Children, obey your parents" is found in A) Ephesians 6:1 B) the arithmetic book C) the song sheet D) the alphabet
4. Respecting my father and mother means A) answering them politely B) shouting at them C) ignoring them D) hiding from them
5. Fill in: When I do wrong I say ____ to God the same day.
6. Mention three times we are to pray each day and one thing we ask God for.
7. Write two sentences on how you help in the house and why it pleases God.

**My own work**

For one week, write in a tick-box the prayers you said in the morning, at the table and at night, and show it on Friday.

Draw two things you do because you are a child of God, and colour the one you did this week.

---

### WEEK 5 — THINGS WE MUST DO AS CHILDREN OF GOD (PARTS 3 AND 4)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W8 (Part 3) and W9 (Part 4), pp 640–641 — the school puts two parts in one week.*

**You will learn to**

• speak the truth when a lie would pay me
• share my things and play nicely
• forgive those who hurt me
• worship God in the church on the Lord's day

**Things to know**

• Part 3 is about **living with people**; Part 4 is about **worship**.
• Be **truthful and honest**: do not tell lies, do not take what is not yours, do not hide a broken thing and say the cat did it.
• A lie is a small gain and a great loss; the truth keeps my name clean for a long time.
• **Share** what I have: the chalk, the bench, the orange, the ball; a child of God is not tight-fisted.
• Be **friendly and play nicely**: take turns, do not push, do not laugh at the one who loses.
• **Forgive** those who offend me: if my friend broke my pen and said sorry, I let it go.
• Say sorry myself when I hurt somebody — first, before the other comes.
• Going to **church to worship God**: sit well, listen, sing, do not run in the aisle.
• In church I **listen to Bible stories** and learn about God; the Bible is God's word.
• **Singing songs of praise** is also prayer: a happy voice teaches the heart.
• **Giving offerings** is done with a cheerful heart — God loves the one who gives gladly, not the one who grumbles.
• We keep **Sunday** (the Lord's day) special for God: church, rest, and doing good.

**Words for my notebook**

Truth, sharing, forgiveness. On the Lord's day we go to church to hear God's word, sing and give.

**Let us talk**

1. Which is better: a lie that saves me or the truth that pains me for a while?
2. What do you do when somebody takes your thing?
3. What happens when you forgive?
4. Name three things you do in church.


![In front of a small church with a plain cross on its roof, one child hands a toy to another child on a patch of grass with one flower.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week5-the-toy-handed-over.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Being honest means A) copying B) hiding things C) telling lies D) not taking what is not yours
2. When a friend hurts me and says sorry, I should A) never speak to him again B) fight him C) forgive him D) take his bag
3. The day we keep special for God is A) Saturday only B) Monday C) Sunday D) Friday
4. Giving an offering should be done with a ____ heart. A) sleepy B) angry C) cheerful D) quiet
5. Fill in: In church we listen to ____ stories and learn about God.
6. Mention four things children of God must do among people.
7. Write two sentences on the last time you forgave somebody.

**My own work**

Write one thing you will share tomorrow at school and one person you will say sorry to or forgive.

Draw a hand that helps and a hand that hurts; colour the helping hand.

---

### WEEK 6 — THINGS WE MUST DO AS CHILDREN OF GOD (PART 5)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Second Term W10 (Part 5, pp 640–641) — care for what God created.*

**You will learn to**

• take care of the things God made
• be kind to animals
• keep plants and trees from destruction
• keep my environment clean

**Things to know**

• God made the world and He said it was **very good**; so what I break, I break from His hands.
• **Kindness to animals**: give water to the dog, do not tie the goat's leg too tight, do not stone the cat, do not take the bird's eggs for fun.
• The Bible says a good man cares for the life of his beast.
• **Do not destroy plants and trees**: do not snap the young stem, do not pluck the bark, do not burn the grass round the tree; water what is planted.
• Trees give shade, fruit, clean air and wood; a cut tree takes years to be replaced.
• **Keeping the environment clean**: sweep the compound, throw refuse in the bin, do not pour water on the road, keep the drain open.
• Dirty water and blocked drains breed mosquitoes; cleanliness protects the health God gave us.
• Do not **waste** what God made: turn the tap off, use one cup of water, do not throw away half a bread.
• Do not make noise on purpose; the ears of the neighbours are part of my care too.
• Caring for creation is worship: when I look after the garden, I am serving God with my hands.
• We end with a walk round the school: pick what is on the ground, and say — God made this and gave it to us to keep.

**Words for my notebook**

God made the world and gave it to us to keep. Be kind to animals, spare the plants, keep the place clean.

**Let us talk**

1. What did God say about the world He had made?
2. How do we show kindness to the animals in our street?
3. Name two things that spoil the environment.
4. How is cleaning the compound a way of serving God?


![One child pours water on a young tree while another sweeps the compound; a goat drinks from a bowl and a bucket waits empty.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week6-water-the-tree-sweep-the-compound.png)
**Worksheet**

1. When God had finished making the world He said it was A) finished only B) small C) very good D) bad
2. Kindness to an animal includes A) tying its leg tight B) giving it water C) taking all its eggs D) stoning it
3. Cutting down trees carelessly takes away shade, fruit and A) clean air B) water only C) books D) music
4. Blocked drains are dangerous because they A) save water B) keep the road cool C) grow food D) breed mosquitoes
5. Fill in: Wasting water is not right because God made it for our ____ and not for spoiling.
6. Mention four ways of taking care of the things God created.
7. Write two sentences on one thing you will do this week to keep your environment clean.

**My own work**

Water one plant every day this week and pick five things from the ground around your house; write what happened to the plant on Saturday.

---

### WEEK 8 — DISCOVERING GOD THROUGH JESUS CHRIST (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Third Term W1 (pp 642–643).*

**You will learn to**

• say who Jesus is
• know that Jesus shows us what God is like
• name two miracles of Jesus
• say that Jesus teaches us about God's love

**Things to know**

• **Jesus is the Son of God** who came to earth as a baby in Bethlehem, born of Mary.
• Jesus came to **show us God**: nobody has seen God, but when we look at Jesus we see what God is like.
• The life of Jesus is God's **love written in action** — healing the sick, feeding the hungry, receiving the outcast.
• A **miracle** is a work that only God can do, done in front of people to show His power.
• Miracles of Jesus: water changed into wine, the five loaves and two fish feeding five thousand, the blind receiving sight, Lazarus raised from death, the storm calmed.
• Jesus had **no sin** in Him: He never lied, never hated, never took what was not His.
• Jesus **died on the cross** for our sins and **rose again** on the third day — that is the gospel we preach.
• He walked about doing good and is still alive; He sits at the right hand of God and **prays for us**.
• Learning about Jesus makes me know God better: God does not sit far off, He came near.
• The Bible that tells us this is called the New Testament; the four first books are Matthew, Mark, Luke and John.
• We say the verse together: "For God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son" (John 3:16).

**Words for my notebook**

Jesus is the Son of God. He shows us God, does miracles, died for our sins and rose again.

**Let us talk**

1. Where was Jesus born, and of whom?
2. What is a miracle?
3. Name two miracles of Jesus.
4. How does Jesus show us what God is like?


![A basket of five round loaves and two fish stands on a cloth by the water, with a small boat and one bird beside it.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week8-five-loaves-two-fish-and-a-boat.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Jesus is called the ____ of God. A) brother B) prophet only C) servant D) Son
2. A work that only God can do, done in public, is called a A) journey B) miracle C) parable D) fast
3. With five loaves and two small fish Jesus A) bought a house B) built a boat C) fed a great crowd D) healed the wall
4. Jesus died on the cross and rose again on the ____ day. A) tenth B) seventh C) first D) third
5. Fill in: The four books that first tell us about Jesus are Matthew, Mark, Luke and ____.
6. Mention four miracles of Jesus.
7. Write two sentences on how Jesus shows us the love of God.

**My own work**

Write or draw one miracle of Jesus and one line under it on what the miracle tells us about God.

---

### WEEK 9 — DISCOVERING GOD THROUGH JESUS CHRIST (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · Third Term W2 (pp 642–643).*

**You will learn to**

• say that Jesus was a teacher
• explain what a parable is
• tell how Jesus was kind to everybody
• know that Jesus loves children

**Things to know**

• Jesus was a **teacher**; people called Him **Rabbi**, which means master teacher.
• Jesus taught mostly by **stories** called **parables** — a simple story with a deep lesson in it.
• The **sower**: the seed that fell on good ground grew and gave much fruit; the good ground is the heart that hears God's word.
• The **prodigal son** (the lost son): the boy left home and wasted all he had, but when he returned his father ran to embrace him — that is how God receives a returning sinner.
• The **good Samaritan**: a stranger helped the beaten traveller when the religious men passed by; my neighbour is anybody I can help.
• Jesus was **kind to everybody**: children, grown-ups, the sick, the poor, the woman at the well, the tax collector Zacchaeus.
• Jesus loved **children**: they were brought to Him, He took them in His arms and blessed them.
• "Suffer the little children to come unto me, and forbid them not: for of such is the kingdom of God" (Mark 10:14) — let the children come, do not stop them.
• Jesus is not only kind to the good people; He ate with sinners and forgave them.
• The way Jesus taught is the way I learn to talk: tell the truth in a story people can hold.
• What Jesus asks of me: love God, and love my neighbour as myself.

**Words for my notebook**

Jesus was a teacher who used parables. Jesus loved children and said, "Let the little children come to me."

**Let us talk**

1. What is a parable?
2. Who was the good neighbour in the story of the beaten traveller?
3. What did Jesus do when children were brought to Him?
4. Say the lesson of the sower in your own words.


![Three children sit on a mat with a bowl of seeds between them and a little bird pecking at the ones that fell.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/christian-religious-studies/week9-seeds-on-the-mat.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A simple story with a deep lesson is called a A) psalm B) parable C) miracle D) hymn
2. In the story of the sower, the good ground means A) the farm B) the road C) the heart that hears God's word D) the rain
3. Jesus said, "Suffer the little ____ to come unto me." A) children B) dogs C) kings D) birds
4. In the story of the good Samaritan, the man who helped was A) a stranger B) the ruler C) the owner D) a priest
5. Fill in: People called Jesus ____, which means master teacher.
6. Mention three people Jesus was kind to.
7. Write two sentences on what the story of the lost son teaches us about God.

**My own work**

Tell one parable of Jesus to a friend or a younger child at home, then write the lesson in one sentence.

Draw one picture for the parable Jesus told, and colour it.


---

# Basic Digital Literacy

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W2, W3, W4, W7, W8, W9 and W10
(pp 827–828) — the school redistributes this content into the lower classes, so the Primary 4 week is
the depth floor and every line here is pitched as naming, pointing and handling, not as reading and
writing code. The school's own rows decide what each week is about.*


### WEEK 1 — CHANGES IN SIZE, SPEED AND COST OVER TIME

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W2 (History of Digital Devices, pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• say that machines became smaller and faster as time went on
• name the abacus as an old counting machine
• tell that the first computers were as big as a room
• say that a thing once too dear for anybody is now in many hands

**Things to know**

• The **abacus** is a frame with beads on wires; people slide the beads to count, long before any computer.
• **Charles Babbage** is called the father of the computer — he made the plan of a counting machine.
• **Ada Lovelace** wrote the steps for that machine — the first programme, and the first person to see that a computer could do more than count.
• A **generation** is one stage of a machine's life; the computer has passed through **five generations**.
• 1st: **vacuum tubes** — big glass bulbs that ate power and gave out heat. 2nd: **transistors** — small, cool, they made machines smaller.
• 3rd: **integrated circuits** — many tiny parts put on one little chip. 4th: **microprocessors** — the whole brain of the computer on one chip.
• 5th: **artificial intelligence (AI)** — a machine that can learn, answer questions and recognize a face or a voice.
• Over time, three things kept changing: **size** went down, **speed** went up, **cost** per job went down.
• The first computer filled a whole hall and cost more than a house; the phone in a shop today is faster than that hall of machines and thousands of people can afford one.
• Things got smaller: room → cupboard → table → lap → hand → wrist.
• Words for counting speed: a machine does a **million** sums in a second; my finger does one in a second.
• Small, fast and cheap is why a machine that only officers had is now in the hands of traders, farmers and children.

**Words for my notebook**

Old: big, slow, dear. New: small, fast, cheaper. Babbage planned the computer; Ada Lovelace wrote its first programme.

**Let us talk**

1. What did people use to count with before machines?
2. Which was bigger — the first computer or today's phone?
3. Name one thing that has become cheaper over time.
4. What does a machine of the fifth generation do that old ones could not?


![The counting machines in a row: a computer as big as the room, an abacus with beads on wires, and one small phone lying flat on a table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week1-the-room-machine-and-the-phone.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The counting machine with beads on wires is the A) monitor B) fan C) abacus D) printer
2. As computers changed, they became smaller and A) faster B) heavier C) wetter D) slower
3. Charles Babbage is known for the plan of A) the computer B) the bus C) the radio D) the cutlass
4. The fifth generation of computers is about A) steam B) wood C) artificial intelligence D) paper tape
5. Fill in: The first computers were as big as a ____.
6. Mention the three things that kept changing about digital devices.
7. Write two sentences on how a phone today is better than the first computers.

**My own work**

Ask for one old thing at home or in the house of a neighbour — a radio, a burner, a small torch — and write one line on how the new one is smaller or faster.

Draw three things from long ago and the same three today, then colour the modern one in each pair.

---

### WEEK 2 — HARDWARE AND SOFTWARE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W3 and W4 (Parts of Digital Devices I and II, pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• tell hardware from software
• name the parts I can touch and the parts I cannot touch
• say what the CPU, the memory and the store are for
• say that the two must work together

**Things to know**

• **Hardware** is every part of the computer I can **touch**: screen, keys, mouse, box, wires.
• **Software** is the part I **cannot touch** — the instructions and the pictures inside; it is what tells the hardware what to do.
• I can break hardware with a stone; I cannot spill water on software.
• A **program** (an **app**) is a piece of software: the drawing app, the counting app, the music app.
• Inside the box are four parts to know: the **CPU**, **RAM**, the **hard drive (storage)** and the **motherboard**.
• The **CPU** is the **brain** — it does the work and gives the orders.
• **RAM** is **temporary memory** — it holds what I am doing now; when power goes off, what is in RAM is gone.
• The **hard drive** is **permanent memory** — it keeps my drawing and my story until I ask for them again.
• The **motherboard** is the big board that **connects everything**, the way the road joins the houses.
• The computer also has a **power supply** (it brings the electricity in) and a **cooling fan** (it carries the heat out, or the machine becomes too hot to work).
• Input devices bring work **in**: keyboard, mouse, microphone, scanner, webcam. Output devices take work **out**: monitor, printer, speakers, headphones.
• The parts work as one team: I press a key (input) → the CPU thinks (work) → the screen shows it (output).
• A machine with no software is like a schoolbag with no book in it.

**Words for my notebook**

Hardware is what I touch; software is what tells it what to do. CPU = brain, RAM = now, hard drive = later.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to three pieces of hardware on this computer.
2. Can you hold software in your hand? Why not?
3. Which part is the brain?
4. What happens to what is in RAM when the power goes off?


![The parts I can hold: the box, a blank screen, the keyboard, the mouse on its cord, one disc and one floppy case on the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week2-parts-i-can-touch.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Hardware means the parts of a computer that we can A) dream of B) forget C) touch D) name only
2. The brain of the computer is the A) CPU B) cover C) mouse pad D) chair
3. Work kept in the machine until I ask for it is stored in the A) hard drive B) fan C) key D) screen glass
4. Which one is software? A) mouse B) keyboard C) drawing app D) cable
5. Fill in: The big board that connects all the parts is the ____.
6. Mention the four inside parts of a computer.
7. Write two sentences on how input, work and output go round once when I press a key.

**My own work**

Draw the box of a computer and put four labels on it: keyboard, mouse, screen and CPU.

---

### WEEK 3 — INPUT DEVICES: KEYBOARD AND MOUSE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W8 (Input Devices, pp 827–828), with W3–W4 for care.*

**You will learn to**

• find letters and numbers on the keyboard
• say what the special keys do
• click, double-click, drag and drop with the mouse
• care for the keyboard and the mouse

**Things to know**

• The **keyboard** is for typing; its letters are not in ABC order but in the **QWERTY** layout — the first six letters on the top row of letters.
• Keys to know: **letters**, **numbers**, **Space bar** (makes a gap), **Enter** (goes to the next line / says "go"), **Backspace** (takes out what is before the mark), **Caps Lock** (makes big letters).
• The letters should be typed with the fingers, not pecked one by one with two thumbs.
• The keyboard is kept **flat and clean**; nothing is placed on it, and no food or drink is beside it.
• Crumbs in the keys make them stick; the keyboard is turned upside down and shaken gently, or wiped with a dry cloth.
• The **mouse** is for pointing; on it are a **left button**, a **right button** and a **wheel** between them.
• **Click** = press once (to choose). **Double-click** = press twice fast (to open). **Right-click** = show a little list of choices.
• **Scroll** = roll the wheel up and down to move the page without pressing anything.
• **Drag and drop** = hold the left button on the icon, move the mouse, let the button go — the icon follows my hand.
• The **touchpad** on a laptop does the mouse's work: slide one finger to move, tap to click. A **touchscreen** answers the finger directly.
• A mouse with a ball underneath is turned over and the ball wiped clean when it stops moving well; an optical mouse only needs its glass wiped.
• The cable is held, not pulled; a yanked wire breaks the socket inside.

**Words for my notebook**

Keyboard: keys for typing. Mouse: click, double-click, scroll, drag and drop. No water near either.

**Let us talk**

1. Show me the space bar and say what it does.
2. Which key takes out a wrong letter?
3. What must I do to open a picture: click or double-click?
4. Show me drag and drop on the screen.


![A child sits straight on the stool with one hand on the mouse, blank keys in front and one empty square on the screen.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week3-one-hand-on-the-mouse.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The arrangement of letters on a keyboard is called the ____ layout. A) ABC B) QWERTY C) Friday D) number
2. The key that moves us to the next line is A) Esc B) Caps Lock C) Enter D) Shift
3. To open a folder we usually A) click once B) shake the mouse C) double-click D) pull the cable
4. Holding the button, moving the mouse and letting go is A) printing B) drag and drop C) saving D) scanning
5. Fill in: The key that makes all the letters big is ____.
6. Mention three things that spoil a keyboard.
7. Write two sentences on how you move a picture from one place to another on the screen.

**My own work**

Type or trace your own name, then your father's name, using only the correct letters, on a keyboard or a drawn paper keyboard.

---

### WEEK 4 — THE MICROPHONE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W10 (Computer Care and Safety, pp 827–828) for the care lines, with W3–W4 for the device itself.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a microphone is for
• show how voice goes in and comes out
• handle the microphone well
• sit well and rest the eyes when I work

**Things to know**

• A **microphone** is an input device that takes **sound in** — my voice, a song, a hand-clap.
• It changes sound into something the machine can keep, so I can hear it again, or send it to a friend.
• Things that use a microphone: the **phone** (calling), the **recorder**, the **radio**, the **computer** for voice typing, the **microphone on a stage**, a **headset**.
• Speak **across** the microphone, not **into** it — a hard "p" and "b" blow pop sounds into it.
• Keep it about the width of two fingers from the mouth; hold it by the body, never by the cable.
• Care: never drop it, keep it from water, from dust and from being pulled; a microphone that has fallen must be shown before it is used again.
• With earphones the sound goes **in** as well as **out**: I speak, I hear myself — that is how a recording is checked.
• Sound must not be too loud: loud sound in the ear hurts the small parts inside and can spoil hearing for life.
• **Ergonomics** is sitting well: back straight against the chair, both feet on the floor, the screen level with the eyes, arms loose, keyboard flat.
• The eyes rest too: every little while, look far away out of the window for a count of twenty, and blink.
• **Lab rules**: one to a machine unless told otherwise, no food or drink, hands clean and dry, ask before the power is switched on, switch off at the end and push the chair in.
• A **surge protector** saves the machine when lightning or a fault sends too much power along the line; the plug is held by the hard part, not the wire.
• Virus protection means: do not put in a flash drive that is not yours, and do not click strange pictures that say you have won something.
• Shut down the right way — the machine closes itself; pulling the plug while it is thinking spoils the work in RAM.

**Words for my notebook**

A microphone takes sound in. Hold it by the body, speak across it, keep it dry. Sit straight, screen at eye level.

**Let us talk**

1. Which kind of device is a microphone — input or output?
2. Why do we not shout into the microphone?
3. Show me how you sit when you use the computer.
4. What do we do before switching a machine off?


![A child sits upright at the table and speaks quietly into a microphone on its stand, with three thin lines carrying the voice.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week4-speak-quietly-into-the-microphone.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A microphone is used to A) cool the CPU B) print paper C) store files D) take sound in
2. We hold a microphone by its A) body B) cable C) shadow D) plug pin
3. Sitting well while we work keeps our A) shoes clean B) hands wet C) back straight D) bag closed
4. The small rest our eyes need is to look A) far away B) at the floor C) nearer D) down
5. Fill in: A ____ protector saves the machine when too much power comes along the line.
6. Mention four rules of the computer lab.
7. Write two sentences on a time you spoke into a microphone or a recorder.

**My own work**

Record one verse of a song or say your own name into a phone and play it back, then write one line on whether the sound was clear.

Draw a microphone standing on a desk and colour it; then draw your own mouth open as you speak into it.

---

### WEEK 5 — SCANNER, WEBCAM AND OTHER PERIPHERALS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W8 (Input Devices) and W7 (Peripheral Devices, pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a scanner and a webcam do
• tell a scanned picture from a photographed one
• name the plugs that join a device to a machine
• say what a peripheral is

**Things to know**

• A **scanner** takes in a **flat paper thing** — a drawing, a letter, a photo — and puts it into the computer as a picture.
• The paper lies face-down on the glass; the light bar travels under it once; the cover is closed so daylight does not wash the picture out.
• A **webcam** is a small camera on top of the screen; it takes in **moving pictures and my face** for a call, not paper.
• Scanner = still and flat; webcam = live and moving. A phone camera is like a webcam I can carry.
• Use of a scanner: to keep a drawing, to send a homework, to put a birth certificate on paper of a computer, to copy a picture into a story.
• Care: the glass is wiped with a soft dry cloth and never with water; nothing heavy is stood on the lid; the paper is flat, no clip or pin on the glass.
• A **peripheral** is an outside device **connected** to the computer to give it more power.
• Peripherals I know: **printer, scanner, external hard drive, USB flash drive, webcam, speakers, mouse, keyboard, headset**.
• They join by **USB** (the small rectangular plug for most things), **HDMI** (the wide flat plug for picture and sound to a television) or **Bluetooth** (joining with no wire at all).
• A **flash drive** is small, carries my work in my pocket, and is pulled out only after the machine says it is safe — pulling it while it writes spoils the file.
• With Bluetooth I must first put the device in **pairing** so the two find each other; then it works from across the room.
• The cable is unrolled, not wound tight round the plug, and the plug goes in the right way — force bends the pins.

**Words for my notebook**

A scanner takes in paper; a webcam takes in my face and the room. A peripheral is a device joined by USB, HDMI or Bluetooth.

**Let us talk**

1. Which device would you use to put your drawing into the computer?
2. Where does the paper lie on the scanner?
3. Name a peripheral that needs no wire.
4. Why must a flash drive not be pulled out while it is writing?


![A scanner with its lid open and one blank sheet on the glass, a small webcam perched on a blank screen, and one plug with a coiled cord.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week5-the-scanner-and-the-webcam.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A scanner is used to A) sweep the floor B) take in a paper picture C) print nothing D) cook food
2. A small camera on top of the screen is the A) printer B) webcam C) mouse D) fan
3. Which one joins a device without a wire? A) Bluetooth B) HDMI C) a nail D) USB
4. A flash drive carries A) my work B) food C) chalk D) water
5. Fill in: On the scanner, the paper lies face-____ on the glass.
6. Mention five peripherals of a computer.
7. Write two sentences on how a scanned picture and a photographed picture differ.

**My own work**

Have your best drawing scanned or photographed, and write one line under it saying who made it and when.

---

### WEEK 6 — OUTPUT DEVICES: THE MONITOR

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W9 (Output Devices, pp 827–828), with W3–W4 for care.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a monitor shows me
• tell the screen from the box
• keep the screen clean and my eyes safe
• say what "resolution" means for a picture

**Things to know**

• The **monitor** (also called the **screen** or **display**) is the output device that **shows** me the work with pictures and words.
• It shows only what the computer has already done — it does not do the thinking; the box does that.
• A **television** is like a big monitor for the sitting room; a **monitor** is for the computer on the table.
• A screen is measured across, in **inches**: the class machines are about the size of a book opened wide; the television at home is much wider.
• **Resolution** is how many small dots make up the picture: many dots give a **sharp** picture, few dots give a **blurry** one with blocks.
• **LCD** screens are flat and light; the old heavy ones (CRT) were deep boxes with a curve on the glass.
• Care: switch off when leaving, wipe with a **soft dry cloth** in a circle, never spray water or detergent on the glass (it runs down into the edge and kills it).
• Do not press a finger on the glass, do not knock it, do not hang on it; a crack inside cannot be mended, only the whole screen changed.
• Keep the screen **out of the sun's bright line** and away from a window's glare, so I can see it without squinting.
• The top of the screen should be about the level of my eyes and an arm's length away; that is why the stand can be raised or lowered.
• Brightness is turned down in a dark room and up in daylight; too much light on the eyes makes the head ache.
• A **projector** is an output device too — it throws the picture big onto a wall for the whole class to see.

**Words for my notebook**

The monitor shows the work. Many dots make a sharp picture. Wipe with a dry cloth, never with water.

**Let us talk**

1. Is the monitor an input or an output device?
2. What makes a picture look blurry?
3. Show me how high the top of the screen should be for you.
4. What must we never spray on a screen?


![A child sits straight on the stool with the eyes level with the top of a blank screen, and a folded cloth waits on the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week6-eyes-level-with-the-top-of-the-screen.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The monitor is a device that A) shows work on a screen B) stores files C) prints paper D) takes in sound
2. A picture made of many small dots looks A) dark B) blurry C) sharp D) wet
3. A screen is cleaned with A) water and soap B) a wet mop C) sandpaper D) a soft dry cloth
4. Which device throws the picture big on the wall for the class? A) projector B) flash drive C) keyboard D) scanner
5. Fill in: The old heavy monitors were deep boxes; the flat light ones are ____ screens.
6. Mention four things that can spoil a monitor.
7. Write two sentences on how you sit when you look at a screen.

**My own work**

Watch a television or a screen at home for ten minutes and write one line on what was shown and one line on how you kept your eyes comfortable.

Draw a monitor on your desk and colour the picture that is showing on its screen.

---

### WEEK 8 — THE PRINTER

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W10 (Computer Care and Safety, pp 827–828) for care, with W9 for the device.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a printer does
• tell an inkjet printer from a laser printer
• put paper in the tray without crumpling it
• care for the printer and the ink

**Things to know**

• A **printer** is an output device that puts the work from the screen **onto paper** so that I can hold it.
• An **inkjet printer** squirts tiny drops of **liquid ink** on the page; it is good for my coloured drawing and it is the common home printer.
• A **laser printer** uses **powder (toner)** and heat to burn the picture onto the paper; it is fast, gives sharp black words, and is the kind in the school office.
• Steps of printing: the work is sent → the machine wakes → paper is pulled up one sheet at a time → the page comes out and dries.
• Paper goes in **flat** in the tray, against the guide, and not too many sheets at once — too many makes the machine **jam**.
• A **jam** means the paper is stuck inside; the machine is switched off and the paper pulled out **slowly with both hands** so no bit tears off inside.
• **Ink/toner is a cartridge**: it is handled gently and kept away from the fingers and the eyes; a black smear on the shirt is hard to wash.
• Do not open the printer while it is moving; the inside parts are hot in a laser printer.
• Quality: choose the paper type — plain paper for words, coated paper for the coloured picture; wrong paper gives a wet or a pale print.
• A printer left on a shelf gathers dust on the rollers; it is covered when not in use.
• Before printing I **look at it on the screen first** (a preview) — printing by mistake wastes paper and ink.
• The spare page from a wrong print is not dust: the back is used for rough work.

**Words for my notebook**

A printer puts work on paper. Inkjet uses liquid ink; laser uses powder and heat. Too much paper in the tray causes a jam.

**Let us talk**

1. What comes out of a printer?
2. Which printer is better for a coloured drawing?
3. What do we do when the paper sticks inside?
4. Why do we look at the picture on the screen before printing?


![A blank sheet comes out of the printer, a neat stack of paper waits beside it and one ink cartridge rests on the table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week8-the-printer-and-the-blank-sheet.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A printer puts the computer's work on A) the floor B) paper C) a plate D) water
2. The printer that squirts liquid ink is the A) laser B) inkjet C) speaker D) scanner
3. Too much paper in the tray makes the printer A) cool B) sleep C) jam D) sing
4. Before we print we look at A) the wall B) the shoes C) a preview on the screen D) the clock
5. Fill in: A laser printer uses powder called ____ and heat.
6. Mention three rules for taking care of a printer.
7. Write two sentences on printing one page: what you sent and what came out.

**My own work**

Print or have printed one page of your own writing or drawing, and keep the wrong print from the pile for rough work at the back.

Draw a printer with one sheet of paper coming out of it, and colour the paper white.

---

### WEEK 9 — SPEAKERS AND HEADPHONES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W10 (Computer Care and Safety, pp 827–828) for care and volume, with W9 for the devices.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a speaker and a headphone are for
• know that both are output devices
• keep the sound at a safe level
• care for the small parts that make the sound

**Things to know**

• A **speaker** is an output device that sends **sound out** of the machine so the room can hear it.
• A **headphone** (or **earphone**) also sends sound out, but only **one person** hears it — the sound goes straight into the ear.
• Sound comes from a thin part that **vibrates** (moves to and fro very fast) and pushes the air; that push is what the ear catches.
• The **volume** control makes the sound bigger or smaller; too loud is not more fun, it is damage.
• Loud sound for a long time hurts the ear for life — the ear does not grow back what the noise killed. So: low volume, short time, and I can still hear somebody speaking next to me.
• **Do not share earphones** — the ear passes small dirt and infection from one person to another.
• Care: speakers hate water; the headphone is not wound tight round the plug but looped loosely; the cable is not pulled over the desk edge or under a chair leg.
• The small holes of the earphone are kept clean and free of fluff; a blocked hole sounds quiet and tempts me to raise the volume.
• Speakers come in pairs — one for the left and one for the right — so that I know which side a sound came from.
• Sound is also made by the machine's internal **buzzer**; if the sound comes from the phone or the monitor, that is called a **built-in speaker**.
• Music in class is used for time and for movement: it starts, it stops, and when it stops the class is quiet — that is the rule for the speaker.
• If nothing is heard, first check the volume and the plug, then tell the person in charge of the machine; do not open it.

**Words for my notebook**

Speakers fill the room; headphones serve one ear. Loud sound for long hurts the ear. Keep them dry and clean.

**Let us talk**

1. Is a speaker an input or an output device?
2. Why should we not share earphones?
3. How do you know the sound is too loud for you?
4. What do you do first when no sound comes at all?


![A child stands wearing big headphones with one hand lifted to the ear cup, beside two box speakers on a low table.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/basic-digital-literacy/week9-one-hand-to-the-ear-cup.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A speaker sends ____ out of the machine. A) wind B) paper C) water D) sound
2. A headphone is for A) one person B) the class C) the driver D) the whole room
3. Sound is made when a part of the speaker A) freezes B) dries C) sleeps D) vibrates
4. The safest way to listen is with the volume A) low and steady B) shaking C) at the highest D) off all day
5. Fill in: A blocked hole in an earphone makes us raise the ____, and that hurts the ear.
6. Mention four ways of caring for speakers and headphones.
7. Write two sentences on what sound is and how we keep our ears safe.

**My own work**

Listen to one song or one story at a low volume, then write one line on whether you could still hear a voice in the same room.

Draw the headphones that go over your ears and colour the two cups the same colour.
