# Nigerian History

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing and Awards — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W1–W4, W7–W10 (pp 651–652) — the
school moves these topics down to Nursery 2, so the Primary 1 content is the minimum extent of depth
and the names, dates and deeds below are kept exactly as the scheme sets them.*


### WEEK 1 — HEROES AND HEROINES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W1 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a hero and a heroine are
• name the qualities that make a hero
• tell bravery, kindness and selfless service in my words
• name people who help others in our community

**Things to know**

• A **hero** is a person we admire because of what he **does for others**; a **heroine** is a woman who does the same.
• A hero is not somebody with power over people; a hero is somebody with **hands out to people**.
• Three qualities every hero has: **bravery**, **kindness** and **selfless service**.
• **Bravery** is doing the right thing even when the heart is beating fast with fear.
• **Kindness** is a soft heart: helping without being asked, speaking gently, sharing what I have.
• **Selfless service** is work done for others without asking for payment or praise.
• A hero **tells the truth** when telling it is dangerous, and **keeps his word** when keeping it is costly.
• Heroes are also ordinary: the woman who carries a neighbour's water, the man who fills the hole in the road with stones.
• Heroes are **remembered** — with a name on a street, a story told to children, a statue, a day in the calendar.
• A **bully is the opposite of a hero**; so is a thief, and so is a liar, whatever his size.
• In our own community the hero may be the nurse at the primary health centre who stays after closing.
• We say it together: a hero is known by what he gives, not by what he takes.

**Words for my notebook**

A hero or heroine is a person we admire for helping others — brave, kind and selfless.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a heroine?
2. Tell me something brave you have done, and something you were afraid to do.
3. Who helps people in our community without pay?
4. Why do people remember heroes?

**Worksheet**

1. A hero is a person we A) fear B) pay money C) avoid D) admire for helping others
2. Doing the right thing when you are afraid is A) bravery B) pride C) rest D) play
3. Selfless service means helping others A) without asking for reward B) for a prize C) only on Sunday D) for payment
4. Which is NOT a quality of a hero? A) bravery B) bullying C) truth D) kindness
5. Fill in: A woman who is a hero is called a ____.
6. Mention the three qualities of a hero.
7. Write two sentences on one person in our community who helps others.

**My own work**

Write the name of one person you call a hero in our area and one thing he or she did.

Draw one person who helps your community and colour the cloth they wear.

---

### WEEK 2 — HEROES AND HEROINES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W2 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• name the local heroes of our community
• say what each helper does for us
• tell why teachers, nurses and drivers are heroes
• say that everyday heroes are heroes too

**Things to know**

• A **local hero** is a person who serves the people near us; we can see them, greet them and thank them.
• **Teachers** are heroes: they give knowledge away every day, mark books after class, and stay with a child who has not understood.
• **Doctors and nurses** are heroes: they heal the sick, sit up at night in the clinic, and give medicine without looking at whose child it is.
• **Police officers** are heroes: they keep us from thieves, stand at the crossing, and take those who hurt others to court.
• **Firefighters** are heroes: they run into the smoke when everybody else runs out, and they put out the fire on the house and in the bush.
• Other helpers in the same row: the **midwife**, the **driver** who takes the sick to hospital, the **cleaner** who sweeps the market before dawn, the **water-seller**, the **imam and the pastor** who counsel the quarrelling.
• **Parents and guardians are everyday heroes**: they work, they feed us, they pay school fees, they wake before us and sleep after us.
• Everyday heroes do not wear a special uniform; their heroism is that they **do it again tomorrow**.
• We can thank a local hero: greet them, say "thank you, sir/madam", keep the class clean for the cleaner, do not litter in front of the market woman.
• A hero is also known by the **risk** he takes: the nurse holds the needle, the firefighter holds the pipe, the driver holds the wheel on a bad road.
• Our class will make a wall chart: "Heroes of our community" with one drawing each.

**Words for my notebook**

Teacher, nurse, doctor, police officer, firefighter, parent — all are heroes because they serve us.

**Let us talk**

1. What does a teacher give that nobody can take back from you?
2. What do firefighters do while other people run out?
3. Who is an everyday hero at home, and what did they do yesterday?
4. How can we thank the cleaner of the market?

**Worksheet**

1. Teachers are heroes because they A) drive buses B) sell books C) give us knowledge D) close the school
2. The people who put out fires are A) firefighters B) farmers C) traders D) soldiers
3. Police officers are heroes because they A) sweep the road B) keep us safe from thieves C) cook for the class D) heal the sick
4. An everyday hero at home is A) the man in the stadium B) a father or mother who works for the family C) a stranger on television D) the king only
5. Fill in: The ____ stays with a child who has not understood the lesson.
6. Mention five heroes of our community and what each does.
7. Write two sentences on the helper you would most like to thank.

**My own work**

Draw two heroes of our community and write under each what he or she does for us.

---

### WEEK 3 — NIGERIAN NATIONAL HEROES (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W3 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a national hero is
• name **Herbert Macaulay** and what he did
• say why he is called the father of Nigerian nationalism
• tell his story in four sentences

**Things to know**

• A **national hero** is a person whose work was for the **whole country**, not for one family or one town.
• **Herbert Samuel Johnson Macaulay** was born in Lagos in **1864** and died in **1946**.
• His grandfather was one of the first freed slaves returned to Africa; the family lived in Lagos.
• Macaulay was trained as a **surveyor and engineer** — he measured land and mapped towns for the government.
• He left the government service because the colonial rulers would not treat Nigerians fairly, and then he spent his life **fighting for Nigerians with words, courts and papers** instead of guns.
• He founded the **first political party in Nigeria** (the Nigerian National Democratic Party, 1923) — before that, Nigerians had no party of their own to choose leaders with.
• He started a **newspaper** so that the people could read the truth and not only hear what the chiefs were told.
• He led protests against unfair taxes and against the removal of the people's land; he took the people's cases to court.
• That is why he is called the **Father of Nigerian Nationalism**: he first made Nigerians feel like **one people** with one demand.
• Nationalism means loving your country and asking that it be run by its own people.
• Macaulay is remembered in Lagos by a **street name**, and in the story every school child learns.
• Picture study: an old gentleman in a cap and long coat, with mutton-chop whiskers; that is how the photographs of him look.

**Words for my notebook**

Herbert Macaulay (1864–1946) — father of Nigerian nationalism; he formed the first political party and used the newspaper and the court for Nigeria.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a national hero?
2. Where was Macaulay born?
3. With what three things did he fight for the people?
4. Why is he called the father of nationalism?

**Worksheet**

1. A national hero is a person whose work was for A) the church only B) one shop C) one family D) the whole country
2. Herbert Macaulay was born in the year A) 1999 B) 1960 C) 2000 D) 1864
3. Macaulay formed the first political ____ in Nigeria. A) bus line B) school C) party D) farm
4. Macaulay is called the father of Nigerian A) cinema B) army C) nationalism D) farming
5. Fill in: Macaulay was trained as a ____ and engineer.
6. Mention four things Macaulay did for Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on why we remember Herbert Macaulay.

**My own work**

Copy his name and dates in your book and write one line on what you would do for your country if you were brave like Macaulay.

---

### WEEK 4 — NIGERIAN NATIONAL HEROES (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W4 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• name **Nnamdi Azikiwe** and his praise-name
• say that he was the first President of Nigeria
• tell how he loved education and Nigeria
• take three lessons from his life

**Things to know**

• **Nnamdi Azikiwe** was born in **1904** in Onitsha land and died in **1996**.
• People called him **"Zik of Africa"** — a name the whole continent knew.
• He was a **journalist** who wrote about freedom for black people, and a **student** who read widely and worked hard.
• He founded a **newspaper** (*The West African Pilot*) and used it to teach Nigerians that they could govern themselves.
• He helped to found a political party (**NCNC**) and worked with other leaders to win **independence for Nigeria in 1960**.
• When Nigeria became a republic in **1963**, Azikiwe became the **first President of Nigeria** — the first Nigerian to hold that seat.
• He built a **university** at Nsukka and gave scholarships to Nigerian students; he said education was the way forward for the country.
• Lessons from his life: **read**, **write the truth**, **do not beg for what you can win**, **put the country before your pocket**, **keep on when people say no**.
• Azikiwe spoke of one Nigeria above tribe; he asked that the Igbo, Yoruba and Hausa child sit in the same class.
• His name is on the airport in Abuja, on Nnamdi Azikiwe University, on the 1960s coins and on the street in every city.
• Both our national heroes this term — Macaulay and Azikiwe — fought **with the pen and the platform**, not with a machete.

**Words for my notebook**

Nnamdi Azikiwe (1904–1996), "Zik of Africa", journalist and first President of Nigeria (1963).

**Let us talk**

1. What was Azikiwe's praise-name?
2. What did he use to teach the people about freedom?
3. What office did he hold in 1963?
4. Which lesson from his life will you keep?

**Worksheet**

1. Nnamdi Azikiwe was called Zik of A) Lagos B) Africa C) Europe D) the school
2. Azikiwe became the first ____ of Nigeria. A) driver B) chief C) president D) farmer
3. The tool Azikiwe used to teach the people was the A) newspaper B) flute C) net D) cutlass
4. Azikiwe was born in the year A) 1999 B) 1904 C) 2015 D) 1960
5. Fill in: Azikiwe helped to win ____ for Nigeria in 1960.
6. Mention four lessons we can take from the life of Azikiwe.
7. Write two sentences on what Macaulay and Azikiwe have in common.

**My own work**

Write the praise-name of Azikiwe and one thing you will do this week that shows you love learning.

Draw the flag of Nigeria with green at each side and white in the middle, then colour it.

---

### WEEK 5 — NIGERIAN NATIONAL HEROINES (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W7 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a heroine is
• tell the story of **Queen Amina of Zazzau**
• name the things she built and won
• say that women can be heroes too

**Things to know**

• A **heroine** is a woman whose bravery and work people remember; Nigeria has many.
• **Queen Amina** ruled **Zazzau** — the city we now call **Zaria** — in the old Hausa land, long ago (she is placed by historians about the **1500s**).
• She was the daughter of a chief and was trained from girlhood: riding, fighting, and leading.
• She became the ruler of her people and led her **own armies** on horseback.
• Amina was a **warrior queen**: she fought to make her kingdom safe and wide, and her cavalry was feared.
• She is remembered for the **city walls** — the long banks and ditches round towns called **ganuwa** — some of which are still standing and were named after her.
• She opened **roads and markets** and made the trade routes safe, so that grain, cloth, leather and iron could travel.
• Under her, Zazzau grew rich and strong; her people were fed in the dry season from what the farms stored.
• The lesson the school takes from Amina: **a woman can lead, plan, build and defend**; she is not a side character in history.
• Girls in the class are told her name for the same reason boys are: heroes are not a male word.
• We look at the picture of the Zaria city walls and count how many gates we can see.

**Words for my notebook**

Queen Amina of Zazzau (Zaria), a warrior queen about the 1500s, who led armies and built the city walls called ganuwa.

**Let us talk**

1. Where did Queen Amina rule?
2. What did she learn when she was a girl?
3. What buildings are still remembered with her name?
4. Why do we tell girls about Amina?

**Worksheet**

1. A heroine is A) a queen's servant B) a woman people remember for brave work C) a market title D) a song
2. Queen Amina ruled at A) Zazzau (Zaria) B) Ibadan C) Calabar D) Benin
3. Queen Amina was famous for leading A) a ship B) a farm C) armies on horseback D) a choir
4. The walls round the old towns built in her time are called A) compound wall B) ile C) moat D) ganuwa
5. Fill in: Queen Amina made the trade ____ safe so goods could travel.
6. Mention three things Queen Amina did for her people.
7. Write two sentences on what Queen Amina teaches the girls of our class.

**My own work**

Draw a city wall with a gate and write under it: "Queen Amina of Zazzau built walls to keep her people safe."

---

### WEEK 6 — NIGERIAN NATIONAL HEROINES (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W8 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• tell the story of **Funmilayo Ransome-Kuti**
• say how she helped women
• name **Margaret Ekpo** and her work
• name other notable Nigerian women

**Things to know**

• **Funmilayo Ransome-Kuti** (1900–1978) was a teacher from Abeokuta and one of Nigeria's greatest women.
• She led the **Abeokuta Women's Union** — thousands of market women who were angry that unfair taxes were put on them.
• In **1946–1949** she marched, sang and negotiated with the chiefs and the colonial officer until the tax on the women was **taken away**.
• Her people called her the **"Lioness of Lisabi"** and **Iye oja** — mother of the market.
• She wore her **adedaye cloth** and her cap in front of the rulers so that the women's dress became a badge of dignity, not of shame.
• She fought for **women's right to vote** and to be chosen as representatives; she stood for office herself.
• She was the **first Nigerian woman to drive a car**, she studied abroad, and she travelled to many countries for her people.
• **Margaret Ekpo** (1914–2006) of Aba did the same work in the east: she organized the **market women**, taught them to register to vote, and became one of the **first women in the regional and federal House of Representatives**.
• Both women used **peaceful protest, the song and the petition** — they never told their followers to burn or kill.
• Other notable Nigerian women to name: **Queen Moremi** of Ile-Ife, who gave herself to save her town; **Madam Efunroye Tinubu** of Abeokuta and Lagos, a trader and kingmaker. **Professor Grace Alele-Williams**, mathematician and the first Nigerian woman to be a vice-chancellor; and **Chimamanda Ngozi Adichie**, whose books tell Nigeria to the world.
• We celebrate our heroines with a song, a line of writing and one drawing each on the class board.

**Words for my notebook**

Funmilayo Ransome-Kuti led the Abeokuta market women and removed an unfair tax; Margaret Ekpo organized the women of Aba.

**Let us talk**

1. What were the market women of Abeokuta angry about?
2. What did Funmilayo use instead of fighting?
3. What did Margaret Ekpo do in Aba?
4. Name two other notable Nigerian women and one thing each did.

**Worksheet**

1. Funmilayo Ransome-Kuti was a teacher from A) Jos B) Warri C) Sokoto D) Abeokuta
2. She led the Abeokuta Women's ____ against an unfair tax. A) club B) farm C) choir D) union
3. Margaret Ekpo worked among the market women of A) Enugu only B) Kano C) Aba D) Ibadan
4. Funmilayo Ransome-Kuti was the first Nigerian woman to A) drive a car B) build a road C) own a school D) print paper
5. Fill in: Queen Moremi gave herself to save the town of ____.
6. Mention four ways the heroines of this week fought for their people.
7. Write two sentences on why we call Funmilayo a heroine of Nigeria.

**My own work**

Write the name of one Nigerian heroine and one line on what you would do for your people if you were her.

Draw one heroine you learned about this week and colour the thing she carried.

---

### WEEK 8 — DEVELOPING THE ATTITUDE OF SELFLESS SERVICE (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W9 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• say what selfless service is
• help others without expecting a reward
• be kind to my classmates and friends
• share what I have

**Things to know**

• **Selfless service** is working for somebody's good while you **look after their need, not your own gain**.
• The test of it: if nobody pays you and nobody thanks you, do you still do it?
• Small acts at school: carry a friend's bag when the strap has cut his hand, share chalk, lend a pencil, hold an umbrella for a smaller child, help a blind pupil across the yard.
• Being **kind to classmates**: greet them, do not call names, include the child sitting alone, do not laugh when somebody answers wrongly.
• **Sharing**: if I have three oranges and three of us, each takes one; if I have one, the one who has none is given half and I do not make a noise about it.
• Simple service at home: fetch water, wash my plate, sweep, carry firewood, fan somebody with heat, arrange the chairs — without being told twice.
• Kindness costs nothing and buys everything; a hard word builds a wall, a soft word pulls it down.
• A selfish child helps only when he is watched; a selfless child helps when he sees the need.
• What selflessness is **not**: doing good to be made class captain, giving so that you are given back, helping only the rich child.
• Service has a **cost** of its own: time, comfort, sometimes the football match I missed while I helped; that paid price is the proof.
• Our class rule for this week: **do one unasked thing every day** and put a tick beside the day in your book.

**Words for my notebook**

Selfless service is helping others without asking for a reward or a thank-you.

**Let us talk**

1. What is selfless service?
2. What did you do for someone this week without being asked?
3. Why should we include the child sitting alone?
4. When is giving not yet sharing?

**Worksheet**

1. Selfless service means helping others A) for money B) only at home C) for a prize D) without expecting a reward
2. Which is a small act of service at school? A) holding an umbrella for a smaller child B) taking his bag C) pushing him D) hiding his chalk
3. If I have one biscuit and a friend has none, I should A) share it B) sell it to him C) hide it D) eat it quickly
4. A selfish child helps only when A) the bell rings B) he is tired C) it rains D) he is watched
5. Fill in: A hard word builds a wall; a ____ word pulls it down.
6. Mention four acts of service you can do at home without being told.
7. Write two sentences on one unasked thing you did this week and how it felt.

**My own work**

Keep a tick-box for five days: one unasked helpful thing each day, and write on Friday whether anybody thanked you.

---

### WEEK 9 — DEVELOPING THE ATTITUDE OF SELFLESS SERVICE (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · Second Term W10 (pp 651–652).*

**You will learn to**

• volunteer to help in the community
• keep our environment clean
• help elderly people
• say what a good citizen does

**Things to know**

• To **volunteer** is to put your hand up and say "I will do it" before anybody asks you.
• Service in the community: the class sweeps the street in front of the school, clears the gutter, plants and waters seedlings, picks plastic from the field.
• **Keeping the environment clean** is service to everybody: the dust that is burned or buried is one less sickness in the street.
• Rules of clean service: refuse in the bin, not on the gutter; broken glass wrapped in paper so the sweeper is not cut; wash hands after the work.
• Helping **elderly people**: give them your seat, carry their load, cross them the road, open the door, do not answer them rudely, sit when they speak and listen to the whole story.
• The aged have time for a child that the busy do not; their stories are our history without books.
• Being a **good citizen** in Nursery 2 language: obey the school rules, pay for what you take, do not cheat in the game, tell the truth at the market, respect the flag, keep public property.
• Public property belongs to **everybody and to nobody** — so a desk with a broken top is somebody's younger brother's problem.
• In a **role play** we practise: one child carries a heavy basin, what do you do? An old man cannot find his way, what do you say?
• Service is taught by being seen: when the class leader picks a paper without complaining, three others do the same on the way home.
• Nigeria is held together by people who serve without looking at tribe or religion; help the child who is not from your father's town first.
• We close with our own pledge: *I will keep my place clean, I will help without being asked, I will not wait to be paid for kindness.*

**Words for my notebook**

Volunteer — offer before you are asked. Clean street, helped elder, kept public property: that is a good citizen.

**Let us talk**

1. What does it mean to volunteer?
2. Name three things our class can do to keep the community clean.
3. How do you help an old person at the market?
4. What is a good citizen's behaviour with a school desk?

**Worksheet**

1. To volunteer means to A) refuse work B) wait to be paid C) run away D) offer to help before being asked
2. Cleaning the gutter helps the community because A) it looks busy B) nobody sees it C) water will flow and sickness will fall D) we skip class
3. Broken glass should be A) wrapped in paper before disposal B) kicked aside C) left on the ground D) thrown into the gutter
4. When an old person is speaking we should A) shout B) walk off C) answer rudely D) sit and listen
5. Fill in: A school desk belongs to everybody, so it is called ____ property.
6. Mention four ways of serving our community.
7. Write two sentences on a service you gave the community this term and what it taught you.

**My own work**

Plan one service your group can give before the term ends — a clean sweep of one corner, or watering the school garden for a week — and write the day you will do it.

# Physical and Health Education (PHE)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Physical and Health Education · Second Term W2, W3, W4, W7, W8, W9
and W10, Third Term W1 (pp 552–563) — the master puts this band one class below the scheme, so what
is printed here is the minimum extent of depth to reach.*


### WEEK 1 — DEVELOPMENT THROUGH SPORT ACTIVITIES: EMOTIONAL DEVELOPMENT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W2 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• name the feelings that come when I play
• tell the good feelings from the hard ones
• say how a game helps me manage my feelings
• play on after losing without becoming wild

**Things to know**

• **Emotions** are the feelings of the heart: joy, fear, anger, pride, disappointment.
• Games and sports move my feelings; that is one reason play teaches more than the leg.
• **Good feelings from play**: **zeal** (I am eager to try), **excitement** (my heart is quick with happiness). **contentment** (I am satisfied just to have taken part), **joy**, **confidence** (I believe I can), **pride** in a thing I have done well.
• **Hard feelings from play**: **anxiety** (nervous before the race), **apathy** (I do not care at all), **anger** (somebody fouled me), **boredom** (this game is not for me), **hostility** (I want to hurt the other side), **disappointment** (we lost).
• Every hard feeling has a way through it: **anxiety is beaten by practice**; **apathy is beaten by choosing a game I like**; **anger is beaten by self-control**. **boredom is beaten by trying something new**; **hostility is beaten by playing peacefully**; **disappointment is beaten by good sportsmanship**.
• **Sportsmanship** means: play fairly, lose quietly, win without insulting, shake the hand of the one you beat.
• Winning and losing are **both part of a game** — the one who has never lost has never really played.
• **Resilience** is the heart coming back: I fall, I am sad, and I still run the next race.
• The body and the feeling work together: when I breathe deep and slow, the fear in the chest goes down; when I jump and shout, the joy grows.
• Feeling talk: it is right to say "I am angry"; it is not right to hit because I am angry.
• After every game we ask two questions: *What did I enjoy?* and *What do I do with what I felt?*

**Words for my notebook**

Play brings joy, excitement, confidence and pride; it also brings fear, anger and disappointment. Managing those feelings is what sport teaches.

**Let us talk**

1. How did you feel the last time your team lost?
2. What do you do when you are angry in a game?
3. Name a feeling you like to have while playing.
4. What does "contentment" mean after a game you did not win?


![One child sits on the bench with head bent while another stands beside it with an arm stretched out in welcome, a plain ball on the ground.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week1-the-hand-held-out.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Emotions are the A) feelings of the heart B) rules of the game C) shoes we wear D) muscles of the leg
2. Feeling sure that you can do it is called A) fear B) anger C) confidence D) sleep
3. When we lose and do not fight or cry wildly we show good A) sportsmanship B) noise C) speed D) dress
4. Nervousness before a race is best beaten by A) running away B) shouting C) practice D) skipping
5. Fill in: The heart coming back after a fall or a loss is called ____.
6. Mention three good feelings that come from playing and two hard feelings.
7. Write two sentences on a game that made you angry and what you did about it.

**My own work**

Play one game this week and write one line on the feeling you had at the start, one at the end, and what you did with it.

Draw two children sharing one ball and colour the ball.

---

### WEEK 2 — DEVELOPMENT THROUGH SPORT ACTIVITIES: SOCIAL DEVELOPMENT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W3 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• play with others toward one goal
• name the ten social things a game teaches
• take turns at leading and at following
• welcome a new player into my game

**Things to know**

• **Social development** is learning to live and work with other people; a game is a small town with rules.
• **Cooperation** is working together and helping a team-mate when his line fails.
• **Teamwork** is all of us pushing toward one goal, each knowing his own job: one throws, one catches, one runs.
• **Tolerance** is accepting that another child is slower and I do not laugh; being patient with a mistake.
• **Friendliness** is kind words to team-mate and to opponent; a game makes friends faster than a classroom does.
• **Fairness** is playing by the rules, not cheating, and accepting what the one who judges says.
• **Hospitality** in a game means welcoming the new child who does not know the rules yet, and teaching him instead of sending him out.
• **Respect** is for the one who trains us, for the opponent, for the team-mate, for the equipment and for the rule.
• **Communication** is speaking clearly to my side ("here!", "mine!") and listening when instructions come; shouting nonsense is not communication.
• **Leadership** has two faces: sometimes I lead the line, sometimes I follow — both are needed and neither is bigger.
• These are **life skills**: the child who can wait for his turn in a game can wait his turn at a counter when he is grown.
• Character is built when nobody is watching the ball and I still do not push.

**Words for my notebook**

Cooperation, teamwork, tolerance, friendliness, fairness, hospitality, respect, communication, leadership — a game teaches all ten.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the difference between cooperation and teamwork?
2. A new child does not know the rules: what do you do?
3. When is it your turn to lead, and when to follow?
4. Which social lesson was hardest for you this week?


![Four children hold hands in a ring with one gap left open, and a smaller child is being led into that gap.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week2-the-ring-opens-for-a-new-player.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Working together toward one goal is A) teamwork B) resting C) running D) fighting
2. Accepting another child's mistake without laughing is A) speed B) luck C) tolerance D) anger
3. Welcoming a new player into your game is A) hospitality B) silence C) fear D) pride
4. Sometimes leading and sometimes following is called A) rest B) leadership C) luck D) shouting
5. Fill in: Speaking clearly to your side and listening to instructions is ____.
6. Mention five things a game teaches us about living with people.
7. Write two sentences on a time you helped a team-mate.

**My own work**

Play one team game this week and write the name of one thing you did for the team rather than for yourself.

Draw the team you played with and colour every shirt a different colour.

---

### WEEK 3 — MARCHING EXERCISE: CONCEPT AND IMPORTANCE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W4 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• stand at attention
• march on the spot and march forward in time
• turn left, right and about on command
• say why we march

**Things to know**

• **Marching** is organized walking in rhythm and in a formation — the feet step, the arms swing, everybody moves as one.
• **At attention**: heels together, toes a little apart, body straight, arms at the sides, eyes front, mouth quiet, chest up.
• **Arm swing**: the arm swings from the shoulder, not the elbow; the hand goes to about the level of the waist-belt in front and a little behind.
• **High knee** on the spot: lift the knee toward the chest, put the foot down on the ball, alternate.
• **Forward march**: the left foot starts; the step is short and even; I do not look at my feet.
• **Spacing**: an arm's length to the child in front and to each side, so that no one is kicked and no one is left behind.
• **Commands**: "Attention!" "Forward — march!" "Halt!" "Left turn!" "Right turn!" "About — face!"
• **Turning**: on "Left turn" I turn on the heel of the left foot and the ball of the right, ninety degrees, sharp and without stumbling.
• **About-face** is a half turn to the left, done in one movement, not two.
• Marching teaches **discipline and focus**: the ear listens for the word, the body waits for it.
• It gives **good posture**, **coordination** of hand and leg, **teamwork** (all feet fall on the same beat), and it trains **listening**.
• We march for events: parade, the founding day, the sports fete, a funeral honour, the flag day — marching is how a people show that they are organized and respectful.
• The beat comes from a drum; the left foot falls on the strong beat, so a class of forty makes one sound.

**Words for my notebook**

Attention — forward march — halt. Left foot starts, arms swing from the shoulder, one arm's length between us.

**Let us talk**

1. Which foot starts when we march forward?
2. What does "at attention" look like?
3. Why must there be space between us?
4. Name two events where people march.


![One child stands at attention with heels together and arms at the sides while the other marches on the spot with a knee lifted.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week3-standing-straight-and-marching.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Marching is best described as A) organized walking in rhythm B) sleeping well C) hopping on one leg D) running freely
2. The command that makes everybody stand straight and quiet is A) Halt B) Attention C) March D) Rest
3. When we march forward the first foot to move is the A) right B) both C) left D) none
4. The distance we keep from the child in front is about A) an arm's length B) a mile C) two steps only D) a foot
5. Fill in: "____ — face!" turns us right round.
6. Mention four benefits of marching.
7. Write two sentences on how the class looked and sounded when you marched as one.

**My own work**

Practice marching on the spot in your own space for one minute, twenty times high knee, and write whether your arms and legs went together.

Draw six children marching one behind the other and colour their shoes.

---

### WEEK 4 — BODY EXERCISE: SWINGING, TWISTING, JUMPING AND BENDING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W7 (Swinging and Twisting) and W8 (Jumping and Bending), pp 552–557 — the school keeps both in one week.*

**You will learn to**

• swing arms and legs with control
• twist the trunk from side to side
• jump on two feet and land soft
• bend forward, sideways and down

**Things to know**

• **Swinging** moves a body part back and forth, or round and round, without leaving the ground.
• **Arm swings**: forward and back, side to side, big circles front and big circles back; both arms together, then one after the other.
• **Leg swings**: hold the wall or a bench, swing one leg forward and back, then across the body side to side.
• **Trunk swing**: bend a little from the waist and let both arms swing from side to side, touching the ground on each side.
• **Twisting** turns a part round on itself: hands on waist, hips still, shoulders left and right — look over the shoulder each time.
• Neck turns are **gentle and slow**: chin toward the shoulder, never a hard circle. Wrist and ankle rotations go round both ways.
• Swinging **loosens the joints**, twisting **improves flexibility and range of motion**, and both **warm the muscles** before the fast work.
• **Jumping**: two feet together, arms up, land with the knees bent — a **soft landing** means the feet say nothing.
• Kinds of jump: **jumping jacks** (arms and legs out, then in), jump and turn a quarter or half way, jump forward and back, side to side, over a rope on the ground, into a pattern of squares.
• **High jump** reaches up to touch; **long jump** goes forward for distance from a short run and two feet.
• **Bending**: forward bend to touch the toes, side bend left and right with the arm over the head, knee bends (squats) with the heels down and back straight.
• A backward bend is done **only with help and on a soft mat**, never alone and never on a hard floor.
• All movement is **controlled, not jerky**; a bounce that hurts is a sign to stop.
• The four movements go into a routine: warm with swings, turn with twists, be loud with jumps, end with slow bends and a breath.

**Words for my notebook**

Swing, twist, jump, bend. Warm up first, land softly with bent knees, never jerk a movement.

**Let us talk**

1. Which movement loosens the joints first?
2. Show me a soft landing.
3. Why do we not do a hard circle with the neck?
4. Count your jumping jacks aloud — how many did you do?


![Two children do the body exercises: one jumps with both feet off the ground and arms swung up, the other bends forward with arms hanging toward the feet.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week4-the-jump-and-the-bend.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Moving a body part to and fro without leaving the ground is A) swinging B) jumping C) bending only D) sleeping
2. A jump lands best with the knees A) crossed B) locked C) straight D) bent
3. Twisting the shoulders from side to side improves A) appetite B) eyesight C) flexibility D) hearing
4. A backward bend should be done A) with eyes closed only B) on a hard floor C) alone and fast D) with help on a soft mat
5. Fill in: A routine begins with ____ so that the muscles are warm.
6. Mention four kinds of jump and two kinds of bend.
7. Write two sentences on the movement that was hardest for you today.

**My own work**

Do ten arm swings, ten twists each way, ten jumps and ten forward bends in your own space, and write which one made you breathe hardest.

Draw yourself jumping in the air and colour the shoes you jumped in.

---

### WEEK 5 — NEUROMUSCULAR SKILLS: CLIMBING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W9 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• say what neuromuscular means
• climb steps, a low ladder and a frame with control
• place my hands and feet before I move my weight
• say the safety rules of climbing

**Things to know**

• **Neuromuscular** means **the nerves (brain and messages) working with the muscles**: the eye sees, the brain decides, the muscle moves, and the body keeps balance.
• **Climbing** is moving the body up or over something using hands and feet together.
• Before I move: **three points stay, one point moves** — two hands and a foot on the ladder while the other foot searches for the rung.
• **Hand-eye and foot-eye coordination**: the hand goes exactly where the eye put it; the foot finds the rung without looking down too much.
• **Body awareness** is knowing where my legs are without looking; it is trained by slow climbing, not fast.
• Ways to climb stairs: forward one step at a time, two steps at a time, sideways holding the rail, and backward very carefully with help.
• Ways on the playground: up the **jungle gym** bars, along the ladder, down the slide's steps; **over obstacles** — a mat, a bench at low height, a soft block.
• A **wall climb** is only on a wall made for it, with a mat below and the belt and the hold checked.
• Strength needed: **arms** to hold, **legs** to push (the legs do most of the work), **trunk** to keep the body steady.
• **Problem solving**: I look up first and plan my route — which hand, which rung, where to come down.
• **Confidence and courage**: I climb as high as I am ready; the fear that stops me at a bad hold is a good teacher, and I come down slowly rather than jump.
• **Safety**: only where an adult has said yes, on a mat or sand below, no pushing under me. No climbing with a slick hand, nothing sharp or heavy in the pocket, one child on a rung range at a time.
• Climbing is a **full-body** work; after it we shake the hands and walk the legs before sitting.

**Words for my notebook**

Neuromuscular = brain, nerves and muscles working together. In climbing: three points stay, one moves; plan the route first.

**Let us talk**

1. What does "three points stay, one moves" keep you from?
2. Which part of the body does most of the pushing in a climb?
3. What do you check before you put your weight on a hold?
4. Name two safety rules of the climbing frame.


![One child is halfway up a low climbing frame with a hand and a foot on the same rung while another stands waiting at the side.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week5-a-hand-and-a-foot-before-the-weight.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Neuromuscular means the nerves working with the A) ball B) shoes C) muscles D) ears
2. On a ladder, while one foot moves, how many points should still be on it? A) none B) one C) three D) half
3. In climbing, most of the pushing is done by the A) legs B) chin C) nose D) fingers
4. Looking up to decide which rung to use is a form of A) problem solving B) singing C) jumping D) sleeping
5. Fill in: We climb only where an adult has said ____ and with a mat below.
6. Mention four things climbing improves in the body.
7. Write two sentences on the highest thing you climbed this week and how you felt.

**My own work**

Climb a low, safe thing twice — once slowly and once a little faster — and write which one felt more in control.

Draw a ladder and colour the rung your hand reaches highest.

---

### WEEK 6 — TRACK EVENTS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W10 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• run a short race straight to the line
• take the standing and the crouching start
• run a race in pairs with a partner
• keep the rules and the spirit of the race

**Things to know**

• A **track event** is a race run on a marked path called the **track**; on the day of the sports fete we run in our **lane** and do not cross into another child's.
• Our short distance is the **25-metre race** — about the length of the class hall laid flat.
• The **standing start**: feet one behind the other, weight forward, eyes on the line.
• The **crouching start**: on the marks, hands behind the line, fingers making a bridge, one knee down; at the word the legs push the body out low.
• The signal: "On your marks — set — go!" (or a whistle); I move **only** at the last sound, not before.
• **Running form**: eyes front, mouth a little open, arms bent and swinging from the shoulder, knees lifting, land on the front of the foot, do not twist the body.
• **Do not slow down before the line**: run **through** the finish, ten steps past it, then walk — never stop dead, because the legs must carry the blood back.
• **Rules**: stay in your lane, no pushing, no cutting the corner, no blocking, do the false start again if told, and take the result of the one who judges.
• **Sportsmanship**: greet before, shake hands after, thank the one who lost to you; a win by cheating is a loss.
• In **running in pairs** the two of you run side by side to the line at the same speed; one waits if the other stumbles.
• A harder pair game: the two hold a bean bag or a small stick **between you** without hands and run without dropping it — that is cooperation carrying speed.
• Everybody who runs is celebrated, not only the first: we clap the last child home loudest, because he did not stop.

**Words for my notebook**

Track = the marked path. Move only at "go", run through the line, walk after, shake hands whatever happened.

**Let us talk**

1. How far is our short race?
2. Show me the position of the hands at a crouching start.
3. Why do we not stop dead at the line?
4. What do you do if your partner stumbles in a pair race?


![Two children run side by side toward a small flag standing on the finishing line.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week6-the-race-to-the-flag.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A track event is a A) board game B) song C) race on a marked path D) swim in a bucket
2. The word we move on is A) the last signal, such as a whistle B) "run" whispered C) the bell for break D) the clapping
3. Good running form keeps the eyes A) closed B) upward only C) to the front D) on the feet
4. After crossing the line we should A) lie on the track B) turn back fast C) sit down at once D) keep going a little then walk
5. Fill in: In a pair race, if your partner stumbles, you ____ for him.
6. Mention the two kinds of start and three rules of a race.
7. Write two sentences on a race you ran and how you behaved at the end.

**My own work**

Time yourself over ten long steps on a safe ground three times, and write which run was the fastest and how you started it.

---

### WEEK 8 — POSTURE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W11 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• say what posture means
• sit, stand, read and sleep well
• tell what bad posture does to the body
• keep a straight back when I write

**Things to know**

• **Posture** is the position in which I hold my body when I sit, stand, move or rest.
• **Good sitting posture**: back against the chair, bottom well back, both **feet flat on the floor**, back straight, shoulders relaxed, head level and not pushed forward.
• Bad sitting: **slouching** (body sunk down, back curved), one leg under the body, chin on the desk, the head bent to one hand.
• Sitting badly **hurts the back and the neck**, makes breathing shallow (the chest is squeezed), and makes it hard to concentrate; the child who looks tired may only be sitting crooked.
• **Good standing posture**: feet about hip-width apart, weight on both feet evenly, knees not locked, stomach gently in, shoulders back and down, head up, chin level, arms loose at the sides.
• Bad standing: leaning on one hip, head forward, shoulders rolled in, knees bent backwards — these tire the legs and bend the spine.
• **Good reading posture**: the book or paper held about the length of a forearm from the eyes, **at a level a little below the eyes**. Back supported, light coming from in front or the side, **not** lying on the stomach or in a dark corner; rest the eyes now and then.
• Reading in bad light or too close pulls the eyes and gives a headache; that is why we do not read while walking or in a moving bus.
• **Good sleeping posture**: on the **side** with the knees a little bent and a pillow between them, or on the **back** with a thin pillow; the **front (tummy)** position twists the neck and presses the chest — avoid it.
• The pillow should not push the head high; the mattress should be firm enough that the back does not sink into a hammock.
• **Carrying posture**: the school bag is worn on **both shoulders** and sits high on the back; a bag hung on one shoulder pulls the spine sideways.
• A simple check: heels, bottom and shoulders touching the wall, eyes front — if the head cannot find the wall without tilting, the sitting of this week has bent it forward.
• Good posture looks **confident** before it feels healthy: the straight child appears ready, and in our mock examination practice we sit tall on purpose.

**Words for my notebook**

Posture = how I hold my body. Feet flat, back straight, eyes an arm's length from the book, bag on both shoulders.

**Let us talk**

1. What is posture?
2. Show me how you should sit when you write.
3. Which sleeping position should we avoid?
4. Why is a one-shoulder bag bad for the back?


![Two children at two desks: one sits straight-backed with both feet flat, the other is bent double with a leg tucked under the chair.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week8-the-straight-back-and-the-bent-one.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Posture means the way we A) choose shoes B) hold our body C) sing D) eat food
2. While sitting to write, the feet should be A) raised B) flat on the floor C) crossed behind D) on the stool rung
3. The best sleeping position among these is A) face down with a tall pillow B) on the side C) on the elbow D) on the tummy
4. A school bag should be carried on A) both shoulders B) the head only C) the fingers D) one shoulder
5. Fill in: While reading, the book should be about an arm's length from the ____.
6. Mention two effects of bad sitting on the body.
7. Write two sentences on the posture you must correct in yourself.

**My own work**

Set a line on your exercise book and write one page sitting tall with both feet flat; then write a second line slouched and compare the two.

Draw one child sitting straight and one bending over; colour the one with good posture.

---

### WEEK 9 — INDOOR GAMES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Third Term W1 (pp 558–563).*

**You will learn to**

• say what an indoor game is
• name games we can play inside
• play Ludo, puzzles and simple card games well
• balance indoor play with outdoor play

**Things to know**

• An **indoor game** is a game played **inside a building**, in a room or a hall, that needs **little space** and makes **little noise**.
• We play indoor games when the **weather is bad**: rain, too much sun, or a harmattan so dusty that running would hurt the chest.
• Indoor games exercise the **mind**: thinking, remembering, counting, planning, waiting.
• **Ludo**: a board, a dice and four pieces each; you throw, count the squares, and the piece that reaches **home** first wins — patience and counting, not running.
• **Chess** begins with names: the king, queen, rooks on the corners, knights and bishops, the line of pawns in front; we learn how the pieces move, and the full game comes later.
• **Checkers (draughts)**: men move forward diagonally, capture by jumping, and become kings at the far line.
• **Dominoes**: matching ends with the same number of spots; a child learns to count the pips and to plan two turns ahead.
• **Card games** we can play: matching pairs by number or colour, and the **memory game** with cards face down — turn two, if they match keep them; that game trains the eye and the memory.
• **Puzzles**: putting pieces back into a picture, counting and matching; **Snakes and Ladders** teaches the ladder up and the snake down while we count.
• A **table game** needs a table or a mat; a **video game** needs a screen, and its rules are the screen's: short time, good light, the pause at the eyes.
• Indoor manners: no shouting, do not flip the board when losing, take turns, put the pieces back in the box so the next class can play.
• **Balance**: the body still needs outdoor running; an hour of the mind inside is answered by an hour of the legs outside.

**Words for my notebook**

Indoor games are quiet games for a room: Ludo, cards, puzzles, dominoes, checkers, chess. Mind and legs both need play.

**Let us talk**

1. Why do we play indoor games on a rainy day?
2. What must happen to a piece in Ludo to win?
3. What does the memory game train?
4. How do we behave when we are losing a board game?


![A plain board game with a cross path and four counters, six puzzle pieces half joined into a rectangle, and two cards lying face down.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/physical-and-health-education/week9-the-board-the-puzzle-and-two-cards.png)
**Worksheet**

1. An indoor game is played A) in the river B) inside a room C) on the field D) in the rain outside
2. In Ludo the winner is the one whose piece first A) reaches home B) turns over C) leaves the board D) falls down
3. The card game with cards face down trains A) the memory B) the legs C) the lungs D) the voice
4. Which game uses pieces with spots to match ends? A) football B) skipping C) dominoes D) hopscotch
5. Fill in: The pieces of a board game must be put back in the ____ after play.
6. Mention five indoor games.
7. Write two sentences on an indoor game you enjoyed and what it made your mind do.

**My own work**

Play one indoor game with a friend or a sibling, and write what you had to think about while you played.

# Prevocational Studies (PVS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W4 (pp 833) and Third Term W7
(School Garden Activities, p 835) — this subject is redistributed into the lower classes, so the
Primary 4 weeks are the ceiling of vocabulary and the school's own rows decide what each week is about.*


### WEEK 1 — FARMERS AND THEIR WORK

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (Concept of Agriculture, p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say who a farmer is
• name the kinds of farmer and the work each does
• tell the daily work of a crop farmer
• say that farming is a job that needs skill

**Things to know**

• A **farmer** is a person who grows crops or keeps animals for food and for selling.
• Not everybody who has a farm is a farmer: the one who **works** the land is the farmer.
• Kinds of farmer: **crop farmer** (yam, cassava, maize, rice, vegetables) · **animal keeper** (poultry birds, goat, sheep, pig, cattle) · **fish farmer** (fish in a pond) · **tree crop farmer** (cocoa, oil palm, rubber).  The **forester** grows and keeps the trees.
• Work of the crop farmer, in order: **clear the land → till and make the beds → choose and plant the seed → weed**.  Then **water or wait for rain → guard against pest and bird → harvest → dry, store and sell**.
• The animal keeper's daily work: **open the house, give water, give feed, clean out the litter, look for the sick one, collect the eggs, close the house at night** so the thief and the dog do not come.
• The fish farmer feeds the fish at set times, keeps the water moving and clean, and nets the big ones out.
• A farmer also **keeps record**: how many bags, how many birds, how much was spent on bag and on medicine.
• Farming work begins early: the farmer is on the farm before the sun is high, when the body is cool and the weeds are soft.
• Farming gives the town its food, the market its money, the factory its raw material — so the farmer works for all of us.
• Tools and machines belong to the next weeks; today we know that **the hand, the plan and the patience** are the farmer's first three tools.

**Words for my notebook**

A farmer grows crops or keeps animals for food and for sale. Clear, plant, weed, water, harvest, sell.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is a farmer?
2. Name three kinds of farmer you know.
3. What does a poultry keeper do every morning?
4. Why does a farmer begin work early?


![A hoe, a sheathed cutlass, a watering can, a knapsack sprayer with its hose coiled and a tipped basket of seeds lie before three young plants.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week1-the-tools-of-a-farm-worker.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A farmer is a person who A) grows crops or keeps animals B) mends shoes C) sells only cloth D) drives a bus
2. A poultry farmer keeps A) goats only B) birds C) fish D) bees only
3. Which work comes first on a new farm? A) harvest B) drying C) selling D) clearing the land
4. Collecting eggs, giving water and cleaning the house are the work of a A) fisherman only B) tailor C) driver D) poultry keeper
5. Fill in: After planting, the farmer removes the ____ so the crop can grow well.
6. Mention the six works of a crop farmer from clearing to selling.
7. Write two sentences on a farmer you know and what he or she grows or keeps.

**My own work**

Find out what one farmer near us grows or keeps, and write the name of the crop or animal and one thing that person does every day.

Draw a farmer's cutlass, hoe and basket, then colour the basket.

---

### WEEK 2 — FARM VISITS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833) — the farm visit is where the concept of agriculture is seen.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a farm visit is and why we go
• observe and report what I saw on the farm
• keep the rules of the visit
• name the things on the farm I want to ask about

**Things to know**

• A **farm visit** is when the class goes to a farm to **see with the eyes** what was told in the class.
• We go to a **crop farm**, a **poultry house**, a **fish pond**, a **garden**, or the school's own plot.
• Before we start: the bag is left in the class, the feet are shut well, the hat keeps off the sun, and the water flask is filled.
• Rules of the visit: **walk, do not run on the beds; touch nothing without being told; do not pick the fruit; do not chase the birds; stay with the line; ask, then listen**.
• What to look for on a crop farm: the **shape and height of the plant**, the **kind of soil**, how the **rows** are lined, whether there are **weeds**, whether the beds are **watered or dry**. And any **hole or yellow leaf** that shows a pest.
• What to look for in the animal house: the **type of feed**, the **water pot**, the **smell and cleanliness**, the **space per animal**, and whether any animal sits **apart** — that is the sick one.
• What to ask: *When did you plant? What do you feed them? What kills your crop? Where do you sell? What is dear this year?*
• The visit has **three parts**: before (what we will look for), during (what we see and hear), after (what we write and draw).
• Our **report** has: the name of the farm, the day, the person who showed us, three things we saw, one thing we learnt, one question we still have.
• When we come back we **wash the hands and the shoes**, and the mud is not carried into the class.
• A farm visit teaches respect for the labour: the bowl of rice is a year of somebody's back.

**Words for my notebook**

On a farm visit we look, listen and ask; we touch nothing without leave and we write a report after.

**Let us talk**

1. Why do we go to a farm instead of only reading about one?
2. Name two rules of the visit.
3. What on the farm did you want to touch most?
4. What will your report contain?


![The farm row: a rope fence, a goat with its rope lying loose, a basket of maize, a heap of yams, two hens at a feeder and a cutlass on a stump.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week2-what-we-saw-on-the-farm.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A farm visit means the class goes to the farm to A) buy food for the school B) sleep C) see things with the eyes D) hide
2. On the farm we must A) run on the beds B) chase the birds C) pick the fruit D) walk and stay with the line
3. An animal sitting apart from the others is probably A) new B) sleeping well C) sick D) happy
4. A good question to ask the farmer is A) where do you live? B) how old are you? C) when did you plant? D) what is your name?
5. Fill in: Before the visit we leave our bags in the ____ and shut our shoes well.
6. Mention five things to look for on a crop farm.
7. Write two sentences on the farm we visited or would like to visit and what you saw there.

**My own work**

Write your farm-visit report on one page: where we went, three things you saw, one thing you learnt and one question you still have.

---

### WEEK 3 — COMMON FARM TOOLS: HOE AND CUTLASS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W3–W4 (Simple Agricultural Tools, p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• draw and name the hoe and the cutlass
• say what each tool is used for
• say which part of each tool does the work
• keep the tool safe and store it well

**Things to know**

• A **farm tool** is a thing we hold in the hand to make farm work easier; most of ours are **simple tools** — no engine on them.
• The **hoe** has a **handle** (wood) and a broad flat **blade** at a right angle; it is used for **tilling, making ridges and beds, earthing up the plant and weeding between rows**.
• The blade of the hoe is **pushed into the soil and pulled** — the digging is by the arms and the back, not by a swing.
• The **cutlass** (cutlass, a short heavy blade with a wooden handle) is for **clearing**: cutting grass, brushing small sticks, cutting the weed and slashing the bush.
• A cutlass cuts by a **clean swinging stroke**; the blade must be sharp — a blunt blade needs more force and slips more.
• **Parts of a tool**: **blade** (the working metal part), **edge** (the cutting side), **handle** (the part in the hand), **tang/ferrule** (the joint that holds the blade on the handle).
• A tool with a loose handle must be **fixed before it is used**, or the blade will fly off in the swing.
• Safe use: clear the ground of stones and people first, keep an arm's length of **space** between two workers, never swing toward a person, and never carry a cutlass point-forward.
• Carry a cutlass **blade down and away** from the body, or sheathed; carry the hoe with the blade down.
• Care: wipe the mud off after use, keep the edge dry so rust does not eat it, put a drop of oil on the metal now and then; a rust-eaten tool breaks.
• **Storage**: tools hang on a wall or lie on a rack, **edge down or sheathed**, out of the rain, and never where a child will step on them.
• Sharpening is done with a **file or a stone** by an adult, and the blade is held away from the body.

**Words for my notebook**

Hoe: blade and handle — for tilling, ridging and weeding. Cutlass: a sharp edge — for clearing. Clean, dry, sheathed, hung up.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part of the hoe goes into the soil?
2. What do we use a cutlass for?
3. Why must a tool handle be tight?
4. Where should a cutlass be kept after work?


![A long-handled hoe with its flat blade on the ground, and beside it a short cutlass lying in its open sheath.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week3-the-hoe-and-the-cutlass.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The tool used for tilling and making ridges is the A) basket B) cutlass C) rake D) hoe
2. The part of the tool you hold is the A) tang B) blade C) handle D) edge
3. A cutlass works best when its edge is A) wet B) sharp C) blunt D) bent
4. Before swinging a cutlass we must first A) clear people and stones from the ground B) eat C) count the leaves D) water the plant
5. Fill in: Mud is ____ off a tool after use so that rust will not eat it.
6. Draw and label the hoe and the cutlass.
7. Write two sentences on how a tool should be stored after work.

**My own work**

Draw the two tools again, name the four parts of each, and write one rule of safe use under each drawing.

---

### WEEK 4 — COMMON FARM TOOLS: RAKE AND WATERING CAN

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W3–W4 (p 833), with Third Term W7 (School Garden Activities) for the watering work.*

**You will learn to**

• name and draw the rake and the watering can
• say what each is used for
• level a seedbed and water a garden the right way
• tell which tool is for the bed and which for the path

**Things to know**

• The **rake** has a long handle and a cross head with **teeth (prongs)**; it is a tool for **gathering, leveling and smoothing**.
• Work of the rake: pull the **stones, sticks and old leaves** out of the bed; break the small clods; **level** the soil after the hoe; **smooth** the surface before seeds are sown; clean the path.
• **Level** means the top of the bed is flat all across, so the water stands evenly and does not run off one side.
• Two kinds: the **garden/leaf rake** with springy teeth, and the **heavy soil rake** with stiff teeth; the light one is ours.
• A rake is not a toy for the shoulder and never used to fight; its teeth catch a hand or an eye.
• A **watering can** is a vessel that carries water and pours it gently; it has a **body**, a **handle** on top and behind, a **spout**, and a **rose (sprinkler head)** full of small holes.
• The **rose** breaks the water into a soft rain so that a small plant is not knocked flat and the seed is not washed out of the ground.
• Without the rose the water comes as a **stream** — that is for filling a tub, not for a seedbed.
• Water in the **morning or evening**; at noon the sun takes the water back before the roots have drunk, and cold water on hot leaves scorches them.
• Water the **soil around the plant**, not the leaf from far above; a wet leaf at night invites disease.
• New seeds and seedlings want a little water **often**; a big plant wants more water **less often** so the roots go down looking for it.
• In the school garden our work is: rake the bed smooth → mark the rows → sow → water with the rose → weed when the leaf is dry → watch growth.
• Care: the can is emptied and left with its lid open and mouth down so it does not rust or smell; the rake is cleared of soil and hung up.

**Words for my notebook**

Rake: teeth for gathering, leveling and smoothing. Watering can: spout and rose for a soft rain, watered in the cool of the day.

**Let us talk**

1. What are the teeth of the rake for?
2. What does the rose on the watering can do?
3. When is the best time to water the garden?
4. Which tool makes the top of the bed flat?


![A rake with eight straight teeth stands beside a watering can with a long spout, with three young plants and a levelled seedbed between them.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week4-the-rake-and-the-watering-can.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The tool with teeth used for leveling a seedbed is the A) rake B) hoe only C) spade D) cutlass
2. The part of the watering can that makes the water fall like rain is the A) base B) lid C) handle D) rose
3. We water the garden best in the A) night with no light B) morning or evening C) hot noon D) rain only
4. Before sowing seeds, the surface of the bed is A) burnt B) covered in stones C) smoothed with the rake D) salted
5. Fill in: A rake is used to ____ the garden of leaves and small sticks.
6. Mention the parts of a watering can.
7. Write two sentences on how you watered a plant and what the plant did after.

**My own work**

Water the same plant every morning for four days, keep the bed raked smooth, and write one line each day on how it looks.

---

### WEEK 5 — COMMON FARM TOOLS: SPADE AND SHOVEL

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W3–W4 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• name and draw the spade and the shovel
• tell the spade from the shovel
• say which work each does best
• dig a hole with a straight back

**Things to know**

• The **spade** and the **shovel** look like sisters: both have a **long handle**, a **D or T grip** at the top and a **broad flat blade** at the bottom. Both are pushed with the **foot** and lifted with the **back and arms**.
• The **spade** blade is **flat and square-ish with a sharp top edge** — made for **digging**, cutting the soil, lifting turves, and for the hard ground.
• The **shovel** blade is **curved and hollow with a pointed or rounded front** — made for **lifting and moving** loose things: soil, sand, ash, grain, manure.
• The rule the farmers say: **a spade digs, a shovel moves.**
• Parts to name: **grip (handle top)**, **handle (shaft)**, **blade (scoop)**, **socket (the cup that joins the blade to the handle)**, **edge (the part that enters the soil)**.
• The soil is first **watered or loosened**; dry hard ground breaks a tool.
• To dig safely: feet apart, **back straight**, bend the **knees**, turn the soil with the hips and not with a twist of the waist; the load is carried close to the body.
• Dig by **slicing a rectangle** and lifting it out, not by hacking a big piece; a clean hole is a deep hole.
• A **round-point shovel** digs in soft ground and moves a heap; a **square-point (transfer) shovel** is the one for grain and sand.
• On a farm both are used to **dig the planting hole or pit**, to **make a heap of manure**, to **cover the seed**, to **open a drain** and to **load** the wheelbarrow.
• Care: wash the mud off, dry it, oil the blade lightly, store it **blade down on a board** or hanging. And never leave it standing in the sun on the bare ground where it rusts and the handle dries and cracks.
• A spade is not for prying stones loose by leverage, and never for hitting a nail.

**Words for my notebook**

Spade: flat blade for digging. Shovel: hollow curved blade for lifting and moving. Straight back, bent knees, load close.

**Let us talk**

1. Which tool has the hollow curved blade?
2. What do we do to the ground before we dig it?
3. Show me how you bend to lift a heavy scoop.
4. How do we keep a spade from rusting?


![The two digging tools stand side by side so the blades can be told apart: a flat square-topped spade blade and a deep pointed shovel blade.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week5-the-flat-spade-and-the-pointed-shovel.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The tool whose blade is flat and square, used for digging, is the A) basket B) rake C) hoe D) spade
2. A shovel is best for A) watering B) sowing seeds C) cutting grass D) lifting and moving loose soil
3. Before digging hard ground we should A) salt it B) burn it C) water or loosen it D) leave it
4. When we lift a heavy scoop of soil we should A) twist the neck B) bend the knees and keep the back straight C) hold the breath only D) bend the waist
5. Fill in: The farmers say a spade ____, a shovel moves.
6. Mention the five parts of a spade.
7. Write two sentences on how a spade and a shovel differ.

**My own work**

Draw both tools side by side and write under the picture which one you would take for (a) digging a new bed and (b) moving a heap of sand.

---

### WEEK 6 — COMMON FARM TOOLS: WHEELBARROW AND BASKET

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W3–W4 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• name and draw the wheelbarrow and the basket
• say what each carries on the farm
• load and push a barrow without spoiling the back
• mend and keep them well

**Things to know**

• A **wheelbarrow (barrow)** is a **one-wheeled cart** with a box (tray), two handles and two legs; one person pushes it and it carries the load of many.
• Parts of a barrow: **wheel and tyre**, **frame**, **tray (box)**, **handles (shafts)**, **legs (stands)**, and sometimes a **axle pin**.
• The wheel at the front makes it easy to turn and to lift over a stone; the legs let it stand down without tipping.
• A barrow carries: **manure, soil, sand, water in a drum, harvested crop, bags, stones, waste** — from the heap to the bed, from the farm to the gate.
• A **basket** is a **woven carrier** made of **palm frond, raffia, bamboo, cane or plastic**; it is carried on the **head**, on the **back with a strap**, on the **hip** or in the **hands**.
• A basket carries **yam tubers, cassava, tomatoes, peppers, eggs, maize in a bag, leaves and grass** — the harvest of a small farm.
• Baskets come in sizes: the small **hand basket**, the big **market basket**, the **winnowing basket** with a wide flat rim used to fan chaff from grain.
• Loading a barrow: put the **heavy thing near the wheel** and over it, not at the back handles — that is the difference between light and heavy to lift.
• Balance the load, do not heap above the rim, keep the top level so nothing falls on the path.
• Pushing: stand between the handles, back straight, knees soft, walk with small steps, look where the wheel is going, and on a slope keep the load **uphill** so it does not run away with you.
• A barrow is **not for carrying people**, and never for two children at once on a slope.
• Care: the tyre is pumped, the bolt tightened, the tray cleared of wet soil so it does not rust; a basket with a torn rim is **re-woven**, a split handle is bound with wire or new cane.
• Empty barrow upside down on a dry floor; baskets stacked in a shade, not in the sun which makes the cane brittle.

**Words for my notebook**

Barrow: one wheel, a tray and two handles — heavy near the wheel. Basket: woven carrier for the harvest, worn on head or back.

**Let us talk**

1. How many wheels does a wheelbarrow have?
2. Where do we put the heavy part of the load?
3. What is carried in a basket on our farm?
4. What do we do when a basket rim tears?


![A one-wheeled barrow with two handles and a deep tray stands beside an empty woven basket with two handles.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week6-the-wheelbarrow-and-the-carried-basket.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A wheelbarrow moves on ____ wheel(s). A) four B) two C) one D) six
2. The heavy part of a load in a barrow should be near the A) rim B) legs C) handles D) wheel
3. A basket on the farm is mostly used for A) spraying B) ploughing C) carrying the harvest D) cutting grass
4. The wide flat basket used to fan chaff from grain is the A) market basket B) water can C) egg cup D) winnowing basket
5. Fill in: A barrow must never be used for carrying ____.
6. Mention the parts of a wheelbarrow.
7. Write two sentences on loading and pushing a barrow safely.

**My own work**

Carry a light load across a short distance once in a container in your hand and once balanced on your head, then write which was easier to keep steady.

### WEEK 8 — IDENTIFYING AND DIFFERENTIATING FARM TOOLS

*Depth: no NERDC line is anchored here — the school's own row for this week is followed, using the Primary 4 tool weeks (p 833) as the vocabulary ceiling.*

**You will learn to**

• pick a tool from its shape alone
• say the name of the tool and its work in one breath
• tell two tools apart by the part that does the work
• sort the tools into the right groups

**Things to know**

• **Identifying** means knowing a thing by its marks; the marks of a tool are its **shape, its material, its working part and its work**.
• Look at the **working part** first, not the handle.  **Teeth** → rake · **flat blade at a right angle to a short handle** → hoe · **long handle with a broad flat blade** → spade · **curved scoop** → shovel · **long blade with a wooden grip** → cutlass.  Then: **wheel and tray** → barrow · **woven body** → basket · **can with a spout and a rose** → watering can.
• Sort by **work**: for digging — hoe, spade; for clearing — cutlass, rake; for carrying — barrow, basket, head pan; for watering — watering can, knapsack sprayer.
• Sort by **the part of the plant or soil it touches**: soil tools (hoe, spade, shovel), plant tools (cutlass, watering can), carrying tools (barrow, basket).
• Sort by **material**: metal and wood (hoe, cutlass, spade, shovel), plastic or metal sheet (watering can), woven plant fibre (basket), rubber and metal (barrow wheel).
• Sort by **the strength it needs**: back-and-leg tools (spade, shovel), arm tools (hoe, rake), hand tools (cutlass), no-force tools (barrow, basket) — the machine takes the weight.
• Names to keep away from confusion: a **spade** is not a **hoe** (the spade is pushed with the foot in line with the handle. The hoe is pulled at a right angle); a **shovel** is not a **spade** (the shovel moves, the spade digs); a **rake** is not a **hoe** (teeth, not blade).
• Our game: the bag holds five cut-out tool shapes; one child draws one and names it and its work before the class may say "right".
• A tool can also be known by **how it is held**: two hands (spade, shovel, hoe), one hand (cutlass), pushed not held (barrow), carried not held (basket on the head).
• If you cannot name a tool, describe it: what it is made of, the part that does the work, and where the farmer stands when he uses it.
• We end with the sound of each word said once, then the class calls the tool for its work: "What do I use to level the bed?"

**Words for my notebook**

Know a tool by its working part: teeth, blade, scoop, edge, wheel, weave, rose. Same job means same group.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part of a tool should you look at first?
2. Tell me a tool you would use for carrying and one for clearing.
3. How is a hoe held differently from a cutlass?
4. Name two tools that are easy to confuse, and the one difference.


![Four farm tools laid where the working part can be seen: a hoe blade set square, a rake with teeth, a flat spade and a curved scoop.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week8-the-working-part-of-each-tool.png)
**Worksheet**

1. We can identify a tool best by looking at its A) owner B) colour of rope C) working part D) price
2. A tool with teeth on a cross head is the A) rake B) hoe C) can D) spade
3. A long handle with a broad flat blade you push with the foot is the A) rose B) cutlass C) spade D) basket
4. Tools for carrying, in a group, are the A) rake and spade B) hoe and cutlass C) barrow and basket D) can and hoe
5. Fill in: A shovel differs from a spade because a shovel ____ things.
6. Sort these into digging, clearing and carrying: hoe, cutlass, basket, spade, barrow, rake.
7. Write two sentences on one tool you can now identify from its shape alone.

**My own work**

Cut or draw eight tools and mount them in three groups — digging, clearing, carrying — with the name under each.

---

### WEEK 9 — DIFFERENCES BETWEEN SIMILAR TOOLS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W3–W4 (p 833) — differences between similar tools and proper storage.*

**You will learn to**

• tell similar tools apart in one sentence
• say when each of the pair is the better choice
• name the difference in shape, in hold and in work
• say how both are stored

**Things to know**

• **Spade and shovel**: the spade's blade is **flat and stiff** with a sharp top edge so it can **cut into the ground**; the shovel's blade is **curved and hollow** so it can **hold a loose load**. Dig with a spade, move with a shovel.
• **Hoe and spade**: the hoe's blade is **at a right angle** to the handle and works by **pulling**; the spade's blade is **in line** with the handle and works by **pushing with the foot**.
• **Hoe and hand fork (cultivator)**: the hoe has a blade for slicing weeds and ridging; the fork has **three or four prongs** for loosening soil around a plant without cutting the roots.
• **Rake and hoe**: both are pulled, but the rake **gathers with teeth** and leaves the bed smooth; the hoe **cuts with a blade** and turns the soil over.
• **Cutlass and machete**: both are long blades; a cutlass is **shorter and heavier** for slashing bush, a machete is **longer and lighter** for clearing cane and grass in clean strokes.
• **Watering can and knapsack sprayer**: the can carries **water to the root**; the sprayer carries a **fine mist of medicine or liquid fertilizer to the leaf**; the sprayer's tank is pressurized by pumping and its lance has a nozzle.
• **Wheelbarrow and head pan (basket)**: the barrow **rolls** a big load over distance; the pan or basket **is carried** into rows too narrow for a wheel.
• **Bucket and basin**: the bucket is deep for carrying water; the basin is wide and shallow for soaking seeds or washing crop.
• **Sickle and cutlass**: the sickle is a **curved blade in one hand** for reaping grass and maize; the cutlass is a straight heavy blade for the bush.
• Even when the pair looks the same, ask: **which part moves the soil and which part carries it?** That answer nearly always picks the tool.
• **Storage of both of a pair**: the same rule — clean, dry, edge protected, hung or racked, kept out of children's reach and away from the rain; a tool stored wet is a tool that will be bought twice.
• Tools that are **sharpened together and stored apart** keep their edges; a cutlass lying on a spade dulls both.

**Words for my notebook**

Similar tools differ in one part: flat blade or hollow scoop, pulled or pushed, teeth or edge, carried or rolled.

**Let us talk**

1. Which one would you take to lift sand — spade or shovel? Why?
2. How does a hoe move the soil compared with a spade?
3. What is the difference between a watering can and a sprayer?
4. Why must tools not be stored wet?


![A spade standing in a clean-cut hole and a shovel lifting a scoop of loose soil.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/prevocational-studies/week9-the-spade-and-the-shovel-at-work.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The tool with a flat, stiff blade used to cut into the ground is the A) basket B) spade C) rake D) shovel
2. A knapsack sprayer differs from a watering can because it sprays a A) heap of manure B) load of sand C) fine mist of medicine D) stream of water
3. A hand fork loosens soil with its A) rose B) wheel C) prongs D) blade
4. The curved one-hand blade used for reaping grass is the A) spade B) sickle C) cutlass D) hoe
5. Fill in: A cutlass laid on a spade in the store ____ both edges.
6. Mention four pairs of tools that look alike and the one difference for each pair.
7. Write two sentences on which tool you would use to move a heap of soil and why.

**My own work**

Draw one pair of similar tools, write the difference between them in one line, and write how both should be stored.
