# Social and Citizenship Studies (SCS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing and Awards — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Social and Citizenship Studies · Second Term W3, W4, W7, W8, W9 and
W10 (pp 657–658) and Third Term W1–W4 (pp 659–661), with the Nursery 2 Social Habits week on the roles
of family members (pp 379–382) — the school moves this band down, so the Primary 1 content is the
minimum extent of depth and is told in Nursery 2 language.*


### WEEK 1 — MARRIAGE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W3 (pp 657–658).*

**You will learn to**

• say what marriage is
• name the kinds of marriage ceremony we have in Nigeria
• say that marriage brings a family
• name the duties of a husband and a wife

**Things to know**

• **Marriage** is the day a man and a woman are joined to live together as **husband and wife**, before their families and before the law.
• Marriage is the **beginning of a family**: the house, the children and the name all start from it.
• Three kinds of ceremony in Nigeria: **traditional** (family rites, price and blessing by the elders), **religious** (in a **church** or a **mosque**, with vows before God), and **court** (registry — signed papers before a marriage registrar and two witnesses).
• A marriage can have **one or more** of these at once: many couples do the traditional rite, then the church, and then sign at the registry.
• A **bride** is the woman on her wedding day; a **groom** is the man; the **wedding** is the ceremony; the **reception** is the party after it.
• The wedding **attire** carries the culture: **aso-oke, iro and buba, gele, agbada, kaftan, isiagu**; the colour and cloth belong to the family's people.
• In marriage the two **work together**: one does not rule, both decide; they plan the house, the food, the fees and the names.
• The two duties that hold a home: **love** (care shown in words and in work) and **respect** (speaking well of each other, and not before the children).
• A child born of married parents has a **name, a family, a language and a place in the community** — that is what the rite gives.
• Where there is a problem, the wise family **talks**, calls the elders they trust, and does not fight in front of the children.
• In our class we say it plainly: **every child deserves a home**, and a home is kept by two people who keep their promise.

**Words for my notebook**

Marriage joins a man and a woman as husband and wife, and it is how a family begins — by traditional rite, in church or mosque, or at the registry.

**Let us talk**

1. What is marriage?
2. Name the three kinds of ceremony we have in Nigeria.
3. Who is a groom?
4. What two duties hold a home together?

**Worksheet**

1. Marriage joins a man and a woman as A) neighbours B) classmates C) husband and wife D) traders
2. Which of these is a marriage ceremony done with signed papers? A) the court registry marriage B) the harvest rite C) the naming rite D) the market rite
3. The woman on her wedding day is called the A) sister B) bride C) aunt D) mother
4. Two duties of a husband and wife are love and A) quarrelling B) sleeping C) respect D) selling
5. Fill in: A wedding cloth worn by Yoruba families for the ceremony is called ____.
6. Mention the three kinds of marriage ceremony in Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on what marriage gives to a child.

**My own work**

Draw a wedding scene with the bride and groom in their cultural cloth, and write two lines on what makes a home stay strong.

---

### WEEK 2 — RESPONSIBLE PARENTING

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W4 (pp 657–658).*

**You will learn to**

• say what parenting means
• name what parents must give a child
• tell what makes a good parent
• say what the child owes back

**Things to know**

• **Parenting** is the work of raising a child — feeding, keeping, teaching and loving until the child can stand alone.
• A **parent** is a father, a mother, or the person who stands in their place; **guardian** is the grown-up who keeps a child that is not their own.
• Four responsibilities of parents: **food, clothing, education and love**; without any of the four the child is poor.
• Parents must also give **shelter and health**: a roof, a clean bed, immunization, treatment when there is sickness.
• A good parent is **caring** (notices when something is wrong), **patient** (teaches twice, ten times), **provides** (works so the fridge is not empty) and **teaches right from wrong**.
• A good parent **names the child**, gives the child a **language**, a **faith** or a conscience, and a **duty in the house**.
• Discipline that is responsible: rules that are said before, a correction that is not cruel, and punishment that fits the fault.
• Children's side: **obey**, **respect**, say thank you, help in the house, learn what is taught, and do not lie.
• **Appreciation** is not flattery: it is saying "thank you for the school bag" and doing the work the bag is for.
• A child who is fed but not spoken to has been half-parented; a child who is loved but never corrected is also in trouble.
• Where a child has no father or mother, the **grandmother, an aunt, an older brother or a foster home** does the parenting; the love is the same work.

**Words for my notebook**

Parenting is feeding, clothing, teaching and loving a child. The child answers with obedience, respect and hard work.

**Let us talk**

1. What is parenting?
2. Name the four things a parent must give.
3. What does a good parent do when the child repeats a fault?
4. What does the child give back to the home?

**Worksheet**

1. Parenting means A) raising and caring for children B) driving C) building houses D) selling food
2. Which is NOT a responsibility of parents? A) clothing the child B) feeding the child C) paying school fees D) doing the child's homework for him
3. A good parent teaches the child A) how to lie B) how to fight C) right from wrong D) how to hide
4. A grown-up who keeps a child that is not his own is a A) guardian B) friend C) neighbour D) stranger
5. Fill in: The four duties of parents are food, clothing, ____ and love.
6. Mention four qualities of a good parent.
7. Write two sentences on something you do that shows you appreciate what your home gives you.

**My own work**

Write four duties of a child in a family and tick every one you did well this week.

Draw two things that keep your home tidy and colour them.

---

### WEEK 3 — ROLES OF MEMBERS OF THE FAMILY

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W7 (pp 657–658), with NERDC Nursery 2 · Social Habits · First Term W2 (pp 379–382) for the same topic in our own band.*

**You will learn to**

• name the work of each person in my family
• say what a father and a mother do
• say what my own role is as a child
• tell the work of grandparents, uncles and aunts

**Things to know**

• A **family** is the group of people who live together and keep one another: father, mother and children, and often the grandparents.
• **Father's role**: he **provides** (work, money, food, school fees), **protects** the house, **guides and teaches** the children, and **plays with** them.
• A father also decides with the mother — the head of the house is a **team**, not one voice.
• **Mother's role**: she **cares for the children** (feeding, nursing, dressing), **cooks**, **keeps the home in order**, works outside too, and **teaches** manners and language.
• **The children's role**: **obey**, **respect** one another and the elders, **help with the chores of the house**, **study well**, keep the place tidy, do not break what is not yours.
• **Grandparents' role**: they give **advice and wisdom**, **tell the stories** that carry our history, watch the children when the parents work, and teach the culture and the prayers.
• **Extended family**: uncles, aunties and cousins are the **support** of a family — they lend, they keep, they attend the naming ceremony and the funeral.
• Simple chores for a child in Nursery 2: set and clear the plates, sweep one corner, fetch water, arrange the slippers, water a plant, fold my own cloth, keep my books in the bag.
• **Everybody working together makes the home light**: one person sweeping a compound is slow, three persons finish before dusk.
• **Appreciation** is naming what each one does: today the food is cooked, today the fees were paid, today the child washed his own plate — all three are work.
• A role is not a punishment; when the child shares the work of the house, he is being trusted, not used.

**Words for my notebook**

Father provides and protects; mother cares, cooks and teaches; children obey, help and study; grandparents give advice and stories.

**Let us talk**

1. What are two duties of a father?
2. Who cooks and keeps the home in order in your house?
3. Which chore will you take up at home this week?
4. What do grandparents give that school cannot?

**Worksheet**

1. The role of the father in the family includes providing and A) cooking for the market B) playing games all day C) protecting the family D) sweeping the road
2. Helping with chores and studying well is the role of A) the driver B) the child C) the visitor D) the neighbour
3. Grandparents give advice, stories and A) punishment B) school fees always C) homework D) wisdom
4. When everybody works together in a home the work becomes A) finished never B) light and fast C) heavier D) secret
5. Fill in: Uncles, aunties and cousins are the ____ family that supports us.
6. Mention two duties each of a father, a mother and a child.
7. Write two sentences on the chore you do in your house and who it helps.

**My own work**

List the members of your family and write one duty each of them carries; then write the one duty you will carry every day next week.

### WEEK 4 — CULTURE (PARTS 1 AND 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W8 (Culture Part 1) and W9 (Culture Part 2), pp 657–658 — the school keeps both parts in one week.*

**You will learn to**

• say what culture is
• name the elements of culture
• tell the dress, food and greeting of our people
• say how our culture is kept

**Things to know**

• **Culture** is **the way of life of a people**: what they speak, eat, wear, greet, marry, bury, sing and celebrate.
• Culture is learnt, not born with: we catch it from the home, the street and the elders.
• The **elements of culture** to name: **language, food, dressing, festivals, greetings, music and dance, religion, names, marriage and burial rites**.
• Nigeria is **rich and diverse**: three big groups — **Hausa, Igbo, Yoruba** — and over two hundred and fifty other peoples, each with a culture.
• **Traditional dressing**: the **wrapper** and **buba** (loose shirt), the **iro** (wrapper tied round the waist) with **gele** (the head tie), the **kaftan** and the **agbada** (the big flowing robe), the **fila** cap, the **hula/beehive** cap of the north.
• **Nigerian foods** to name: **jollof rice, pounded yam, egusi soup, tuwo, akara, moi-moi, eba, amala, pepper soup, kilishi, suya, kosai**.
• **Greetings** carry respect: the Yoruba boy **prostrates (doba)**, the Yoruba girl **kneels (doble/kunrin)**; the Hausa man puts his hands together and lowers them. The Igbo elder is greeted with "ndewo/nno" and a seat is offered; in the west a handshake with the right hand is common.
• A greeting said well is a culture kept; a child who forgets to greet has begun to forget his people.
• **Festivals** in our community: **Eid-el-Fitr and Eid-el-Kabir** (the Muslim celebrations after the fast and at the rams), **Christmas and Easter**, the **new yam festival (Iwa Ji/Emumu)**, **Egungun, Osun-Osogbo, Durbar of Katsina, Argungu fishing festival, Eyo festival of Lagos**.
• At a festival there is **dance, drum, masquerade, new cloth, special food, horsing, and the blessing of the elders** — that is culture in one place.
• **Preserving culture** means speaking our own language at home, wearing our cloth on our days, learning the folk tales, eating the food of our people, greeting the elders, and writing the names right.
• Culture is not everything old: it is what our people **do with what they have learnt**, so a Nigerian child who codes and greets is not losing his culture.

**Words for my notebook**

Culture is the way of life of a people — language, food, dress, greeting, festival and music. Nigeria has many cultures and we keep ours.

**Let us talk**

1. What is culture?
2. Name the food of our home and the cloth of our father's people.
3. How does a Yoruba girl greet an elder?
4. Name two festivals in Nigeria and what happens at one.

**Worksheet**

1. Culture is best described as A) a market name B) a school subject C) the way of life of a people D) a church rite
2. The cloth wrapped and tied on a Yoruba woman's head is the A) hula B) buba C) fila D) gele
3. Pounded yam, egusi soup and jollof rice belong to Nigerian A) names B) food C) dressing D) music
4. The Yoruba boy's way of greeting an elder is to A) shout B) clap only C) prostrate D) run
5. Fill in: The three big ethnic groups of Nigeria are Hausa, Igbo and ____.
6. Mention five elements of culture.
7. Write two sentences on a festival in our community and one thing done there.

**My own work**

Write or draw four things your family keeps from its culture: the language you speak at home, a food, a cloth and a greeting.

### WEEK 5 — INTRODUCTION TO NIGERIA

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W10 (pp 657–658).*

**You will learn to**

• say that Nigeria is our country and where it is
• name the states and the capital
• name our languages and religions
• say our national motto and know my own state

**Things to know**

• **Nigeria is our country**; it is in **West Africa**, and it faces the sea in the south — the **Atlantic (Bight of Benin)**.
• Nigeria's neighbours: **Benin Republic** on the west, **Niger** on the north, **Chad** on the north-east, **Cameroon** on the east.
• Nigeria has **36 states** and one **Federal Capital Territory (FCT)** — the FCT holds **Abuja**, the **capital city**.
• Before Abuja, the capital was **Lagos**; the seat of the government moved to Abuja in **1991**.
• The largest city and the money town is still **Lagos**; the three big cities are Lagos, Kano and Ibadan.
• Our **official language is English**; it is the language of the school, the court and the paper. Our **national languages** are **Hausa, Igbo and Yoruba**, and there are more than **250 other languages**.
• The major religions are **Christianity, Islam and Traditional religion**; the north is mostly Muslim, the south mostly Christian, and the middle belt holds both and the old faith.
• Nigeria's national motto is **"Unity and Faith, Peace and Progress"** — it is written on the coat of arms.
• The **flag** is **green–white–green**: green for the farms and the land, white for peace.
• Our national anthem is *Arise, O Compatriots*; the pledge is said in school on the flag day.
• **Our own state**: name it, name its capital, and name the **local government** we live in; a citizen knows at least three circles — home town, state, country.
• Nigeria is **the most populous country in Africa** (over 200 million people) and one of the biggest producers of **crude oil** and of **farm produce** — cocoa, groundnut, palm oil, yam and rubber.

**Words for my notebook**

Nigeria is in West Africa: 36 states and the FCT with Abuja as capital. English is our official language; our motto is "Unity and Faith, Peace and Progress".

**Let us talk**

1. Where is Nigeria?
2. How many states does Nigeria have, and what is our capital?
3. Which three languages are called our national languages?
4. Say the motto of Nigeria. What colour is in the middle of our flag?

**Worksheet**

1. Nigeria is found in the ____ part of Africa. A) northern only B) southern C) western D) eastern
2. The capital city of Nigeria is A) Ibadan B) Kano C) Lagos D) Abuja
3. Nigeria has ____ states and one Federal Capital Territory. A) 50 B) 21 C) 30 D) 36
4. The official language of Nigeria is A) English B) Yoruba C) Hausa only D) Arabic
5. Fill in: The colours of the Nigerian flag are green, ____ and green.
6. Mention the three national languages of Nigeria and our national motto.
7. Write two sentences on your own state, its capital and your local government.

**My own work**

Write the name of your state, its capital, your local government and one thing the state is known for.

---

### WEEK 6 — ATTITUDES AND VALUES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Third Term W1 (pp 659–661).*

**You will learn to**

• say what an attitude is and what a value is
• tell a good attitude from a bad one
• name the core values of Nigeria
• show honesty and hard work in class

**Things to know**

• An **attitude** is how I **think and feel** about a thing, and how that shows in what I do.
• A **value** is what I hold to be **important and right** — the thing I will not sell.
• Attitude is the way I come; value is what I stand on.
• **Good attitudes**: **positive thinking** (I look for what can be done), **cheerfulness** (I come with a bright face), **willingness to help**, **punctuality**, **patience**, and **the readiness to try again**.
• **Bad attitudes**: **complaining** (the mouth is always finding fault), **laziness** (the body will not start), **selfishness** (all for me), **rudeness**, **cheating**, **giving up**.
• An attitude can be **changed** on purpose: the child who says "I cannot" begins to say "I cannot yet", and next month he can.
• The **core values of Nigeria** to name: **honesty, hard work, respect, integrity, patriotism, discipline, tolerance, commitment to excellence, and the spirit of service**.
• **Integrity** is doing right **when nobody is looking**; honesty is telling the truth **when it costs me**.
• **Patriotism** is loving Nigeria: keeping her things clean, respecting her flag, learning her story, and refusing to spoil what belongs to all of us.
• **Tolerance** is letting a classmate pray, eat and greet in his own way while I keep mine.
• Values are taught by **being seen**: the teacher who returns the extra change, the pupil who hands back a found bag, the class that plays without cheating.
• Our class keeps a **value of the week**: one word on the board, one example each day, and Friday is when we say who showed it.

**Words for my notebook**

Attitude is how I think and act; value is what I hold dear. Honesty, hard work, respect, integrity and patriotism are our core values.

**Let us talk**

1. What is an attitude?
2. What is a value?
3. Which bad attitude do you want to change in yourself?
4. What does integrity look like when you find money on the floor?

**Worksheet**

1. An attitude is best described as A) how we think and feel about things B) a school rule C) a thing we buy D) a market price
2. Which of these is a good attitude? A) cheerfulness B) complaining C) selfishness D) laziness
3. Doing the right thing when nobody is looking is A) integrity B) luck C) fear D) speed
4. Loving your country and keeping her things clean is called A) tolerance B) patriotism C) play D) honesty
5. Fill in: The value that means telling the truth when it costs you is ____.
6. Mention five core values of Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on one good attitude you showed this week.

**My own work**

Choose one core value, write it on a card, and keep it on your desk for a week; on Friday write one thing you did that matched it.

Draw one kind thing you did this week and colour it.

### WEEK 8 — FINDING HELP IN DANGER (PARTS 1 AND 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Third Term W2 (Part 1) and W3 (Part 2), pp 659–661 — the school keeps both in one week.*

**You will learn to**

• say what danger is and name its kinds
• tell who can help me and when to run to them
• keep calm, speak clearly and give my name and place
• keep safe from strangers

**Things to know**

• **Danger** is any situation that can **harm** us — the body, the eyes, the breath or the life.
• Kinds of danger a child meets: **fire, road accidents, getting lost, strangers, bad weather (storm, flood, lightning), deep water, electric wire, sharp objects, poison, a mad dog**.
• An **emergency** is a danger that is **happening now** and needs help at once: a burn, a fall with blood, somebody not breathing, a house on fire.
• People who help: the **grown-up nearest**, the **teacher**, a **parent or guardian**, the **police**, the **fire service**, the **doctor or nurse**, the **neighbour** we know, and the **community watch**.
• **Staying calm** is the first rescue: a child who screams cannot be heard; a child who breathes and speaks can be helped.
• Numbers to know (as our scheme sets them, and as they stand today): **112 — the national emergency number, free from any phone, for police, fire or ambulance anywhere in Nigeria**. **767 — Lagos State emergency line (police, fire and ambulance in Lagos)**. **199 — the police emergency line in some states**; **122 — FRSC for road accidents**; **190 — the Federal Fire Service**. The safest thing a child does is dial **112** and say what happened.
• What to say in an emergency call, in this order: **my name → where I am → what happened → how many people are hurt → the phone number I am calling from**. Then **stay on the line** until told to stop.
• **Trusted adults**: the ones at home, the ones in the school, the ones with a name tag at the bank, the shopkeeper we buy from every day, the **police officer in uniform**. If I am lost, I go to a **shop, a filling station or a police post** — never to a car.
• **Stranger rules**: **do not talk to a stranger, do not follow a stranger, do not accept gifts, sweets or rides, do not go into a stranger's house or car. And do not let anybody touch the parts of the body that the swimsuit covers** — say "NO", run, and tell at once.
• **Secrets**: a bad grown-up may say "do not tell anybody"; we tell that kind of secret the same hour. Telling is not tattling.
• Know by heart: **my full name, my home address, my father's and mother's phone numbers, my class and school** — a child who can say these is half-found already.
• **Body danger drill**: fire — get down low, do not run through smoke, use the stairs, never a lift, do not go back for a bag. Road — stop, look both ways, listen, cross at the corner or the bridge; water — never swim alone.
• In a **storm or flood** we move to high ground and stay away from poles, wires and trees; lightning comes first to the tall thing.

**Words for my notebook**

Danger is anything that can harm me. Keep calm, say your name and place, and dial 112. Say no to strangers and tell at once.

**Let us talk**

1. What is danger? Name four kinds near our home.
2. Which number would you dial in an emergency anywhere in Nigeria?
3. What do you say first when you call for help?
4. A stranger offers you a ride and a sweet: what do you do?

**Worksheet**

1. Danger is a situation that can A) teach us B) amuse us C) harm us D) feed us
2. The national emergency number in Nigeria, free from any phone, is A) 411 B) 112 C) 100 D) 911
3. The first thing to do when help is needed is to A) hide the matter B) go and play C) keep calm and speak clearly D) scream and run
4. A stranger gives you a gift and asks you to come with him. You should A) go quietly B) say no, run and tell a trusted adult C) take the gift and look back D) thank him and wait
5. Fill in: If I am lost, I go to a shop or a police post — never to a ____.
6. Mention the information to give when you call for help, in order.
7. Write two sentences on what you will do if you see a small fire in the house.

**My own work**

Write from memory your full name, your class, your home street and two phone numbers, and say them aloud once at home tonight until you can say them without looking.

Draw a telephone with the three digits you may dial in danger, and colour the phone.

### WEEK 9 — SECURE ENVIRONMENT

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Third Term W4 (Secure Environment Part 1, pp 659–661).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a secure environment is
• name the things that make a place safe
• find the dangers around my home and school
• report a danger I see

**Things to know**

• A **secure environment** is a place where people are **safe from harm** — body, property and feelings all protected.
• Things that make a place secure: **a fence and a gate, locks on doors, lights that work at night, a watchman or security guard, a dog that is known, windows with bars, and neighbours who know one another.**
• **Feeling safe** matters as much as being safe: at home, at school and in the community a child should not be afraid of the corner, the man or the noise.
• Dangers in our own environment to name: **an open well or pit, a busy road with no bridge, a broken wire on a pole, sharp cutlass or broken glass left on the floor. An uncovered drain, a heap of refuse that breeds mosquitoes, water in a bucket a small child can fall into, a loose wall, a mad dog, a dark passage.**.
• **Rules keep us safe**: close the gate behind you, do not open to a stranger, do not climb the fence. Keep off the road to play, do not touch the wire, do not light a fire in the house, do not go into the water alone.
• **Staying alert** means noticing: the smell of kerosene, the sound of a stranger on the roof, the boy hanging on the gate, the water rising in the street.
• **Reporting a danger** is a duty, not tattling: "the well has no cover", "the glass is on the corridor", "the wire hangs low" — a child who reports prevents an accident.
• At school the safe signs we learn: **no entry**, **mind the step**, **caution wet floor**, **fire exit**, **keep gate closed** — a sign is a rule with a picture.
• Home safety habits: the key is kept in **one place** (not in the lock), the gas is off at night. The matches are high up, the knife is put away, the child does not sleep in a room with a paraffin stove burning.
• A secure place is also clean and lit: **a bright street is a safe street**; a compound with no refuse has no rats and no mosquitoes.
• Our class ends with a **walk of safety**: each child points at one danger on the school ground and one thing that makes the school secure.

**Words for my notebook**

A secure environment is a safe place — fence, lock, light, guard and rules. Report a danger you see; that is good citizenship.

**Let us talk**

1. What makes our school a secure place?
2. Name two dangers around our homes.
3. What do you do when you see broken glass on the floor?
4. Why does a light street keep us safe?

**Worksheet**

1. A secure environment is a place where people are A) quiet only B) many C) safe from harm D) rich
2. Which of these makes a compound secure? A) an open gate B) a working light at night C) a low wire D) a heap of refuse
3. An open well in the yard is dangerous because A) it holds water B) it is old C) it is ugly D) a child may fall into it
4. Telling the person in charge about a danger you saw is A) play B) laziness C) cowardly D) reporting, and it prevents an accident
5. Fill in: The sign "caution — wet floor" is a rule with a ____.
6. Mention four things that make a place secure.
7. Write two sentences on one danger in our school and how we can make it safe.

**My own work**

Walk once round your own house, write down two dangers you saw and two things that keep the house secure, and say to whom you will report the dangers.

Draw your own gate or door and colour the place where it locks.

# Yoruba

Weeks 1 to 6, 8, 9 and 11. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term Break) or Week 10 (Revision);
Week 12 is Closing and Week 13 carries the Examination for this subject.
**Week 11 carries a note** because the school's own table prints it as a teaching row
("Exam Preparation"), not as an examination week.
*Depth: this subject is school-generated — there is no NERDC Yoruba scheme to floor it against. The
extent of each week is the three strands the school itself prints (Ẹ̀dẹ̀ · Àṣà · Lítíréṣọ̀). The master
corpus prints Yoruba without tone marks; tone marks are added in the body of the note so that the
child reads the word correctly, and the topic line is left exactly as the school prints it.*


### WEEK 1 — Ede: Didaruko nnkan ayika ile-iwe / Asa: Eran osin ni ile Yoruba / Litireso: Orin akoniniwa

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 S2 W1.*

**You will learn to**

• name the things around the school in Yoruba
• name the animals a Yoruba family keeps and what they are for
• sing the song of the child who knows his mother's face

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (naming things around the school — dídárúkọ̀ nǹkan ayíká ilé-ìwé)**

• Last term we named things **in** the room; this term we go **round the school**.
• **òde ilé-ìwé** — the school compound · **pápá ìdaráya** — the playground · **_ilé ìwẹ̀** (tóílẹ̀tì) — the toilet
• **ọ̀ṣọ́** — the guard's post · **ọ̀fìn** (kísíìnì) — the kitchen · **ọ̀gbìn** — the garden · **igí** — tree
• **ògiri** — the fence · **ọ̀nà** — the road · **pátákó ìkẹ́ọ̀** — the teaching board · **ìwé** — book
• Say the pattern **"Ní ilé-ìwé wa, a ní ______."** (In our school we have ______.)
• Walking game: we move round the school, and at each place I say one word: *"Èyí ni ọ̀gbìn."*
• Ask and answer: **"Kí ni èyí?"** — **"Èyí ni ..."** · **"Níbo ni ... wà?"** — **"Ó wà ní ..."**
• Spelling of the new words goes in the notebook with one drawing beside each.

**Things to know — Àṣà (the animals of Yorubaland — ẹranko ìsìn ní ilẹ̀ Yorùbá)**

• The school's row prints **eran osin**: **ẹranko ìsìn** — the animals a Yoruba family keeps for the rite, the feast and the honouring of a guest.
• **ewúrẹ́** — goat · **àdìẹ** — fowl · **ọ̀wọ́ọ̀n** — ram · **àgbo** — sheep · **màlúù** — cow · **àyẹ̀yẹ́** — pigeon · **ẹranko ilé** — the animals a house keeps.
• The goat and the fowl are the two animals a Yoruba home can keep in a small yard; they eat scraps and they find their own grain.
• When a child is born, the family **names** him on the eighth day; when a guest of honour comes, the house **kills a fowl** — that is how the animal enters the rite.
• Every animal has its own sound: ewúrẹ́ says **"mèé"**, àdìẹ says **"kòkòrò"** (cock) / **"gèé"** (hen), màlúù says **"moooo"**, ajá says **"grrr — ọ̀ọ̀!"**.
• We are kind to the animals of the house: water before sun, food at the set time, no tying of legs too tight, no beating.
• Some animals are **not** for eating or keeping: ẹ̀kùn (leopard), ẹ̀jọ́ (python), àlùkò (owl) — in Yoruba tradition they belong to the bush and to ìṣẹ́̀gun, and the child keeps away.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (the song the school names: Orin akomoniwa)**

• The row prints the song by its first words, as the school writes it elsewhere: **"Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú"**.
• *Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú, á mọ́ bàbá rẹ̀ lójú;* — a child who knows his mother's face will know his father's face too;
• *Ọmọ tó ṣe ìbéèrè, kò sẹ́nu mọ́lẹ̀.* — a child who asks does not end with his mouth shut.
• Meaning: a child who knows where he comes from knows where he is going; we greet because we were taught.
• Sing it twice: once slowly with the hands flat on the chest, once with a step to each line.
• Answer the line: the class calls **"Ọmọ tó mọ̀!"** and I answer **"Ó mọ́ ilé rẹ̀."**

**Words for my notebook**

Òde ilé-ìwé · pápá ìdaráya · ọ̀gbìn · ogiri. Ewúrẹ́ · àdìẹ · ọ̀wọ́ọ̀n · màlúù — ẹranko tí ilé ń tọ́. Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú.

**Let us talk**

1. Name five things you saw outside the class this morning, in Yoruba.
2. Which two animals can a small yard keep?
3. When does a family kill the fowl?
4. Say the first line of the song and what it means.

**Worksheet**

1. "Pápá ìdaráya" is the A) playground B) garden C) kitchen D) fence
2. The Yoruba for goat is A) agbo B) ewúrẹ́ C) àdìẹ D) màlúù
3. The animal that gives us eggs in the morning is the A) sheep B) goat C) cow D) fowl
4. "Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú" teaches that a child should A) hide B) run far C) sleep late D) know his own family
5. Fill in: "Ní ilé-ìwé wa, a ní ____." — the missing word is any thing in our school.
6. Name four things around the school in Yoruba and four animals a home keeps.
7. Sing or say two lines of the song and say what the song teaches.

**My own work**

Walk once round the school, write six Yoruba names of things you saw, and under two of them draw the thing.

---

### WEEK 2 — Ede: Kika alifabeeti H-Q / Asa: Ounje ile wa / Litireso: Ewi kika (ibowo fagba)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W2.*

**You will learn to**

• read the letters h to q again, better than last term
• name the food of our home in Yoruba
• say a poem on giving honour to an elder

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (reading the letters H–Q — kíkà alífábéètì)**

• We met these letters in Week 9 of last term; this week we read them **backwards, forwards and out of order**.
• The line: **h · i · j · k · l · m · n · ń · o · ọ · p** — the row says H–Q, and in the Yoruba alphabet the letters in that range are h, i, j, k, l, m, n, o, p, for **there is no c, q, v, x or z** in Yoruba writing.
• Sound each letter with the seven vowels: **ha · hẹ · he · hi · ho · họ · hu**, then the same for j, k, l, m, n — say one line fast and one line slow.
• Read the words made from these letters: **ilé** (house) · **ìdí** (bottom/reason) · **ọ̀nà** (road) · **omi** (water) · **inú** (inside) · **ọkọ̀** (vehicle) · **òdo** (river) · **ọ̀pọ̀** (many).
• The tricky pair: **o** (oral) and **ọ** (closed) — **òdo** (river) is not **ọ̀dọ́** (young); say both and hear the mouth change.
• Reading rule: eyes on the line, mouth open, one breath per word, no pointing with the finger at the end of the week.

**Things to know — Àṣà (the food of our home — oúnjẹ ilé wa)**

• The food of the Yoruba home is pounded and wrapped: **ìyan** (pounded yam) · **àmàlà** (yam-flour) · **èbà** (garri) · **ẹ̀̀kọ́** (cassava mash, èkọ́) · **túwò** (grain swallow).
• Soups that go with them: **ẹ̀fẹ́** (leaf soup) · **ègúsí** (melon seed) · **ẹ̀wẹ̀dú** (beans pudding soup) · **ògọ́rò** · **árasá** (for the little one).
• Small foods and drinks of the home: **àkàrà** (bean cake) · **àrà** (roasted plantain) · **ìrẹ̀** (corn pap) · **ògì** (pap with groundnut) · **wàìn ọ̀pẹ̀** (palm wine, for the elders only).
• How the food is paired: **ìyan** goes with **ẹ̀wẹ̀dú** or **ègúsí**; **àmàlà** goes with **ógọ́rọ́** or **ẹ̀wẹ̀dú**; **èbà** goes with **ẹ̀fẹ́** or **ègúsí**; **ìrẹ̀** is for breakfast, not for the feast.
• Foods of the farm we name in the same breath: **iṣu** (yam) · **ọ̀̀̀sà** (cassava) · **ìbà** (cocoyam) · **àgbàdọ̀** (maize) · **ìrẹsì** (rice) · **àrà** (plantain).
• In the Yoruba home we wash the hands first, we sit on a mat or stool, and we eat with the **right hand**, three fingers making the spoon.
• Before a big meal the family says **"Ẹ̀ jẹun, ọmọ mi"**; the child answers **"Ọ́ ńlẹ̀, ìyá/bàbá."**
• Our own food is not lesser food: the child who knows the name of his food knows the name of his people.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (saying a poem on honour for elders — èwì kíkà: ìbọ̀wọ̀ fún àgbà)**

• A **ẹ̀wì** is chanted, not read; it goes with the head and the hands.
• *Àgbà kìí rí nǹkan ṣe,* — an elder never sees a thing done wrongly
• *kó jẹ́ ḱ ọ̀dọ́ ṣe é;* — and let the young one do it;
• *Ọ̀dọ́ kìí rí nǹkan ṣe,* — a young one never sees a thing done wrongly
• *kó jẹ́ ḱ àgbà réra lẹ́yìn.* — and the elder be left behind.
• Meaning: the old and the young work together; honour is shared, not only given.
• Ways of honour in Yoruba: **ìdòbì** (the boy prostrates), **ìkúnlẹ̀** (the girl kneels), the two hands cupped low when we pass in front, and standing when the elder sits down.
• We say the poem three times: whisper, then normal, then loud with the action.

**Words for my notebook**

h i j k l m n ń o ọ p · ìyan, àmàlà, èbà, ẹ̀fẹ́, ègúsí · Àgbà kìí rí nǹkan ṣe, kó jẹ́ ḱ ọ̀dọ́ ṣe é.

**Let us talk**

1. Read from h to p without pointing.
2. Which is the Yoruba word for pounded yam?
3. What does a girl do to greet an elder?
4. Say the poem and give its meaning.

**Worksheet**

1. Which letter is NOT used in writing Yoruba? A) q B) j C) gb D) ṣ
2. "Ìyan" means A) pounded yam B) rice C) palm oil D) bean cake
3. A Yoruba girl greets an elder by A) shouting B) running C) kneeling D) prostrating
4. "Àmàlà" is made from A) plantain B) yam flour C) cassava D) maize
5. Fill in: The Yoruba for water is ____.
6. Name five foods of our home and the soup that goes with two of them.
7. Say the poem on honour and write what it teaches in one sentence.

**My own work**

Write the names of the foods eaten in your house this week, and say the poem to somebody at home before you eat.

Draw three foods your family eats and colour them.

---

### WEEK 3 — Ede: Onka 11-20 / Asa: Ere idaraya / Litireso: Alo pipa (apamo / apagbe)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W3.*

**You will learn to**

• count from eleven to twenty in Yoruba
• name the games we play and the words we say while playing
• begin the telling of àlọ́, with its call and its answer

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (counting 11–20 — ìkà 11–20)**

• From ten (**ẹ́wọ́/ogún** is twenty) we go up with **-lá**, which means "above ten":
• 11 **ọ̀kànlá** · 12 **èjìlá** · 13 **ẹ̀tálá** · 14 **ẹ̀rìnlá** · 15 **àárúnlá**
• 16 **ẹ̀fálá** · 17 **ẹ̀jẹ́lá** · 18 **ẹ̀jọ́lá** · 19 **ẹ̀sánlá** · 20 **ogún**
• The other way of counting, "what is taken from twenty": 19 **òdínlógún** · 18 **èjìlógún** · 17 **ẹ̀tálógún** · 16 **ẹ̀rìnlógún** · 15 **àárún lógún**
• 14 **ẹ̀fálógún** · 13 **ẹ̀jélógún** · 12 **ẹ̀jọ́lógún** · 11 **ẹ̀sánlógún** — the two roads meet at the same number.
• Clap and count: 11 claps for ọ̀kànlá up to twenty claps and a stamp for **ogún**.
• Ask and answer: **"Ogún ló nǐ?"** — **"Bẹ́ẹ̀ni, ogún ni."** (Do you have twenty? — Yes, I have twenty.)
• Use the numbers on things: 12 chalks, 15 bottle tops, 20 steps from the gate to the door.

**Things to know — Àṣà (games — eré ìdárayá)**

• **Eré ìdárayá** means games that make the body happy and strong.
• Games with words in Yoruba: **"Kìké"** (hopscotch) · **"Aro"** (the clapping game, "aro, aro ko lẹ́sẹ̀") · **"Ṣégbé-ṣégbé"** (a counting-out rhyme) · **"Tẹ̀gbẹ̀tẹ̀"** (hide and seek) · **"Igí àti eré"**.
• Games with a ball: **bọ́ọ̀lù** — pass, catch, throw at a mark; games with a rope: **fífo ọ̀pá** (skipping the rope) and **dídè** (jumping over a stretched band).
• We say before a game: **"Ẹ̀ jẹ́ ká bẹ̀rẹ̀!"** and after: **"A ṣe é, ẹ̀ ṣé."**
• Rule of the Yoruba game: the one who is caught becomes "it" without crying; the winner carries nobody's bag.
• The body of the player: knees bend, hands free, head up, the shout is for cheering and not for insulting.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (the telling of àlọ́ — àlọ́ pípá: apamo àti apagbe)**

• An **àlọ́** is a story made for children, and it is **told** (àlọ́ **pípá**), not read.
• The teller opens with a call; the row prints two of them: **"Àlọ́ àpámọ̀"** and **"Àlọ́ àpágbé"**. The class answers with the same breath.
• Call: **"Àlọ́!"** — Answer: **"Àlọ́ ni!"** · Call: **"Àpámọ̀!"** — Answer: **"Ọmọ ẹyẹ́ ló n fò."**
• A àlọ́ has three parts: **ìbẹ̀rẹ̀** (the opening), **àárín** (what happens), **ìparí** (how it ends and what it teaches).
• A short one to tell this week: the **Ìjàpá** (tortoise) who wanted all the food at the feast of the birds, borrowed feathers, flew up, and fell when the feathers were taken back.
• Its lesson: **gbèsè** (greed) spoils a person; the one who takes everything ends on the ground.
• After every àlọ́ we say the closing: **"Òlúkòòlú ni àlọ́ mi, kí n ó r'ọkọ̀ nínú rẹ̀."** (I have told it crookedly — may the carriage be inside it.)

**Words for my notebook**

ọ̀kànlá · èjìlá · ẹ̀tálá · ẹ̀rìnlá · àárúnlá · ẹ̀fálá · ẹ̀jẹ́lá · ẹ̀jọ́lá · ẹ̀sánlá · ogún. Àlọ́ àpámọ̀! Àlọ́ ni!

**Let us talk**

1. Count from ọ̀kànlá to ogún.
2. Which game did you play yesterday, and what did you say while playing it?
3. How do we answer when the teller says "Àlọ́"?
4. What did the tortoise learn at the end of his story?

**Worksheet**

1. The Yoruba for fifteen is A) ogún B) ẹ̀fálá C) àárúnlá D) àárún
2. Ogún means A) twenty B) fifty C) twelve D) ten
3. A story told for children, with a lesson in it, is called A) oríkì B) ẹ̀wì C) àlọ́ D) orin
4. Before a game we say A) "káàrọ̀" B) "a ṣe é" C) "ọ̀ dàbọ̀" D) "ẹ̀ jẹ́ ká bẹ̀rẹ̀"
5. Fill in: 11 in Yoruba is ____ (the "one-above-ten" way).
6. Count from 11 to 20 in Yoruba, then backwards.
7. Tell a short àlọ́ in three sentences and say its lesson.

**My own work**

Count twenty steps from your door to the gate, saying each number in Yoruba, and write the number at which you turned.

Draw the game you play at break time and colour the ball or the rope.

---

### WEEK 4 — Ede: Alifabeeti P-Y / Asa: Iwa omoluabi / Litireso: Orin kiko (omo rere)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W4.*

**You will learn to**

• read and name the letters p to y
• say what an ọmọlúàbí is
• sing the song of the good child with the right actions

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (the letters P–Y — alífábéètì p sí y)**

• The last of the letters: **p · r · s · ṣ · t · ì · w · y**.
• Say each with the vowels: **pa pe pọ pi po pọ · ra re rọ ri ro rọ · sa sọ ṣa ṣe ṣọ · ta te tọ ti to tọ · wa wọ wi wo wọ · ya yọ ye yi yo yọ**.
• The letter **ṣ** is the one English has not: the tongue is flat and quiet — **ṣọ** (guard) is not **sọ** (fall).
• Letters we do **not** use: c, q, v, x, z; the digraphs we **do**: **gb** and **j**, and the letters with dots: **ẹ, ọ, ṣ** and **ń**.
• Read these words now: **ọkọ̀** (car) · **ọwọ́** (hand) · **ìyàwó** (bride/younger wife) · **ṣùúrú** (a food) · **tutù** (cold) · **ìsàlẹ̀** (below) · **yíyá** (running).
• Write the whole alphabet from a to y in one line, then the seven vowels under it: **a e ẹ i o ọ u**.
• End of the week test: read any line on the chart from p to y without stopping at the dots.

**Things to know — Àṣà (the child of good character — ìwà ọmọlúàbí)**

• **Ọmọlúàbí** is a child brought up well: **ọmọ + ilé + àgbà** — the child of the elder's house, the one people point at and say "that one is well brought up".
• Marks of an ọmọlúàbí: he **greets**, he **does not lie**, he **does not steal**, he **shares**, he **respects the elder and the younger**, he **keeps his word**, and he **works before he plays**.
• The Yoruba say: **"Ọmọlúàbí kìí ṣe nǹkan tí kò yẹ, bí ẹnikẹ́ni kò bá rí i."** — a well-brought-up child does not do a wrong thing even when nobody is watching.
• **"Ìwà lẹ́wàá"** — character is the real beauty; a fine face with a bad temper is an ugly face.
• **"Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́, àgbàlagbà á kà á."** — the child who is not taught, grown-up life will correct, and correction from life is harder.
• A ọmọlúàbí in our class: raises the hand, does not laugh at a wrong answer, picks the paper that is not his, returns the pencil.
• We name one ọmọlúàbí each morning — and it is not always the same child.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (a song to sing — orin kíkọ́: ọmọ rere)**

• A song with an action is learnt faster than a song with only the mouth.
• *Ọmọ rere, ọmọ ilé wa,* — a good child, the child of our home;
• *ó ń kúnlẹ̀, ó ń dọbì;* — he kneels, he prostrates;
• *ó ń jẹun, kò ń jà,* — he eats and does not quarrel;
• *ọmọ rere nìyẹn o!* — that one is a good child!
• Actions: kneel on line two, touch the floor on line two, rub the tummy on line three, thumbs up on line four.
• Sing it in a circle: one child stands in the middle and is pointed at on the last line; next time it is another child — everybody is a good child in one round.

**Words for my notebook**

p r s ṣ t ì w y · Ẹ, Ọ, Ṣ, Ń àti gb, j. Ọmọlúàbí = ọmọ tí a kọ́ ní ilé àgbà. Ìwà lẹ́wàá.

**Let us talk**

1. Read the letters from p to y.
2. Which sounds has English not got?
3. Name five marks of an ọmọlúàbí.
4. Sing the song and show me the action on the second line.

**Worksheet**

1. Which of these letters is NOT used in writing Yoruba? A) ṣ B) v C) w D) ń
2. "Ọmọlúàbí" means a child who is A) rich B) tall C) the oldest D) well brought up
3. "Ìwà lẹ́wàá" teaches that A) money is beauty B) speed is beauty C) clothes are beauty D) character is beauty
4. The Yoruba word for "hand" is A) ẹsẹ̀ B) etí C) ojú D) ọwọ́
5. Fill in: The seven vowels of Yoruba are a, e, ẹ, i, o, ọ and ____.
6. Write the letters from p to y in order.
7. Say two lines of the song of the good child and what each line shows.

**My own work**

Write four things an ọmọlúàbí does at home, and practise the song with its actions until you can sing it twice without stopping.

Draw one polite thing you did this week and colour it.

---

### WEEK 5 — Ede: Alifabeeti A-Y (leta nla ati kekere) / Asa: Ojuse ebi / idile / Litireso: Orin kiko lori obi

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W5.*

**You will learn to**

• match the big letter and the small letter, all the way from A to Y
• say the duties of the family in order
• sing a song on the parent and clap the beat

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (capital and small letters, A–Y — lẹ́tà nlá àti kékeré)**

• The **big letter (lẹ́tà nlá)** is used at the beginning of a name and at the start of a sentence: **Ọ̀lú, Àṣẹ̀, Mo, Bí**.
• The **small letter** is for the rest of the word: **ọ̀lú, àṣẹ̀, mo, bí**.
• Say the pairs aloud in ten: **Aa Bb Dd Ee Ff Gg Gb Hh Ii Jj**.
• Next ten: **Kk Ll Mm Nn ŃŃ Oo Ọọ Pp Rr Ss Ṣṣ**.
• Last: **Tt Ii (with dot: Ìì) Ww Yy** — the line is complete when you have said all twenty-five.
• Tricky ones: **Gb** is one letter, not two — write it **Gb gb**; the dotted letters must keep their dots, for **o** and **ọ** are not the same word.
• Game: I write my own name with the first letter big; then I write it small; then I write my father's name and my town with big first letters.
• A letter that is written well sits on the line and does not fall below it.

**Things to know — Àṣà (the duties of the family — iṣẹ́ ẹbí àti ìdílé)**

• A **dílé** (family) is the people of one house; an **ẹbí** is the wider family — father's brothers and mothers' sisters, the cousins and the grandmothers.
• Duties of the **father (bàbá)**: **ìpèsè** (providing), **ààbò** (protection), **ìnáwó** (paying for what the house needs).
• Duties of the **mother (ìyá)**: cooking (**ìpèsè oúnjẹ**), nursing the little ones, teaching the language, keeping the house in order.
• Duties of the **children (àwọn ọmọ)**: learning (**kíkọ́**), helping (**ìrànwọ́**), respecting (**ìbọ̀wọ̀**), keeping the place clean, and telling the truth.
• Duties of the **grandparents (àgbà)**: advice (**ìmọ̀ràn**), the names and the stories (**ìtàn àti orúkọ**), blessing the children.
• A family where each one carries his own load does not tire; where one carries all, he falls.
• We name our duties in Yoruba with the verb first: **Mo ń fọ aṣọ** (I wash cloth) · **Mo ń ta omi** (I fetch water) · **Mo ń kọ̀wé** (I write).

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (a song on the parent — orin kíkọ́ lórí òbí)**

• The song: *Ìyá ọwọ́, Bàbá ọwọ́,* — honour to mother, honour to father;
• *ọwọ́ méjèèjì ló ń gbé wa sókè.* — it is both hands together that lift us up.
• *Bí a bá kó wọn jọ, ilé á dúró;* — when they stand together, the house stands;
• *bí wọ́n bá tì, a ó ṣubú.* — if they fall apart, we fall too.
• Clap the beat: two claps on **ọwọ́**, a rest on the comma, a swing of the arms up on **sókè**.
• The song is not for singing only: at the end we say **"Ẹ̀ ṣé, ìyá. Ẹ̀ ṣé, bàbá."** out loud, once each.

**Words for my notebook**

Aa Bb Dd Ee Ff Gg Gb Hh Ii Jj Kk Ll Mm Nn ŃŃ Oo Ọọ Pp Rr Ss Ṣṣ Tt Ìì Ww Yy · Ìyá ọwọ́, bàbá ọwọ́.

**Let us talk**

1. Where do we use a big letter?
2. Say three duties of a child at home.
3. Which two hands lift the child up, in the song?
4. Write your own name with the first letter big.

**Worksheet**

1. The first letter of a person's name is written A) dotted B) big C) crossed D) small
2. "Gb" in the Yoruba alphabet is A) a vowel B) one letter C) not used D) two letters
3. "Ìyá ọwọ́" means A) honour to mother B) mother's food C) the big hand D) father's house
4. Which of these is NOT a duty of children in the family? A) learning B) respecting C) helping D) paying the rent
5. Fill in: The dotted vowels of Yoruba are ẹ, ọ and the letter ____.
6. Write the seven big letters and their small partners for the first half of the alphabet.
7. Sing the song on the parent and say in one sentence what it teaches.

**My own work**

Write five duties you and your family carry at home, each sentence beginning with "Mo ń ..." or "Ìyá ń ...".

---

### WEEK 6 — Ede: Oro ati idakeji / Asa: Ikini ni asiko iseje / Litireso: Alo apagbe

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W6.*

**You will learn to**

• say Yoruba words and their opposites
• greet people when the community gathers
• tell a short àlọ́ àpágbé with the class answering

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (words and their opposites — ọ̀rọ̀ àti ìdàkejì)**

• An **opposite (ìdàkejì)** is the word on the other side: big and small, hot and cold.
• Pairs to say in one breath: **ńlá — kékeré** (big — small) · **gígùn — kúkúrú** (tall — short) · **jìnnà — súnmọ́** (far — near) · **òkè — ìsàlẹ̀** (up — down)
• **ìwájú — ẹ̀yìn** (front — back) · **ọ̀tún — òsì** (right — left) · **gbígbóná — tútù** (hot — cold) · **rírọ̀ — líle** (soft — hard)
• **funfun — dúdú** (white — black) · **dídùn — títọ́** (sweet — bitter) · **àyọ̀ — ìbànújẹ́** (joy — sorrow) · **rírọ̀ — líle** (soft — hard).
• Game with the body: when I say **ńlá** you stretch your arms wide; when I say **kékeré** you squat; when I say **ọ̀tún** you point right.
• Use the pair in a sentence: **Òkò rẹ̀ ńlá, ṣùgbọ́n àǹfààní rẹ̀ kékeré** — his body is big, but his sense is small (a playful line the class laughs at).
• A pair is learnt better when the two words are said **together, back to back**, ten times in a line.

**Things to know — Àṣà (greeting at a gathering — ìkíini ní àṣíkò ìṣẹ̀jọ́)**

• An **àṣẹjọ (ìṣẹ̀jọ́)** is a meeting of the people: the class assembly, the ward meeting, the family gathering, the church or mosque after service.
• When we come into a gathering we say: **"Ẹ̀ káàbọ̀, ẹ̀ nı́ káàárọ̀"** — and the gathering answers **"Ẹ̀ nı́ kaàárọ̀, ọmọ mi."**
• The order of greeting at a meeting: **the eldest first, then the rest** — a child greets the head of the assembly, not the door.
• Standing is the way of the young in a gathering: we stand till the elder says **"Ìjọ̀kalẹ̀"**, sit down and keep quiet.
• To a person who is speaking we do not add our noise; the Yoruba say **"Àgbà kìí wà ní ojú ọ̀nà, kí ọmọdé tó tẹ̀ ẹ́ sọ́̀"** — the elder is present so the child does not stumble.
• Words to keep at a meeting: **"Ẹ̀ ṣé fún àǹfààní"** (thank you for the chance to speak) · **"Ẹ̀ jọ̀wọ́ ẹ̀ máà gbà mí"** (please receive me) · **"Ẹ̀ ṣé, ẹ̀ jọ̀wọ́."**
• After the meeting, we greet the ones we did not see: **"Ṣé ẹ̀ rí mi lójú ìjọ̀"?** — and the answer is **"Ìméjì"**.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (àlọ́ àpágbé)**

• **Àpágbé** is the family of àlọ́ told to **carry the listener away** into the story — it begins with a nonsense charm so that the ears open.
• Call: **"Àlọ́ àpágbé!"** — Answer: **"Àpágbé lójú, ẹ̀ ṣé!"**
• A short one for this week: the **Ajá** (dog) who wanted the moon; he jumped, fell, jumped again, and the children laughed until he lay down — and the moon stayed where it was.
• The three questions after any àlọ́: *Who was in it? What did they want? What did it teach?*
• We take turns: one child tells the opening and the ending, another says the middle — a story belongs to the class, not to one mouth.

**Words for my notebook**

ńlá—kékeré · gígùn—kúkúrú · jìnnà—súnmọ́ · òkè—ìsàlẹ̀ · ọ̀tún—òsì · gbígbóná—tútù · funfun—dúdú.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the opposite of "kúkúrú".
2. Who is greeted first at a gathering, and what do you say?
3. What do we do when an elder is speaking?
4. Tell the three parts of the story of the dog and the moon.

**Worksheet**

1. The opposite of "ńlá" (big) is A) kékeré B) tútù C) gígùn D) òkè
2. "Ọ̀tún" means A) right B) left C) down D) front
3. At a gathering we greet first the A) visitor B) driver C) eldest person D) smallest child
4. "Ẹ̀ káàbọ̀" means A) goodbye B) goodnight C) welcome D) sorry
5. Fill in: The opposite of "gbígbóná" (hot) is ____.
6. Say six pairs of opposite words in Yoruba.
7. Tell a short àlọ́ with its call, its answer and its lesson.

**My own work**

Say ten opposite words to somebody at home, and next time you enter a gathering, greet in the order the lesson taught and write what was said back to you.

Draw one big thing and one small thing beside it, then colour the small one.

---

### WEEK 8 — Ede: Pipe iro ede (faweli ati konsonanti) / Asa: Iwa rere (ere ranpe) / Litireso: Aroso oloro geere lori aja

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 S2 W8.*

**You will learn to**

• pronounce the vowels and the consonants the right way
• say what ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́ (kindness) looks like
• say a fast tongue rhyme on the dog with a sharp sound

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (saying the sounds — pípe ìró ẹ̀dẹ̀: fawẹ̀lì àti kọ́nsọ́nàǹtì)**

• **Fawẹ̀lì** (vowels) are the sounds the mouth makes open, with no lip or tongue stopping them: **a · e · ẹ · i · o · ọ · u** — seven of them.
• Say them long and short: **á à, é ẹ̀** — the mark of the tone changes the word, so the mouth must not be lazy.
• **Kọ́nsọ́nàǹtì** (consonants) are the sounds made with the lips, teeth or tongue closing: **b, d, f, g, gb, h, j, k, l, m, n, ń, p, r, s, ṣ, t, w, y**.
• Lips do the work in **b, f, gb, m, p, w**; the tongue tip in **d, j, k, l, n, r, s, ṣ, t, y**; the nose in **m, n, ń**.
• The three that English children always spoil: **ọ** (mouth open wide and flat), **ẹ** (the mouth nearly flat but open, not "ay"), and **ṣ** (the tongue flat, no buzzing).
• Say the pairs and hear the mouth change: **òdo** (river) — **ọ̀dọ́** (young one) · **wẹ̀** (to be wet) — **wé** (to enclose) · **ṣa** (to weed) — **sa** (to scatter).
• Practice: put the hand on the throat — **m, n, ń, gb** buzz; **s, ṣ, f, t** do not. That is how to tell the sounds apart when you cannot see.
• Reading a sound means writing it: I say a word slowly, and I put a tick for each sound I hear.

**Things to know — Àṣà (kindness — ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́)**

• **Ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́** is the kindness that helps and expects nothing back; it is the best mark of **ìwà rere**.
• Kindness has hands and a mouth: it **carries**, **shares**, **feeds**, **forgives**, and it **speaks gently**.
• The Yoruba say **"Ọwọ́ tí a fi ń tọ́ka, ìyẹn ni yóò fi ń gba"** — the hand that points will also receive; so point at nobody and help everybody.
• **"Kò sí ẹ̀yà tí kò ní ìdí; kò sí ìwà tí kò ní èsì."** — every fruit has a stalk, every behaviour has a return: kindness comes home.
• Kindness in the class: give water to the one who is thirsty, let the smaller child go first, cover the friend who wet himself, do not laugh at a broken word.
• Kindness to the animals too: the dog at the gate is given water, the goat is not beaten with a stick.
• The test of kindness: do it **once** when it is seen, and **twice** when it is not.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (a fast rhyme on the dog — àròsọ olóró gẹ́lẹ́ lórí ajá)**

• An **àròsọ** is a rhyme of hard, sharp sounds said as fast as possible; it trains the mouth, not the memory only.
• The one on the dog:
• *Ajá ọ̀dọ́, ajá àgbà,* — the young dog and the old dog;
• *ó ń sáré, ó ń sọ̀ ẹ̀rùn;* — it runs, it sniffs the wind;
• *ṣe é ṣé kí á ṣàṣìṣe?* — can we be said to have failed?
• *Aṣìṣe kìí ṣe ọwọ́ ajá.* — a dog's paw does not make mistakes.
• Say it in three speeds: slow and clear, medium, then fast three times — the mouth that trips on the first try should not be laughed at.
• Sounds to catch in it: **aj, ágb, sár, ẹ̀rùn, ṣé, ṣà, ọwọ́** — the **ṣ** cluster is the reason the rhyme is called "olóró" (sharp).
• We finish with the class saying the last line together, in a low voice, and then the loud voice — the loud one is the fun.

**Words for my notebook**

Fawẹ̀lì: a e ẹ i o ọ u. Kọ́nsọ́nàǹtì: b d f g gb h j k l m n ń p r s ṣ t w y. Ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́ = kindness with the hands.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the seven vowels and then the consonants made by the nose.
2. What is the difference between the mouth of "o" and "ọ"?
3. Name four acts of kindness in this class.
4. Say the dog rhyme at the fastest speed you can manage.

**Worksheet**

1. How many vowels are in the Yoruba sound list? A) 5 B) 25 C) 12 D) 7
2. Which of these is a nasal consonant (said with the nose)? A) ṣ B) l C) ń D) p
3. "Ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́" means A) courage B) anger C) kindness D) speed
4. Which word pair shows the difference between o and ọ? A) omi / ọwọ́ B) òdo / ọ̀dọ́ C) ilé / ìdí D) púpọ̀ / ìpèsè
5. Fill in: An ____ is a fast rhyme of hard sounds said quickly.
6. Say the seven vowels and mention three consonants made with the lips.
7. Write two lines of the dog rhyme and say which letters make it hard.

**My own work**

Practise the dog rhyme three times a day for four days and write down on which day your mouth did not trip.

### WEEK 9 — Ede: Atunyewo alifabeeti ati onka 1-20 / Asa: Atunyewo ikini, ounje, eran osin / Litireso: Atunyewo orin, ewi ati alo

*Depth: school-generated — a revision row; the school prints no new topic for this week.*

**You will learn to**

• say the whole alphabet, forwards, backwards and out of order
• count one to twenty without stopping
• greet, name foods and animals, and put the term's songs in order

**What to revise — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (letters and numbers)**

• The alphabet: **a b d e f g gb h i j k l m n ń o ọ p r s ṣ t ì w y** — 25 letters; **c, q, v, x, z** are not Yoruba.
• The seven vowels: **a e ẹ i o ọ u**; the digraphs: **gb, j**; the dotted letters: **ẹ, ọ, ṣ, ń**.
• Big and small letters: **Aa Bb Dd Ee Ff Gg Gb Hh Ii Jj Kk Ll Mm Nn ŃŃ Oo Ọọ Pp Rr Ss Ṣṣ Tt Ìì Ww Yy**.
• Sounds: lips — b f gb m p w · nose — m n ń · tongue — d j k l n r s ṣ t y.
• Numbers 1–10: **ọ̀kan, èjì, ẹ̀ta, ẹ̀rin, àárún, ẹ̀fà, ẹ̀je, ẹ̀jọ, ẹ̀sán, ẹ́wọ́**.
• Numbers 11–20: **ọ̀kànlá, èjìlá, ẹ̀tálá, ẹ̀rìnlá, àárúnlá, ẹ̀fálá, ẹ̀jẹ́lá, ẹ̀jọ́lá, ẹ̀sánlá, ogún** — and the "-lógún" way, 19 = **òdínlógún**.

**What to revise — Àṣà (greeting, food, animals)**

• Greetings: **ẹ̀ káàárọ̀ · ẹ̀ káàṣánṣá · ẹ̀ káàlẹ̀ · ọ́ dàbọ̀ · ọ́ nàṣẹ́ · ẹ̀ ṣé · ẹ̀ káàbọ̀ · ìdòbì · ìkúnlẹ̀**
• Greeting at a gathering: eldest first; stand till you are told to sit; do not add noise to the one speaking.
• Food: **ìyan · àmàlà · èbà · ẹ̀kọ́ · túwò**, with **ẹ̀fẹ́ · ègúsí · ẹ̀wẹ̀dú · ógọ́rò**; farm things: **iṣu, ọ̀sà, ìbà, àgbàdọ̀, ìrẹsì, àrà**.
• Animals of the house: **ewúrẹ́ · àdìẹ · ọ̀wọ́ọ̀n · àgbo · màlúù · àyẹ̀yẹ́** — and the ones we keep away from.
• Character words: **ìwà rere · ọmọlúàbí · ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́ · ìwà lẹ́wàá**.
• Family duties: father **ìpèsè, ààbò, ìnáwó** · mother cooking, nursing, teaching · children learning, helping, respecting.

**What to revise — Lítíréṣọ̀ (songs, poems, tales)**

• Songs of the term: **Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú** (knowing your own) · **Ọmọ rere** (good child, with actions) · **Ìyá ọwọ́, bàbá ọwọ́** (the parent, both hands).
• The poem: **Àgbà kìí rí nǹkan ṣe, kó jẹ́ ḱ ọ̀dọ́ ṣe é** (the old and young work together).
• The **àlọ́**: call **"Àlọ́!"**, answer **"Àlọ́ ni!"** — three parts: beginning, middle, ending; then the lesson.
• The àròsọ of the dog: said fast, with **aj, ṣà, ọwọ́** clear.
• The rule for all of them: say it aloud, with the mouth open, and do not be ashamed of the first try.

**Words for my notebook**

Mo mọ̀ gbogbo alífábéètì. Mo mọ̀ ká ọ̀kan dé ogún. Mo mọ̀ ẹ̀ káàárọ̀, oúnjẹ ilé wa àti ẹranko tí a ń tọ́.

**Let us talk**

1. Read the whole alphabet without stopping.
2. Count from 1 to 20; then from ogún down to ọ̀kan.
3. Greet me as the eldest in a gathering.
4. Sing one of this term's songs and say what it teaches.

**Worksheet**

1. How many letters are in the Yoruba alphabet? A) 21 B) 25 C) 24 D) 26
2. The Yoruba for eighteen is A) ẹ̀jọ́lá B) ẹ̀sánlá C) ẹ̀jọ́ D) ogún
3. "Ẹ̀ káàbọ̀" is said when somebody A) sleeps B) leaves C) eats D) arrives
4. Which food is made from cassava? A) túwò B) ìyan C) ẹ̀kọ́/èbà D) àkàrà
5. Fill in: The animal that gives us eggs is ____.
6. Write the seven vowels and name the two digraphs of Yoruba.
7. Say the lines of one song and one poem from this term, and give what each teaches.

**My own work**

Copy the three revision lists onto one page, read it aloud tomorrow morning, and tick every line you could say without looking.

### WEEK 11 — Exam Preparation

*Depth: school-generated revision row; it revises Weeks 1–9 of this term. The school keeps Week 12 for the examination itself and Week 13 for Closing.*

**You will learn to**

• say everything you learnt this term without looking at the book
• write the Yoruba words you must know with their dots and marks
• answer in full Yoruba sentences, not one word

**What to revise — Ẹ̀dẹ̀**

• the letters **a** to **y**, the 25 letters, and what English letters we do not use
• the seven vowels **a e ẹ i o ọ u**; the digraphs **gb, j**; the dots on **ẹ, ọ, ṣ, ń**
• big letters and small letters, and where a big letter is used
• counting **ọ̀kan** to **ogún** (1–20), both the **-lá** way and the **-lógún** way
• opposite words (ìdàkejì) and the sounds of the vowels and consonants

**What to revise — Àṣà**

• greetings at each time of day, and how a boy and a girl greet an elder (**ìdòbì, ìkúnlẹ̀**)
• greeting in order at a gathering (**àṣẹjọ**), standing till you are told to sit
• the food of our home and the animals a Yoruba family keeps (**ewúrẹ́, àdìẹ, àgbo, màlúù**)
• **ọmọlúàbí**, **ìwà rere** and **ọ̀rẹ̀ẹ́-ránpẹ́** — and the duties of father, mother, children and grandparents

**What to revise — Lítíréṣọ̀**

• the songs: **Ọmọ tó mọ ìyá rẹ̀ lójú · Ọmọ rere · Ìyá ọwọ́, Bàbá ọwọ́** — the words, the actions, and what each teaches
• the poem: **Àgbà kìí rí nǹkan ṣe, kó jẹ́ ḱ ọ̀dọ́ ṣe é**
• the **àlọ́**: the call and answer, the three parts, and the closing line
• the **àròsọ** of the dog, said fast

**Words for my notebook**

Mo mọ̀ alífábéètì, ìkà 1–20, ìkíni, oúnjẹ àti ẹranko ilé wa. Mo sì lè kọ ọ́.

**Let us talk**

1. Read from a to y, then from y to a.
2. Count to twenty in Yoruba without stopping.
3. Greet me as an elder at a gathering, in the right way.
4. Sing one song of the term and say what it teaches.

**Worksheet**

1. The Yoruba word for twenty is A) ẹ́wọ́ B) ẹ̀sánlá C) àárún D) ogún
2. Which of these letters is not used in writing Yoruba? A) ń B) x C) gb D) ṣ
3. "Ìkúnlẹ̀" is how ____ greet an elder. A) dogs B) girls C) boys D) strangers
4. The poem "Àgbà kìí rí nǹkan ṣe" teaches A) eating B) speed C) honour for the old and the young working together D) counting
5. Fill in: The opposite of "jìnnà" (far) is ____.
6. Write six Yoruba foods and four animals of the home.
7. Say two lines of any song of this term, then write what the song teaches in one sentence.

**My own work**

Write one page that holds the four things you must not forget in the examination — the alphabet line, the numbers 11–20, five greetings and one song — and read it back each morning this week.

Draw one picture for each of the four things you will revise and colour the one you know best.

# Mathematics & English

## LITERACY — LANGUAGE DOMAIN

### WEEK 1 — CAPITAL LETTERS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Second Term W1 (pp 470–474).*

**You will learn to**

• name and form all the capital letters A–Z
• say the three times a capital letter must be used
• write my own name and my friends' names rightly
• tell the capital and the small partner of each letter

**Things to know**

• Letters come in two sizes: **capitals (big letters)** and **small letters (lower case)** — A is the capital of a, Q of q.
• Our alphabet has **26 capitals**; the Yoruba alphabet has 25 letters and no c, q, v, x or z, so those five capitals are only for English words.
• **Rule one — a capital begins every sentence**: "**T**he sun is hot." · "**I** can read."
• **Rule two — a capital begins a name**: a person's name (****B**ola, **M**usa, **C**hinelo**), a place (**L**agos, **A**buja, **N**igeria), a month or a day.
• **Rule three — the word "I" is always a capital**, wherever it sits: "Ada and **I** went."
• Names to practise today: my own first name, a friend's name, my father's name, my town, my country — each with the first letter big and the rest small.
• Days of the week (the first letters are capitals): **Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday, Sunday** — Wednesday and Saturday are the two that lose their first sound when we say them fast.
• Months of the year (all begin with a capital): **January, February, March, April, May, June, July, August, September, October, November, December** — twelve, and each one is a name, not a common word.
• Forming the letters: every capital stands on the line and rises to the top. The small letters **g, j, p, q, y** dip their tails **below** the line, and **b, d, f, h, k, l, t** go up like a flag.
• Match the pairs at the board: **A a · B b · C c … Z z** — the shape of some is quite different (**G g, R r, Q q**), and some are only smaller (**C c, S s, X x**).
• Convert: "bola went to lagos" → "**B**ola went to **L**agos." — circle every letter that must be made big.
• Where else we see capitals: the school gate sign, the Bible, the shop board, the front of my own exercise book — the capital letter is a small flag that says *this is important, this has a name*.

**Words for my notebook**

A capital begins a sentence, begins a name, and is used for the word "I". Monday · January · Lagos · Bola.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the three rules for using a capital letter.
2. What is the capital partner of "y"? Which small letters go below the line?
3. Write your name and your town with the right big letters.
4. Say the days of the week and then the months.


![A child writes on a sheet ruled with four empty lines while three plain cards stand folded on the desk, every one of them blank.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/mathematics-english-language/week1-three-blank-cards-and-a-ruled-page.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A capital letter is used A) only for "I" B) never C) in the middle of every word D) at the start of a sentence
2. Which is written correctly? A) bOLA went to LAGOS B) Bola Went to Lagos C) Bola went to Lagos D) bola went to lagos
3. The first letter of a month is A) a capital B) a dot C) small D) crossed
4. How many letters are in the English alphabet? A) 25 B) 24 C) 27 D) 26
5. Fill in: The word ____ is always a capital letter, wherever it stands.
6. Write the seven days of the week with their first letters big.
7. Correct this sentence: "tunde and ada came from abuja on friday."

**My own work**

Write five names of people, three names of places, the day you were born on and the month you like best, each beginning with a capital letter.

---

### WEEK 2 — SIGHT WORDS: ONE, WE, SOME

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Second Term W2 (pp 470–474).*

**You will learn to**

• read, write and use the words **one**, **we** and **some**
• say what each word means with my hands
• read short sentences made of these three words
• know that "one" is a number and a word at once

**Things to know**

• Three new sight words: **one · we · some** — read them with the eye, not by blending.
• **one** = 1 — it is a **word and a number** at the same time: "I have **one** bag." It also means *a single one*: "**One** day I will be big."
• **we** = me and others together: "**we** are in class." — the word that has no "you and I" in it but means exactly that.
• **some** = more than one, but not counted: "**some** pens", "**some** water" — it does not tell us how many, only that there is enough for a little.
• **One car · two cars · some cars** — "one" counts, "some" does not; that is the difference between the number word and the little word.
• Sentences to read: "**We** see **one**." · "**Some** are here." · "**We** have **some**." · "**One** is for **me**, **some** are for **us**."
• Hand signals: one finger for **one**, both arms wide open for **some**, the whole class standing together for **we** — when the word is read, the hand shows the meaning.
• **we** begins with **w** (two V shapes joined) and ends with **e** — do not write "wi" or "wee"; **some** has a quiet **s** at the front and an **e** at the end that is not shouted.
• Sound hunt: which of the three words has the sound /w/? (we) · which ends in the sound /n/? (one) · which begins with /s/? (some).
• Memory game: the three cards face down; turn two, say both words, and if you can say a sentence with them you keep the pair.
• Writing: each word is written **three times** — once small and fast, once big and slow, once in a sentence.
• Counting connection: **one, two, three…** — "one" is the first number we ever say; the word is the number, and the number is a shape (**1**).
• Look for them at home: "one" on a page of a book, "some" on a food packet, "we" in a song — tick each word when you find it.

**Words for my notebook**

one · we · some — "I have one bag." "We are in class." "We have some water."

**Let us talk**

1. Which word means me and you together?
2. Is "one" a number or a word? (Both — say why.)
3. Give me three of something and one of something else; which word describes each?
4. Read this: "Some are here, we see one."


![One ball on the left, three children holding hands in the middle and a loose heap of marbles beside a bowl of two on the right.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/mathematics-english-language/week2-one-ball-three-friends-a-heap-of-marbles.png)
**Worksheet**

1. "____ have some pens." A) Am B) One C) We D) Some
2. The word "some" tells us A) none B) exactly how many C) more than one, not counted D) only one
3. "one" is a word and also A) a colour B) a name C) a sound D) a number
4. Which sentence is right? A) We have some. B) We has some. C) One are here. D) Some are we.
5. Fill in: "I can see ____ bag." (the number word)
6. Write one sentence with "we" and one with "some".
7. Say the three words as fast as you can, then write them.

**My own work**

Find one thing, then some things, in your own bag; write one sentence about each and read them to the class tomorrow.

---

### WEEK 3 — DOUBLE CONSONANTS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Second Term W3 (pp 470–474).*

**You will learn to**

• see two same consonants together in a word
• say and read words with bb, dd, gg, ll, mm, nn, pp, rr, ss, tt, zz
• know that a double consonant is one sound, written twice
• spell the double letter without forgetting it

**Things to know**

• A **double consonant** is **the same consonant letter written twice together**: ra**bb**it, la**dd**er, o**ff**, e**gg**, be**ll**.
• The twelve to look for: **bb · dd · ff · gg · ll · mm · nn · pp · rr · ss · tt · zz**.
• Words for each pair: **rabbit** (bb) · **ladder, add** (dd) · **off, muffin** (ff) · **egg, giggle** (gg) · **bell, ball, tall** (ll) · **summer, hammer** (mm) · **dinner, banner** (nn) · **happy, puppy** (pp) · **purr, carrot** (rr) · **dress, miss** (ss) · **butter, kettle** (tt) · **buzz, jazz** (zz).
• The big rule to hear: the double consonant makes **one sound**, not two — we do not say "e-g-g"; we say **egg** once and let the **g** be firm.
• The **short vowel** rule (first idea): the double consonant usually comes right after a **short** vowel — **a** in ha**pp**y is short; compare **hoping** and **hopping**: the double tells the ear the o is short.
• Position: a double consonant comes in the **middle** (rabbit, dinner) or at the **end** (off, buzz, fill) — never at the **start** of an English word; that is a good test question.
• Word building with cards: **ra + bb + it** → rabbit; **la + dd + er** → ladder. Now take the double out and the word is wrong — "raler" is not a word: that is why the doubled letter matters.
• Sorting game: write the words in two columns — **has a double consonant** / **has none**: egg · pen · bell · sun · happy · cat · off · ball.
• Spelling check: after I write a word, I look at it and ask *"is there a pair in it?"* — the pairs are the ones we lose most in writing.
• Reading aloud: *"The puppy sat on the ladder and saw the big red ball."* — say every word with a double consonant again, slowly.
• Names can carry a double letter too — **Anna** (nn) and **Rebecca** (cc); if anybody in our class has a name with a pair in it, write it on the wall chart under that pair.

**Words for my notebook**

rabbit · ladder · egg · bell · summer · dinner · happy · butter · dress · buzz — one sound, two letters.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a double consonant?
2. Say the sound in "off" — is it one g or two sounds?
3. Which words have a double: "pen", "egg", "sun", "ball"?
4. Where can a double letter come in a word — the start, the middle, or the end?


![Two spoons, two cups on saucers and two balls lie in matched pairs, each pair exactly alike.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/mathematics-english-language/week3-three-pairs-on-the-mat.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A double consonant is A) two vowels B) a silent letter C) a full stop D) two same consonants together
2. Which word has "gg"? A) gig B) egg C) off D) add
3. The double consonant is pronounced A) silently B) as a vowel C) twice, clearly D) as one firm sound
4. Which word has a double letter? A) pen B) hopper C) hop D) cup
5. Fill in: ra__it — the missing letters are ____.
6. Write five words with double consonants and underline the pair in each.
7. Say the sentence "The puppy sat on the ladder" and name the two words with doubles.

**My own work**

Look through one page of your story book and circle every word that has a double consonant; write five of them in your notebook.

Draw an egg, a comb and a lamp; write the double letter under each picture, then colour the lamp.

---

### WEEK 4 — SIGHT WORDS: EGG, ADD, INK, COMB

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Second Term W4 (pp 470–474).*

**You will learn to**

• read and write **egg · add · ink · comb**
• see the double consonant inside two of them
• say why the **b** in "comb" is not sounded
• put all four words into sentences

**Things to know**

• The four words: **egg** (what the hen lays) · **add** (to put together — the maths word) · **ink** (the black liquid in a pen) · **comb** (the thing for the hair; and a hive of bees is also a comb).
• **egg** has **gg** and **add** has **dd** — the double consonants of last week are now living inside words I must know whole.
• **ink** is three letters and three sounds: /ɪ/ /n/ /k/ — the **k** after **n** is a firm finish, and it is also the sound of the word ending in "think, drink, pink".
• **comb** has a **silent letter**: the **b** is written but **not said** — we say /kəʊm/, "kome"; the b sleeps at the end.
• Other sleeping letters to name: **k**nee (k silent), **w**rite (w silent), li**gh**t (gh silent), **comb** (b silent) — we say it this way: *English still writes what it used to say, even after the mouth moved on.*
• Why "comb" keeps its **b**: long ago the b was sounded; the spelling stayed when the talking changed — the word's history is written on its back.
• Sentences to read: "**Add** one **egg**." · "The **comb** has **ink** on it." · "Do not put **ink** on the **egg**." · "**We** can **add** the **egg**."
• Use the words together: *What do you add?* (egg) — *What do you comb with?* (comb) — *What is inside the pen?* (ink) — this is the sense-check we do when a word is wrong.
• Write each word four ways: small, big, in the air with the finger, and once inside a sentence of my own.
• Sight-word review: the words learnt so far (Week 5–6 of Letter Work, plus one/we/some, plus today's four) are put on one big chart; I read the chart once forwards, once backwards.
• Sorting: which are **maths or school** words (add, ink) and which are **home** words (egg, comb)? A word can belong to both — say so.

**Words for my notebook**

egg · add · ink · comb. The b in "comb" is silent — we say "kome".

**Let us talk**

1. Which of today's words has a double letter?
2. Why do we not say the b in "comb"?
3. Say a sentence with "add" about your lunch.
4. What is the sound at the end of "ink"?


![An egg in its cup, an open ink bottle with a pen across it, a fine-tooth comb and a bowl with beans being added.](assets/img/nursery-2/2nd-term/mathematics-english-language/week4-the-egg-the-ink-and-the-comb.png)
**Worksheet**

1. "____ the numbers together" A) Ink B) Comb C) Egg D) Add
2. The silent letter in "comb" is A) o B) m C) b D) c
3. Which word has "dd"? A) add B) egg C) comb D) ink
4. Ink comes from A) a hen B) a pot of water only C) a comb D) a pen
5. Fill in: "The hen lays an ____."
6. Write a sentence for each of the four words.
7. Say two other words that have a silent letter, and which letter sleeps in each.

**My own work**

Write four sentences using today's words, then find at home one thing you comb with and one thing you add to your food.

Draw one thing for each of your four sight words and colour the four pictures.

---

### WEEK 5 — ALTERNATIVE SPELLINGS (PART 1): ai / ae / ay AND ee / ea

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · Second Term W7 (pp 470–474).*

**You will learn to**

• say the long **a** sound and the three ways it is spelled
• say the long **e** sound and the two ways it is spelled
• choose the right spelling by where the sound sits in the word
• read and write the word families rain/train and tree/see

**Things to know**

• **Alternative spellings** = the **same sound** written in **different ways**; English does this a lot, and it is why spelling must be learnt, not only sounded out.
• The **long a** sound is the letter's **own name**: /eɪ/ — as in "day".
• Three ways to write it: **ai** (r**ai**n, tr**ai**n, m**ai**l, w**ai**t, p**ai**n) · **ay** (d**ay**, pl**ay**, s**ay**, tr**ay**, w**ay**) · **ae** (**ae**robics, **ae**rial) — the last one is rare, so know it and do not chase it.
• The position rule to keep: **ai** sits in the **middle** of a word (rain, tail, nail); **ay** sits at the **end** (day, play, say, may).
• The **long e** sound is /iː/ — the mouth wide, a smile sound.
• Two ways to write it: **ee** (tr**ee**, b**ee**, s**ee**, f**ee**t, sl**ee**p, gr**ee**n) · **ea** (**ea**t, s**ea**, t**ea**, r**ea**d, dr**ea**m, l**ea**f, s**ea**t).
• The same sound, different spelling: **see** and **sea** sound alike; **rain** and **reign** sound alike and only the spelling tells them apart.
• Word families for the week: **rain / train / grain** · **play / say / tray / day** · **tree / bee / free / three** · **tea / sea / pea**.
• Read the line: *"The b**ee** s**aw** the r**ai**n, so she f**lew** to the tr**ee** and sat on a l**ea**f."* — say every word with the long a and the long e.
• Choosing practice: fill **ai** or **ay** — r_n → **rain** · d_ → **day** · tr_n → **train** · pl_ → **play** · s_ → **say**. Then say why: the sound at the **end** of the word takes **ay**, the sound in the **middle** takes **ai**.
• Spelling out loud: say the word, then say the letters, then say which team the sound plays for: "play — p-l-a-y — the long a at the end, so **ay**."
• Why one sound has four coats: English came from many languages, and each brought its own spelling habits; the sound is the child, the spellings are the coats.

**Words for my notebook**

Long a: ai (rain) · ay (day) · ae (aerobics). Long e: ee (tree, bee) · ea (eat, sea, tea). Same sound, different spelling.

**Let us talk**

1. What are alternative spellings?
2. Where in a word do we usually put "ai"? Where do we put "ay"?
3. Say three words with "ee" and three with "ea".
4. Spell "train" aloud and say which spelling team the sound plays for.
